    "Title": "Scalable First-Order Bayesian Optimization via Structured Automatic Differentiation",
    "Main Contributions": "The paper addresses the scalability issues of Bayesian Optimization (BO) in high dimensions, particularly when incorporating gradient information into Gaussian Process (GP) surrogate models. Standard approaches lead to computationally expensive kernel matrices (O(n^3 d^3) for inversion, O(n^2 d^2) for matrix-vector multiplication (MVM)). The main contributions are: 1) Deriving analytical block-data-sparse structures for a large class of gradient kernel matrices, enabling an exact O(n^2 d) MVM. 2) Proposing an Automatic Differentiation (AD) framework that programmatically computes these data-sparse block structures for transformations and algebraic combinations of kernels. 3) Deriving analogous structures for kernel matrices arising from Hessian observations, reducing MVM complexity from O(n^2 d^4) to O(n^2 d^2). These methods apply to a wide range of canonical and complex kernels (e.g., neural network, RBF network, spectral mixture kernels) without additional derivations, thereby scaling first-order BO to high dimensions.",
    "Methodology": "The core methodology involves exploiting structured automatic differentiation to identify and leverage data-sparse structures within gradient and Hessian kernel matrices. For gradient kernels, the approach analyzes kernels expressible as k(x,y) = f(proto(x,y)), where 'proto' can be isotropic (r·r), stationary linear functional (c·r), or dot product (x·y). A chain rule is applied for composite kernels (k = f ◦ g), showing that G[f ◦ g] is a rank-one correction to G[g], preserving O(d) MVM complexity for base kernels. Rules are derived for sums and products of kernels, leading to O(dr) and O(dr) MVM complexity respectively (for r kernels). Direct sums and products (separable kernels) achieve O(d) MVM. Warping functions k(x,y) = h(u(x),u(y)) yield structures allowing O(n^2 d) MVM for n > d ≥ r (where r is the dimension of the warped space). For Hessian kernel structures, similar derivations reduce MVM complexity for isotropic kernels to O(n^2 d^2). The implementation, CovarianceFunctions.jl in Julia, uses user-defined types to represent computational graphs of kernel functions, employs ForwardDiff.jl for regular derivatives, and uses lazy evaluation for O(1) memory complexity during MVM. For Bayesian Optimization, a composite kernel f = g + h is proposed, where g is a quadratic function modeled by a GP with the kernel (x·y+c)^2, and h is a quickly varying, non-convex function modeled by a GP with a Matérn-5/2 kernel.",
    "Experimental Setup": "The experimental setup includes scaling benchmarks on synthetic data and Bayesian Optimization experiments on test functions. For scaling, matrix-vector multiplications (MVM) are benchmarked using non-separable rational quadratic, exponentiated dot-product, and neural network kernels. Experiments were run on modern parallel architectures (24 threads on 12 cores) to demonstrate real-world applicability. Performance is compared against existing libraries like GPyTorch (which only offers naive O(n^2 d^2) implementations for gradient kernels) and approximate methods like D-SKIP (Eriksson et al., 2018). For Bayesian Optimization, the proposed methods are applied to optimize the non-convex Griewank, Ackley, and Rastrigin test functions in 4 and 16 dimensions. Input domains were scaled to [-1,1]^d, outputs to [0,1], and global optima shifted. Performance is evaluated by the average optimality gap over 128 independent experiments, comparing random sampling, L-BFGS, L-BFGS with random restarts, standard BO, BO with the proposed quadratic mixture kernel (BO-Q), First-Order BO (FOBO), and FOBO with the quadratic mixture kernel (FOBO-Q). All BO variants use the expected improvement acquisition function, optimized by L-BFGS with a random restart mechanism to escape local minima.",
    "Limitations": "The global optimization of non-convex functions remains NP-hard, and thus the proposed methods, while improving performance, cannot generally solve this problem. For instance, the optimum of the 16D Ackley function was still elusive for all tested methods. While structured representations for kernel matrices arising from Hessian observations were derived and their computational feasibility demonstrated, this work primarily focused on first-order information. The Hessian observations were not directly used for Bayesian Optimization in the experiments, leaving a comprehensive comparison of first and second-order BO to future work.",
    "Future Research Directions": "1) Linking the proposed structured AD technology with Maclaurin et al. (2015)'s algorithm for computing hyper-parameter gradients to enable efficient first-order BO of hyper-parameters. 2) Addressing the O(n^2) complexity for a large number of observations (n) by developing analysis-based fast algorithms for gradient kernels, similar to Ryan et al. (2022)'s Fast Kernel Transform, especially for low-dimensional problems. 3) Exploiting redundant information in BO trajectories, particularly when converging to a minimum, using sparse linear solvers (e.g., Ament and Gomes (2021)). 4) Investigating the utility of Hessian observations for Bayesian Quadrature, given the established benefits of second-order information for integral estimation (e.g., Laplace approximation).",

    "Title": "Scalable First-Order Bayesian Optimization via Structured Automatic Differentiation",
    "Main Contributions": "The main problem addressed is the computational scalability of First-Order Bayesian Optimization (FOBO) in high dimensions. Standard approaches suffer from prohibitive O(n^3 d^3) operations for kernel matrix inversion when incorporating gradient information. The paper's key contributions are: 1) deriving analytical block-data-sparse structures for gradient kernel matrices, enabling an exact O(n^2 d) matrix-vector multiply; 2) proposing a structure-aware Automatic Differentiation (AD) framework that programmatically computes these structures for transformations and combinations of canonical kernels, extending to complex kernels like neural network, radial basis function network, and spectral mixture kernels; 3) deriving analogous structures for Hessian observations, reducing complexity to O(n^2 d^2) for matrix-vector multiply; 4) demonstrating improved scaling and applicability through numerical experiments; 5) comparing the proposed methods against existing techniques; and 6) applying the developed methods to Bayesian Optimization.",
    "Methodology": "The core methodology involves exploiting structured matrices that arise from incorporating gradient and Hessian information into Gaussian Process (GP) surrogate models. For gradient kernels, the paper systematically derives structured representations for a wide range of kernel functions (isotropic, stationary linear functional, dot product) and their transformations (chain rule, sums, products, direct sums/products, vertical rescaling, warping). These structures reduce the matrix-vector multiplication (MVM) complexity for gradient kernels to O(n^2 d). For Hessian observations, analogous structured representations are derived for isotropic kernels, reducing MVM complexity to O(n^2 d^2). The paper introduces a programmatic approach through an AD framework, implemented in CovarianceFunctions.jl (Julia), which automatically detects and leverages these data-sparse block structures. The implementation uses lazy evaluation for O(1) memory complexity and ForwardDiff.jl for general derivatives. For Bayesian Optimization, the Expected Improvement (EI) acquisition function is used, and a novel quadratic mixture kernel, combining a (x·y+c)^2 kernel for quadratic components and a Matérn-5/2 kernel for non-convex deviations, is proposed to model objective functions.",
    "Experimental Setup": "The experimental setup includes scaling benchmarks on synthetic data and Bayesian Optimization (BO) performance comparisons. For scaling, matrix-vector multiplications (MVMs) with gradient and Hessian kernel matrices were benchmarked using non-separable rational quadratic, exponentiated dot-product, and neural network kernels. Experiments measured execution time as a function of dataset size (n) and dimensionality (d), using both single-threaded runs for scaling plots and multi-threaded runs (24 threads on 12 cores) for heat-maps. Comparisons to prior work involved popular libraries (GPyTorch, GPFlow, Scikit-Learn, Dragonﬂy, BayesOpt) and a specific approximate method called D-SKIP. The BO experiments were conducted on Griewank, Ackley, and Rastrigin non-convex test functions, with input domains scaled to [-1,1]^d and outputs to [0,1], and global optima shifted. Algorithms compared included random sampling, L-BFGS, L-BFGS with random restarts, BO (zeroth-order), BO with the quadratic mixture kernel (BO-Q), FOBO (first-order), and FOBO with the quadratic mixture kernel (FOBO-Q). All BO variants used the expected improvement acquisition function, optimized by L-BFGS, and incorporated a random restart mechanism if new points were too close to existing observations. Experiments were run in 4 and 16 dimensions, with average optimality gap reported over 128 independent runs.",
    "Limitations": "The paper acknowledges that global optimization of non-convex functions remains NP-hard, and thus the proposed methods cannot solve it in general. For instance, the 16D Ackley function's optimum was elusive for all methods tested, possibly due to its exponentially shrinking domain of attraction. Although structured representations for Hessian observations were derived, the primary focus of the experimental evaluation and BO application was on first-order information. A comprehensive comparison of first and second-order BO was explicitly left for future work. The O(n^2) complexity, even with linear-in-d MVMs, can still become expensive for a very large number of observations (n).",
    "Future Research Directions": "Future research directions include: 1) linking the proposed technology with Maclaurin et al. (2015)'s algorithm for computing hyper-parameter gradients to enable efficient FOBO of hyper-parameters; 2) exploring analysis-based fast algorithms, such as derivations of the Fast Kernel Transform for gradient kernels, to address the O(n^2) complexity bottleneck for large numbers of observations (especially in low dimensions); 3) exploiting redundant information in BO trajectories, particularly when converging to a minimum, using sparse linear solvers; and 4) investigating the use of Hessian observations for Bayesian Quadrature, leveraging the established benefits of second-order information for integration (e.g., Laplace approximation).",

    "Title": "Efficient Nonmyopic Bayesian Optimization via One-Shot Multi-Step Trees",
    "Main Contributions": "The paper provides the first efficient implementation of general multi-step lookahead Bayesian optimization, addressing the intractability of computing full lookahead policies for expensive-to-evaluate black-box functions. It introduces \"one-shot multi-step trees,\" a novel scenario tree-based acquisition function that jointly optimizes all decision variables across multiple lookahead steps without nested dynamic programming. This approach leverages a reparameterization trick and auto-differentiation for gradient-based optimization. The work also develops \"fast-fantasies,\" efficient linear algebra methods for conditioning Gaussian Process models, crucial for practical scalability. The proposed method demonstrates superior optimization performance compared to existing myopic (Expected Improvement) and nonmyopic baselines (BINOCULARS) on various benchmarks, while maintaining competitive wall times. Additionally, two scalable special cases, \"multi-step path\" and \"non-adaptive approximation (ENO),\" are introduced, showing surprisingly good performance with linear growth in the lookahead horizon.",
    "Methodology": "The core methodology involves formulating multi-step lookahead as a sequence of nested optimization problems within a multi-step scenario tree. Instead of nested optimization, the paper proposes a \"one-shot\" approach, jointly optimizing all decision variables in the full tree. This is enabled by the reparameterization trick, which allows expressing fantasized outcomes as deterministic functions of query locations and independent base samples, making the acquisition function fully differentiable. Gradient-based optimization (L-BFGS-B with random restarting and warm-start initialization) is then applied. To handle the exponential growth of the tree, \"multi-step fast fantasies\" are introduced. This technique efficiently updates GPyTorch's LOVE caches for posterior inference in O(nrq) time (Proposition 2) and represents different levels of the lookahead tree using batched GP models, leveraging parallelization on modern hardware. Monte Carlo integration or Gauss-Hermite quadrature rules are used for approximating expectations in each stage. Special cases like \"Multi-Step Path\" (restricting to a single fantasy path) and \"Non-Adaptive Approximation (ENO)\" (using non-adaptive batch decisions for future steps) are also explored for improved scalability.",
    "Experimental Setup": "Experiments were conducted following the setting of previous work, using a set of nine \"hard\" synthetic benchmark functions and seven real hyperparameter tuning functions (SVM, LDA, logistic regression, neural network tuning on Boston Housing and Breast Cancer, and robot pushing). For real functions, a Random Forest surrogate model was pre-trained on dense grids. Each experiment began with 2d random initial observations, followed by 20d iterations of Bayesian optimization, with 100 repeats per function and method. Performance was measured by GAP = (yi - y0) / (y* - y0). The surrogate model used was a Gaussian Process with a constant mean and a Matérn 5/2 ARD kernel, with hyperparameters re-estimated via evidence maximization after each iteration. All algorithms were implemented in BoTorch and GPyTorch. Comparisons were made against one-step Expected Improvement (EI), BINOCULARS (best reported variant 12.EI.s), and \"envelope two-step\" (ETS). The proposed methods included \"k-step\" (k=2,3,4 with specific Gauss-Hermite sample counts per stage), \"k-path\" (multi-step path variant), and \"k-ENO\" (non-adaptive approximation variant). All experiments were run on CPU Linux machines, utilizing a single core per experiment, with additional GPU speedup analysis for fast fantasies presented in the appendix.",
    "Limitations": "The general one-shot optimization problem for multi-step trees has a dimensionality that grows exponentially with the lookahead horizon, practically limiting its application to relatively small horizons (e.g., less than 5 steps). Empirical results show diminishing returns in optimization performance beyond a 3-step lookahead, which might indicate challenges in effectively optimizing the increasingly complex multi-step objective or an increased reliance on the accuracy of the surrogate model, which may not always be warranted in practice. While the method enables gradient-based optimization, successful implementation requires careful initialization strategies, such as the advanced warm-starting technique developed in the paper.",
    "Future Research Directions": "Future research could focus on investigating the application of the developed multi-step Bayesian optimization framework to other sequential experimental design problems, such as Bayesian quadrature. Further efforts could also explore methods to enhance the scalability of the approach for larger lookahead horizons, potentially by building upon the insights gained from the efficient \"multi-step path\" and \"non-adaptive approximation\" variants. A deeper investigation into the reasons for diminishing returns beyond a certain lookahead horizon (e.g., 3 steps) is also warranted, considering potential improvements in optimization strategies or ways to mitigate over-reliance on model accuracy in longer horizons.",

    "Title": "Efficient Hyperparameter Optimization with Adaptive Fidelity Identification",
    "Main Contributions": "The paper addresses the challenge in multi-fidelity Bayesian Optimization (BO) for Hyperparameter Optimization (HPO) and Neural Architecture Search (NAS) of adaptively determining the appropriate fidelity for each hyperparameter configuration to fit the surrogate model. It proposes FastBO, a multi-fidelity BO method that leverages the novel concepts of \"efficient point\" and \"saturation point\" for adaptive fidelity identification, leading to efficient and strong performance. A key contribution is also demonstrating that this adaptive fidelity identification strategy can generalize to extend any single-fidelity method to a multi-fidelity setting, highlighting its broad applicability.",
    "Methodology": "FastBO is a model-based multi-fidelity Bayesian Optimization method. Its core methodology revolves around adaptively identifying an \"efficient point\" and a \"saturation point\" for each hyperparameter configuration's learning curve `Ci(r)`. The efficient point `ei` is defined as `min{r | Ci(r) − Ci(2r) < δ1}`, signifying a resource level where doubling resources yields marginal performance improvement, thus serving as the optimal fidelity for fitting the surrogate model. The saturation point `si` is `min{r | ∀r′ > r,|Ci(r′)−Ci(r)| < δ2}`, indicating where performance stabilizes, used as an approximate final fidelity. The FastBO process includes: 1) a warm-up stage to gather early observations and terminate poor configurations, 2) estimating the configuration's learning curve, 3) adaptively extracting its efficient and saturation points, 4) evaluating the configuration up to its efficient point to update the surrogate model, and 5) a post-processing stage where a subset of promising configurations are evaluated to their saturation points to identify the overall optimal configuration. This adaptive fidelity identification strategy is presented as generalizable to extend single-fidelity methods to multi-fidelity scenarios.",
    "Experimental Setup": "FastBO's performance was evaluated by comparing it against several established HPO and NAS methods, including Random Search (RS), standard BO, ASHA, Hyperband, PASHA, A-BOHB, A-CQR, BOHB, DyHPO, and Hyper-Tune. The experiments were conducted on three publicly available benchmarks: LCBench, NAS-Bench-201, and FCNet. The validation focused on \"anytime performance,\" demonstrating FastBO's ability to achieve a performance advantage earlier than other methods and rapidly converge to the global optimum after an initial phase, as shown in comparative figures.",
    "Limitations": "The paper implies potential limitations regarding the current applicability and scalability of FastBO. It mentions \"challenges that demand future improvements\" specifically in refining and expanding FastBO to larger search spaces and distributed computing systems.     "Title": "A General Framework for User-Guided Bayesian Optimization",
    "Main Contributions": "The main problem addressed is the limited ability of traditional Bayesian Optimization (BO) to incorporate diverse prior knowledge or beliefs about black-box functions, beyond typical kernel structures. This limitation reduces BO's appeal for knowledgeable practitioners. The paper introduces ColaBO, a novel Bayesian-principled framework that allows users to inject prior beliefs regarding the optimizer's likely location, the optimal value, and preference relations into the BO process. The key contributions include formally defining ColaBO as a framework for an additional prior on the surrogate model, demonstrating its general applicability to Monte Carlo acquisition functions, and empirically showing that it substantially accelerates optimization with accurate priors while retaining default performance with misleading ones.",
    "Methodology": "ColaBO is a Bayesian-principled framework that extends Gaussian Process (GP) based BO by allowing the injection of user-defined prior beliefs over function properties (e.g., optimum location, optimal value) into the surrogate model. It defines an augmented user belief `ρ(f)` which acts as a likelihood, forming a belief-weighted prior `p(f|ρ)`. This `p(f|ρ)` is updated with observed data `D` to a belief-weighted posterior `p(f|D, ρ)`, which is proportional to `ρ(f)p(f|D)`. The framework leverages *pathwise updates* from decoupled posterior sampling, where the user belief `ρ` influences the draw from the prior and observed data `D` affects a deterministic update. Due to the non-Gaussian nature of the belief-weighted posteriors, ColaBO integrates with Monte Carlo (MC) acquisition functions. It adapts MC-Expected Improvement (MC-EI) and Max-Value Entropy Search (MES) by weighting samples from the posterior according to `ρ(f_l)`. Samples are drawn from the prior, retained based on their probability under `ρ`, and then pathwise updated using observed data. The framework uses Random Fourier Features (RFF) for scalable approximate function draws.",
    "Experimental Setup": "The performance of ColaBO was evaluated on two types of tasks: synthetic functions and real-world hyperparameter optimization (HPO) tasks. Synthetic benchmarks included Hartmann (3D, 4D, 6D), Levy (5D), Rosenbrock (6D), and Stybtang (7D) functions, where noise was added to mimic real-world scenarios. Priors for these tasks were synthetically generated as 'well-located' (10% offset from optimum) or 'poorly located' (maximally offset, mode outside search space). Real-world HPO tasks included five 6D deep learning tasks from LCBench and three 4D deep learning HPO tasks from PD1. Priors for HPO tasks were obtained from MF-Prior-Bench. All methods were implemented in BoTorch using a squared exponential kernel (with some experiments also on Matérn-5/2). Hyperparameters (lengthscale, outputscale, observation noise, mean function constant) were estimated using MAP with `LN(0,1)` or `N(0,1)` priors and updated at every BO iteration. Initialization involved the mode of the prior followed by 2 Sobol samples (for synthetic tasks) or a single initial sample at the prior's mode (for HPO tasks). Baselines included vanilla LogEI, vanilla MES, πBO, and decoupled Thompson Sampling. Monte Carlo approximations used varying budgets for function samples, RFFs, resamples, and optimal values, depending on the task (e.g., 768 function samples, 2048 RFFs, 1.5e5 resamples for synthetic tasks).",
    "Limitations": "ColaBO's crucial reliance on multiple steps of Monte Carlo integration leads to significant computational expense, requiring tens of seconds per evaluation to achieve desired accuracy, which depends on the benchmark size. A major limitation is the exponential scaling of obtaining draws from the prior over the optimum (`ρ_x*`) with the dimensionality of the prior, making ColaBO potentially impractical for priors over more than a handful of variables. The framework also deviates from vanilla MC acquisition functions in three ways: it cannot utilize Quasi-MC for draws from the predictive posterior (only for RFF weights), it cannot fix base samples for acquisition function consistency across the search space, and the RFF approximation of the Gaussian Process prior introduces a bias, which is more pronounced for the Matérn 5/2 kernel compared to the squared exponential kernel.",
    "Future Research Directions": "Future work could focus on developing more accurate and computationally efficient sampling procedures for the belief-weighted prior, potentially incorporating methods like stochastic gradient descent for sampling. Another promising avenue is to explore variational inference or pre-trained model approaches to obtain a representative belief-biased model with an analytical posterior. Such developments would likely reduce ColaBO's runtime and broaden its applicability. Additionally, extending ColaBO to multi-fidelity optimization is suggested as a way to further increase its efficiency, especially for costly deep learning pipelines.",
    "Experiment Code": "def get_sobol_botorch_modular_saas_fully_bayesian_single_task_gp_qnei() -> BenchmarkMethod:\n    generation_strategy = GenerationStrategy(\n        name=\"SOBOL+BOTORCH_MODULAR::SaasFullyBayesianSingleTaskGP_qNoisyExpectedImprovement\",  # noqa\n        steps=[\n            GenerationStep(model=Models.SOBOL, num_trials=5, min_trials_observed=5),\n            GenerationStep(\n                model=Models.BOTORCH_MODULAR,\n                num_trials=-1,\n                max_parallelism=1,\n                model_kwargs={\n                    \"surrogate\": Surrogate(\n                        botorch_model_class=SaasFullyBayesianSingleTaskGP\n                    ),\n                    \"botorch_acqf_class\": qNoisyExpectedImprovement,\n                },\n            ),\n        ],\n    )\n\n    return BenchmarkMethod(\n        name=generation_strategy.name,\n        generation_strategy=generation_strategy,\n        scheduler_options=get_sequential_optimization_scheduler_options(),\n    )",
    "Experiment Result": "This code snippet configures a `BenchmarkMethod` for single-objective optimization using a `GenerationStrategy`. It uses `SaasFullyBayesianSingleTaskGP` as the surrogate model, which is a scalable fully Bayesian Gaussian Process, aligning with ColaBO's use of GP-based BO and scalable approximate function draws. The acquisition function employed is `qNoisyExpectedImprovement` (qNEI), a Monte Carlo (MC) acquisition function, directly corresponding to MC-EI mentioned in the ColaBO method. While this snippet does not explicitly show ColaBO's belief injection (ρ(f)), pathwise updates, or Random Fourier Features, it demonstrates the setup of core components that ColaBO integrates with or extends."

    "Title": "Multi-Fidelity Bayesian Optimization via Deep Neural Networks",
    "Main Contributions": "The paper proposes Deep Neural Network Multi-Fidelity Bayesian Optimization (DNN-MFBO) to address the issue that existing multi-fidelity BO methods often ignore or oversimplify the complex correlations across fidelities, leading to inefficient objective function estimation. DNN-MFBO uses deep neural networks to flexibly capture highly nonlinear and nonstationary relationships between fidelities, thereby improving objective function estimation and optimization performance. A key contribution is the development of a tractable and efficient method for computing a mutual information-based acquisition function using sequential, fidelity-wise Gauss-Hermite quadrature and moment-matching. The method demonstrates superior performance in synthetic benchmark datasets and real-world engineering design applications, achieving better optimization regrets with smaller or comparable query costs and higher computational efficiency compared to state-of-the-art multi-fidelity and single-fidelity BO algorithms.",
    "Methodology": "DNN-MFBO constructs a multi-fidelity model by stacking deep neural networks (NNs), where each NN models one fidelity. For fidelity 'm > 1', the input to the NN `xm` is formed by concatenating the original input `x` with the output from the previous fidelity `fm-1(x)`, thus propagating information. The output of each NN is defined as `fm(x) = w_m^T φ_θm(xm)`. To quantify uncertainty, only the NN weights in the output layer (`wm`) are treated as random variables with a standard normal prior, while all other weights (`θm`) are considered hyper-parameters. A stochastic variational learning algorithm is developed to jointly estimate the posterior distribution `q(wm)` (approximated as Gaussian) and optimize hyper-parameters by maximizing the evidence lower bound (ELBO) using the reparameterization trick. The acquisition function maximizes the mutual information `I(f*, fm(x)|D)` weighted by the query cost `λm`. To handle the nonlinear coupling between fidelity outputs, the posterior distribution `p(fm(x)|D)` is approximated as a Gaussian using fidelity-wise moment matching and Gauss-Hermite quadrature. The conditional entropy `H(fm(x)|f*, D)` is approximated by `H(fm(x)|fM(x) ≤ f*, D)` for `m < M`, which is also approximated as a Gaussian via moment matching and Gauss-Hermite quadrature. Acquisition function optimization is performed using L-BFGS with automatic differentiation.",
    "Experimental Setup": "The DNN-MFBO method was evaluated on three synthetic benchmark functions: Branin (2D input, 3 fidelities), Park1 (4D input, 2 fidelities), and Levy (2D input, 3 fidelities), all featuring nonlinear and/or nonstationary transformations between fidelities. Additionally, two real-world engineering design applications were used: Mechanical Plate Vibration Design (optimizing 3 material properties for frequency, 2 fidelities based on mesh density) and Thermal Conductor Design (optimizing 3 shape parameters for heat conduction speed, 2 fidelities based on mesh density). Competing methods included Multi-Fidelity Sequential Kriging (MF-SKO), MF-GP-UCB, Multi-Fidelity Predictive Entropy Search (MF-PES), Multi-Fidelity Maximum Entropy Search (MF-MES), Multi-Task NN based BO (MTNN-BO), and Single Fidelity MES (SF-MES). DNN-MFBO and MTNN-BO were implemented with TensorFlow, while others used their original implementations. Hyper-parameters for NNs (depth [2,12], width [32,512], learning rate [10e-5, 10e-1]) were tuned using SMAC3 and manual adjustments. Initial training data consisted of randomly queried points (e.g., Branin/Levy: 20, 20, 2 samples across fidelities; real-world: 20 low-fidelity, 5 high-fidelity). Query costs (λm) were set (e.g., 1, 10, 100 for 3 fidelities; 1, 10 for 2 fidelities). Performance was measured by Simple Regret (SR) and Inference Regret (IR) for synthetic tasks, and best queried function values for real-world applications. Average query time was also assessed. All experiments were repeated five times on a Linux workstation with a 16-core Intel Xeon CPU and 16GB RAM.",
    "Limitations": "The paper focuses exclusively on multi-fidelity Bayesian optimization for discrete fidelities, explicitly stating that it does not address continuous fidelities, which some other algorithms do. While the acquisition function calculation is shown to be computationally tractable and efficient due to analytical forms and automatic differentiation, the process itself is acknowledged as 'quite complex,' potentially implying a high degree of mathematical intricacy that could be challenging to implement or extend. Additionally, DNN-MFBO uses a random initialization for L-BFGS during acquisition function maximization, contrasting with some baselines that employ more expensive global optimization algorithms (e.g., DIRECT), which might impact the breadth of the search for the optimal query point, though the results suggest it still performs very well.",
    "Future Research Directions": "A direct future research direction, inferred from the paper's explicit scope, is to extend DNN-MFBO to handle continuous fidelities. Further areas could involve exploring alternative or more advanced neural network architectures for capturing inter-fidelity relationships, investigating different types of acquisition functions or enhancements for DNN-based surrogate models, and potentially refining the computational complexity of the acquisition function for even greater efficiency.",
    "Experiment Code": "#!/usr/bin/env python\n# Copyright (c) 2017 Zi Wang\nfrom push_world import *\nimport sys\n\n\nif __name__ == '__main__':\n    rx = float(sys.argv[1])\n    ry =     "Experiment Result": "The provided code defines a physics-based pushing simulation environment, implemented using the Box2D library, which serves as a benchmark or test function for optimization methods like DNN-MFBO. The core simulation logic resides in `push_world.py`, which sets up a 2D physics world with gravity (0,0), a simulation time step of 0.01 seconds (TARGET_FPS=100), and handles object creation and robotic end-effector movements. The simulation tracks object positions and applies forces/torques.\n\nThree specific simulation scenarios (objective functions) are defined:\n\n1.  **`generate_simudata3.py` (Single Robot, Single Object, Fixed Push Direction):**\n    *   **Objective:** Minimize the Euclidean distance between a circular object's final position and a target position (`gx`, `gy`).\n    *   **Setup:** A single 'rectangle' shaped robot end-effector (size 0.3x1) pushes a 'circle' shaped object (radius 1, friction 0.01, density 0.05). The robot's initial position (`rx`, `ry`) and the simulation duration (`simu_steps`) are configurable inputs.\n    *   **Push Mechanism:** The robot applies force to move towards the object, with the desired velocity fixed at a magnitude of 5 in the direction from the robot's hand to the object's position, plus Gaussian noise (std dev 0.1).\n\n2.  **`generate_simudata4.py` (Single Robot, Single Object, Controllable Push Velocity/Angle):**\n    *   **Objective:** Similar to `simudata3.py`, minimize the Euclidean distance between a circular object's final position and a target position (`gx`, `gy`).\n    *   **Setup:** Uses the same object and robot configurations as `simudata3.py`. Key configurable inputs include robot initial position (`rx`, `ry`), simulation duration (`simu_steps`), target position (`gx`, `gy`), and an explicit `init_angle` for the robot's initial orientation.\n    *   **Push Mechanism:** The robot applies force based on a derived velocity vector (`xvel`, `yvel`) from `rx`, `ry`. This vector is normalized and scaled by 10, with added Gaussian noise (std dev 0.1).\n\n3.  **`generate_simudata_2robot2thing.py` (Two Robots, Two Objects, Multi-Objective Sum):**\n    *   **Objective:** Minimize the sum of Euclidean distances for two separate objects. `ret1` (a 'rectangle' object, size 0.5x0.5) to `(gx, gy)`, and `ret2` (a 'circle' object, radius 1) to `(gx2, gy2)`.\n    *   **Setup:** Features two 'rectangle' shaped robot end-effectors (size 1x0.3) and two distinct objects. Multiple parameters are configurable for each robot and object, including positions (`rx`, `ry`, `rx2`, `ry2`), velocities (`xvel`, `yvel`, `xvel2`, `yvel2`), simulation durations (`simu_steps`, `simu_steps2`), initial angles (`init_angle`, `init_angle2`), and angular torques (`rtor`, `rtor2`).\n    *   **Push Mechanism:** Each robot applies wrench (linear velocity and angular torque) based on its respective input parameters, with added Gaussian noise (std dev 0.01 for velocity). The simulation runs for the maximum of the two robots' specified `simu_steps`.\n\nIn all scenarios, the simulation environment can be run without a graphical user interface (GUI) for faster execution by setting `do_gui=False` in `b2WorldInterface`."

    "Title": "Batch Multi-Fidelity Bayesian Optimization with  Deep Auto-Regressive Networks",
    "Main Contributions": "The paper proposes Batch Multi-Fidelity Bayesian Optimization with Deep Auto-Regressive Networks (BMBO-DARN) to optimize black-box, expensive-to-evaluate functions, particularly for hyperparameter optimization. Its main contributions include: 1) developing a deep auto-regressive model using Bayesian neural networks to capture complex, strong, non-linear, and non-stationary relationships across multiple fidelities, thereby enhancing surrogate learning; 2) designing a simple yet efficient batch querying method based on the Max-value Entropy Search (MES) principle, which explicitly penalizes highly correlated queries to encourage diversity and improve quality; 3) enabling efficient computation of the batch acquisition function via posterior samples and moment matching; and 4) introducing an alternating optimization algorithm to efficiently maximize the batch acquisition function over mixed continuous inputs and discrete fidelities, avoiding costly combinatorial searches. The approach demonstrates superior performance and reduced optimization costs across various real-world hyperparameter optimization tasks compared to state-of-the-art methods.",
    "Methodology": "BMBO-DARN employs a deep auto-regressive model as its probabilistic surrogate. This model consists of a chain of M Bayesian Neural Networks (BNNs), where each BNN `ψWm(·)` models a specific fidelity `fm(x)`. The input to the `m`-th BNN, `xm`, comprises the original input `x` and the outputs of all preceding fidelities (`f1(x), ..., fm-1(x)`), allowing it to capture intricate cross-fidelity correlations. Posterior inference for the BNN parameters (weights `W` and noise precisions `τ`) is performed using Hamiltonian Monte Carlo (HMC) sampling. For querying, a batch acquisition function is proposed, based on the Max-value Entropy Search (MES) principle. This function calculates the mutual information between the predicted values of a batch of `B` queries `({fmk(xk)}B k=1)` and the function optimum `f*`, normalized by the total query cost. To efficiently compute this acquisition function, the method approximates the joint posterior `p(f,f*|D)` as a multivariate Gaussian distribution using `L` independent posterior samples of the BNN weights and moment matching. To maximize the acquisition function and select a batch of `B` inputs `X` and fidelities `m`, an alternating optimization algorithm is used. It iteratively updates one `(xk, mk)` pair at a time while fixing the others, cycling through all pairs, which avoids the exponential complexity of combinatorial search over fidelities.",
    "Experimental Setup": "The effectiveness of BMBO-DARN was evaluated in two main settings: surrogate learning performance on synthetic benchmarks and hyperparameter optimization on real-world applications. For **surrogate learning**, two benchmark functions were used: Levy (two fidelities) and Branin (three fidelities), both featuring nonlinear/nonstationary transformations across fidelities. Models were trained on randomly generated examples (e.g., Levy: {130, 65} examples; Branin: {320, 130, 65} examples) and evaluated on 100 uniformly sampled test points. Performance was measured by Normalized Root-Mean-Square-Error (nRMSE) and Mean-Negative-Log-Likelihood (MNLL), averaged over five runs. The method was implemented using PyTorch and HMC sampling via the Hamiltorch library, with NNs featuring two hidden layers (40 neurons, tanh activation). Competing multi-fidelity models included MF-GP-UCB, MF-MES, SHTL, and DNN-MFBO. For **real-world hyperparameter optimization**, BMBO-DARN was applied to four popular machine learning models: 1) Convolutional Neural Networks (CNN) on CIFAR-10, optimizing 6 hyperparameters across 1, 10, 50 epochs (cost ratio 1:10:50, metric: negative log-loss); 2) Online Latent Dirichlet Allocation (LDA) on 20NewsGroups, optimizing 8 hyperparameters across 1, 10, 50 epochs (metric: perplexity); 3) XGBoost for diabetes diagnosis, optimizing 7 hyperparameters across 2, 10, 100 weak learners (cost ratio 1:5:50, metric: log nRMSE); and 4) Physics-Informed Neural Networks (PINN) for Burger's equation, optimizing 3 hyperparameters across 10, 100, 50K L-BFGS iterations (cost ratio 1:10:50, metric: log nRMSE). All batch querying methods used a batch size of 5. Each method started with 10 random initial queries at each fidelity and ran for up to 100 total queries, with results averaged over five runs. Additional baselines included MF-MES-Batch, SF-Batch, SMAC3, Hyperband, BOHB, and a single-query version of their method (BMBO-DARN-1). Query generation time (including surrogate training) was also evaluated for efficiency.",
    "Limitations": "The paper does not explicitly list limitations of BMBO-DARN. However, implicitly, some aspects could be considered: 1) The use of Hamiltonian Monte Carlo (HMC) for posterior inference, while providing high-quality uncertainty quantification, can be computationally intensive, though the authors note that other approximate inference approaches could be used. 2) The approximation of the joint posterior `p(f,f*|D)` as a multivariate Gaussian distribution using moment matching is an inherent approximation that might impact the accuracy of the acquisition function. 3) The alternating optimization algorithm for maximizing the batch acquisition function is a heuristic designed to avoid combinatorial search, meaning it does not guarantee finding the global optimum for the batch of queries.",
    "Future Research Directions": "Not mentioned",

    "Experiment Result": null

    "Title": "Supervising the Multi-Fidelity Race of Hyperparameter Configurations",
    "Main Contributions": "The paper introduces DyHPO, a Bayesian Optimization (BO) method for multi-fidelity (gray-box) hyperparameter optimization (HPO) that addresses the sub-optimal budget allocation and poor rank correlation issues of existing methods. DyHPO learns to dynamically decide which hyperparameter configurations to train further in a 'dynamic race'. The core contributions include a novel Bayesian surrogate model that predicts validation scores based on hyperparameter configurations, budget information, and learning curves via a deep kernel Gaussian Process. It also introduces a multi-fidelity extension of Expected Improvement as an acquisition function. DyHPO is demonstrated to achieve state-of-the-art results in HPO and neural architecture search, outperforming seven strong baselines with statistically significant margins, converging faster, and efficiently allocating computational budget across diverse deep learning architectures and datasets.",
    "Methodology": "DyHPO employs a Bayesian Optimization approach using a Gaussian Process (GP) surrogate model. The key methodological components are: (1) A novel deep kernel for the Gaussian Process, K(θ, w), which embeds learning curve dynamics. This kernel uses a neural network φ as a feature extractor, which takes the hyperparameter configuration, normalized budget, and the past learning curve as input. The learning curve is processed by a one-dimensional convolution followed by global max pooling. A squared exponential kernel k is then applied to the features extracted by φ. (2) A Multi-Fidelity Expected Improvement (EIMF) acquisition function, which extends the traditional Expected Improvement to consider multi-budget information. It uses a budget-specific incumbent ymax_j, which is the best observed performance for that specific budget or the overall best if no observations exist for that budget. (3) A dynamic budget allocation strategy, where at each HPO step, the algorithm dynamically decides which candidate configuration to train for exactly one additional budget unit, ensuring exploration by slowly increasing the budget for promising candidates. Model parameters (θ, w) are optimized via maximum likelihood using gradient descent and Adam.",
    "Experimental Setup": "DyHPO was evaluated across three distinct settings: hyperparameter optimization for tabular, text, and image classification. Experiments were run on an Amazon EC2 M5 Instance (m5.xlarge). Performance was measured using mean regret and average rank over ten repetitions, with statistical significance assessed using the Friedman test followed by a Wilcoxon signed-rank test (α = 0.05). \n\nBenchmarks included:\n- **LCBench:** A learning curve benchmark with 35 tabular datasets, featuring 2,000 neural network configurations per dataset, trained for 50 epochs with Auto-PyTorch. The search space involved 7 numerical hyperparameters for MLP architectures.\n- **TaskSet:** A benchmark focusing on 12 NLP tasks using RNN architectures, with the Adam8p search space covering 8 continuous hyperparameters. Scores were reported every 200 iterations (steps).\n- **NAS-Bench-201:** A neural architecture search benchmark comprising 15,625 precomputed architectures on CIFAR-10, CIFAR-100, and ImageNet datasets. It involved a search space of 6 categorical hyperparameters, with each architecture trained for 200 epochs.\n\nBaselines for comparison included Random Search, HyperBand, BOHB, DEHB, ASHA, MF-DNN, and Dragonfly (BOCA). An ablation study was conducted to assess the impact of the learning curve input in the deep kernel by comparing DyHPO with a variant that excludes it (DyHPO w/o CNN).",
    "Limitations": "The identified limitations of DyHPO include: (1) A lack of existing tabular benchmarks for hyperparameter optimization on very large deep learning models, such as Transformers-based architectures, which restricts the full characterization of DyHPO's scalability in this domain. (2) The pause and resume training procedure, which is central to multi-fidelity HPO, is only applicable when tuning hyperparameters of parametric models. For other scenarios, training of a hyperparameter configuration would necessitate a full restart. (3) For small datasets that can be trained quickly, the inherent computational overhead of model-based techniques like DyHPO might make simpler approaches, such as random search, more appealing and practically justified.",
    "Future Research Directions": "While not explicitly outlined in a dedicated section, the paper implies several future research directions. These include: (1) Expanding evaluation and development to very large deep learning models (e.g., Transformers) by addressing the current lack of suitable tabular benchmarks. (2) Exploring ways to generalize the pause and resume training procedure to non-parametric models, where current multi-fidelity methods would require restarts. (3) Investigating methods to reduce the computational overhead of model-based HPO techniques to make them more appealing for fast-training tasks on small datasets. (4) The paper also encourages the community to create sparse benchmarks with surrogates to save energy, which could guide future benchmark design for efficient HPO research.",
    "Experiment Code": "class DyHPOAlgorithm:\n\n    def __init__(\n        self,\n        hp_candidates: np.ndarray,\n        log_indicator: List,\n        seed: int = 11,\n 'unknown',\n        output_path: str = '.',\n        surrogate_config: dict = None,\n        verbose: bool = True,\n    ):\n        \"\"\"\n        Args:\n            hp_candidates: np.ndarray\n                The full list of hyperparameter candidates for\n                a given dataset.\n            log_indicator: List\n                A list with boolean values indicating if a\n                hyperparameter has been log sampled or not.\n            seed: int\n                The seed that will be used for the surrogate.\n            max_benchmark_epochs: int\n                The maximal budget that a hyperparameter configuration\n                has been evaluated in the benchmark for.\n            fantasize_step: int\n                The number of steps for which we are looking ahead to\n                evaluate the performance of a hpc.\n            minimization: bool\n                If the objective should be maximized or minimized.\n            total_budget: int\n                The total budget given for hyperparameter optimization.\n            device: str\n                The device where the experiment will be run on.\n            dataset_name: str\n                The name of the dataset that the experiment will be run on.\n            output_path: str\n                The path where all the output will be stored.\n            surrogate_config: dict\n                The model configurations for the surrogate.\n            verbose: boolean\n                If detailed information is preferred in the log file.\n        \"\"\"\n        torch.backends.cudnn.deterministic = True\n        torch.backends.cudnn.benchmark = False\n        torch.manual_seed(seed)\n        np.random.seed(seed)\n\n        if device is None:\n            self.dev = torch.device(\n                'cuda') if torch.cuda.is_available() else torch.device('cpu')\n        else:\n            self.dev = torch.device(device)\n\n        self.hp_candidates = hp_candidates\n        self.log_indicator = log_indicator\n\n        self.scaler = MinMaxScaler()\n        self.hp_candidates = self.preprocess_hp_candidates()\n\n        self.minimization = minimization\n        self.seed = seed\n\n        if verbose:\n            logging_level = logging.DEBUG\n        else:\n            logging_level = logging.INFO\n        self.logger = logging.getLogger()\n\n        logging.basicConfig(\n            format='%(levelname)s:%(asctime)s:%(message)s',\n            filename=f'dyhpo_surrogate_{dataset_name}_{seed}.log',\n            level=logging_level,\n        )\n\n        # the keys will be hyperparameter indices while the value\n        # will be a list with all the budgets evaluated for examples\n        # and with all performances for the performances\n        self.examples = dict()\n        self.performances = dict()\n\n        # set a seed already, so that it is deterministic when\n        # generating the seeds of the ensemble\n        torch.manual_seed(seed)\n        np.random.seed(seed)\n\n        self.max_benchmark_epochs = max_benchmark_epochs\n        self.total_budget = total_budget\n        self.fantasize_step = fantasize_step\n        self.nr_features = self.hp_candidates.shape[1]\n\n        initial_configurations_nr = 1\n        conf_individual_budget = 1\n        self.init_conf_indices = np.random.choice(self.hp_candidates.shape[0], initial_configurations_nr, replace=False)\n        self.init_budgets = [conf_individual_budget] * initial_configurations_nr\n        # with what percentage configurations will be taken randomly instead of being sampled from the model\n        self.fraction_random_configs = 0.1\n\n        self.model = None\n        # An index keeping track of where we are in the init_conf_indices\n        # list of hyperparmeters that are not sampled from the model.\n        self.initial_random_index = 0\n\n        if surrogate_config is None:\n            self.surrogate_config = {\n                'nr_layers': 2,\n                'nr_initial_features': self.nr_features,\n                'layer1_units': 64,\n                'layer2_units': 128,\n                'cnn_nr_channels': 4,\n                'cnn_kernel_size': 3,\n                'batch_size': 64,\n                'nr_epochs': 1000,\n                'nr_patience_epochs': 10,\n                'learning_rate': 0.001,\n            }\n        else:\n            self.surrogate_config = surrogate_config\n\n        # the incumbent value observed during the hpo process.\n        self.best_value_observed = np.NINF\n        # a set which will keep track of the hyperparameter configurations that diverge.\n        self.diverged_configs = set()\n\n        # info dict to drop every surrogate iteration\n        self.info_dict = dict()\n\n        # the start time for the overhead of every surrogate optimization iteration\n        # will be recorded here\n        self.suggest_time_duration = 0\n        # the total budget consumed so far\n        self.budget_spent = 0\n\n        self.output_path = output_path\n        self.dataset_name = dataset_name\n\n        self.no_improvement_threshold = int(self.max_benchmark_epochs + 0.2 * self.max_benchmark_epochs)\n        self.no_improvement_patience = 0\n\n\n    def _prepare_dataset_and_budgets(self) -> Dict[str, torch.Tensor]:\n        \"\"\"\n        Prepare the data that will be the input to the surrogate.\n\n        Returns:\n            data: A Dictionary that contains inside the training examples,\n            the budgets, the curves and lastly the labels.\n        \"\"\"\n\n        train_examples, train_labels, train_budgets, train_curves = self.history_configurations()\n\n        train_examples = np.array(train_examples, dtype=np.single)\n        train_labels = np.array(train_labels, dtype=np.single)\n        train_budgets = np.array(train_budgets, dtype=np.single)\n        train_curves = self.patch_curves_to_same_length(train_curves)\n        train_curves = np.array(train_curves, dtype=np.single)\n\n        # scale budgets to [0, 1]\n        train_budgets = train_budgets / self.max_benchmark_epochs\n\n        train_examples = torch.tensor(train_examples)\n        train_labels = torch.tensor(train_labels)\n        train_budgets = torch.tensor(train_budgets)\n        train_curves = torch.tensor(train_curves)\n\n        train_examples = train_examples.to(device=self.dev)\n        train_labels = train_labels.to(device=self.dev)\n        train_budgets = train_budgets.to(device=self.dev)\n        train_curves = train_curves.to(device=self.dev)\n\n        data = {\n            'X_train': train_examples,\n            'train_budgets': train_budgets,\n            'train_curves': train_curves,\n            'y_train': train_labels,\n        }\n\n        return data\n\n    def _train_surrogate(self):\n        \"\"\"\n        Train the surrogate model.\n        \"\"\"\n        data = self._prepare_dataset_and_budgets()\n        self.logger.info(f'Started training the model')\n\n        self.model.train_pipeline(\n            data,\n            load_checkpoint=False,\n        )\n\n    def _predict(self) -> Tuple[np.ndarray, np.ndarray, List, List]:\n        \"\"\"\n        Predict the performances of the hyperparameter configurations\n        as well as the standard deviations based on the surrogate model.\n\n        Returns:\n            mean_predictions, std_predictions, hp_indices, non_scaled_budgets:\n                The mean predictions and the standard deviations over\n                all model predictions for the given hyperparameter\n                configurations with their associated indices, scaled and\n                non-scaled budgets.\n        \"\"\"\n        configurations, hp_indices, budgets, learning_curves = self.generate_candidate_configurations()\n        budgets = np.array(budgets, dtype=np.single)\n        non_scaled_budgets = copy.deepcopy(budgets)\n        # scale budgets to [0, 1]\n        budgets = budgets / self.max_benchmark_epochs\n\n        configurations = np.array(configurations, dtype=np.single)\n        configurations = torch.tensor(configurations)\n        configurations = configurations.to(device=self.dev)\n\n        budgets = torch.tensor(budgets)\n        budgets = budgets.to(device=self.dev)\n\n        learning_curves = self.patch_curves_to_same_length(learning_curves)\n        learning_curves = np.array(learning_curves, dtype=np.single)\n        learning_curves = torch.tensor(learning_curves)\n        learning_curves = learning_curves.to(device=self.dev)\n\n        train_data = self._prepare_dataset_and_budgets()\n        test_data = {\n            'X_test': configurations,\n            'test_budgets': budgets,\n            'test_curves': learning_curves,\n        }\n\n        mean_predictions, std_predictions = self.model.predict_pipeline(train_data, test_data)\n\n        return mean_predictions, std_predictions, hp_indices, non_scaled_budgets\n\n    def suggest(self) -> Tuple[int, int]:\n        \"\"\"\n        Suggest a hyperparameter configuration to be evaluated next.\n\n        Returns:\n            best_config_index, budget: The index of the hyperparamter\n                configuration to be evaluated and the budget for\n                what it is going to be evaluated for.\n        \"\"\"\n        suggest_time_start = time.time()\n        # check if we still have random hyperparameters to evaluate\n        if self.initial_random_index < len(self.init_conf_indices):\n            self.logger.info(\n                'Not enough configurations to build a model. '\n                'Returning randomly sampled configuration'\n            )\n\n            random_indice = self.init_conf_indices[self.initial_random_index]\n            budget = self.init_budgets[self.initial_random_index]\n            self.initial_random_index += 1\n\n            return random_indice, budget\n        else:\n            mean_predictions, std_predictions, hp_indices, non_scaled_budgets = self._predict()\n            best_prediction_index = self.find_suggested_config(\n                mean_predictions,\n                std_predictions,\n                non_scaled_budgets,\n            )\n            \"\"\"\n            the best prediction index is not always matching with the actual hp index.\n            Since when evaluating the acq function, we do not consider hyperparameter\n            candidates that diverged or that are evaluated fully.\n            \"\"\"\n            best_config_index = hp_indices[best_prediction_index]\n\n            # decide for what budget we will evaluate the most\n            # promising hyperparameter configuration next.\n            if best_config_index in self.examples:\n                evaluated_budgets = self.examples[best_config_index]\n                max_budget = max(evaluated_budgets)\n                budget = max_budget + self.fantasize_step\n                # this would only trigger if fantasize_step is bigger\n                # than 1\n                if budget > self.max_benchmark_epochs:\n                    budget = self.max_benchmark_epochs\n            else:\n                budget = self.fantasize_step\n\n        suggest_time_end = time.time()\n        self.suggest_time_duration = suggest_time_end - suggest_time_start\n\n        self.budget_spent += self.fantasize_step\n\n        # exhausted hpo budget, finish.\n        if self.budget_spent > self.total_budget:\n            exit(0)\n\n        return best_config_index, budget\n\n    def observe(\n        self,\n        hp_index: int,\n        b: int,\n        learning_curve: np.ndarray,\n        alg_time: Optional[float] = None,\n    ):\n        \"\"\"\n        Args:\n            hp_index: The index of the evaluated hyperparameter configuration.\n            b: The budget for which the hyperparameter configuration was evaluated.\n            learning_curve: The learning curve of the hyperparameter configuration.\n            alg_time: The time taken from the algorithm to evaluate the hp configuration.\n        \"\"\"\n        score = learning_curve[-1]\n        # if y is an undefined value, append 0 as the overhead since we finish here.\n        if np.isnan(learning_curve).any():\n            self.update_info_dict(hp_index, b, np.nan, 0)\n            self.diverged_configs.add(hp_index)\n            return\n\n        observe_time_start = time.time()\n\n        self.examples[hp_index] = np.arange(1, b + 1).tolist()\n        self.performances[hp_index] = learning_curve\n\n        if self.best_value_observed < score:\n            self.best_value_observed = score\n            self.no_improvement_patience = 0\n        else:\n            self.no_improvement_patience += 1\n\n        observe_time_end = time.time()\n        train_time_duration = 0\n\n        # initialization phase over. Now we can sample from the model.\n        if self.initial_random_index >= len(self.init_conf_indices):\n            train_time_start = time.time()\n            # create the model for the first time\n            if self.model is None:\n                # Starting a model from scratch\n                self.model = DyHPO(\n                    self.surrogate_config,\n                    self.dev,\n                    self.dataset_name,\n                    self.output_path,\n                    self.seed,\n                )\n\n            if self.no_improvement_patience == self.no_improvement_threshold:\n                self.model.restart = True\n\n            self._train_surrogate()\n\n            train_time_end = time.time()\n            train_time_duration = train_time_end - train_time_start\n\n        observe_time_duration = observe_time_end - observe_time_start\n        total_duration = observe_time_duration + self.suggest_time_duration + train_time_duration\n        if alg_time is not None:\n            total_duration = total_duration + alg_time\n\n        self.update_info_dict(hp_index, b, score, total_duration)\n\n    def prepare_examples(self, hp_indices: List) -> List[np.ndarray]:\n        \"\"\"\n        Prepare the examples to be given to the surrogate model.\n\n        Args:\n            hp_indices: The list of hp indices that are already evaluated.\n\n        Returns:\n            examples: A list of the hyperparameter configurations.\n        \"\"\"\n        examples = []\n        for hp_index in hp_indices:\n            examples.append(self.hp_candidates[hp_index])\n\n        return examples\n\n    def generate_candidate_configurations(\n        self,\n    ) -> Tuple[List, List, List, List]:\n        \"\"\"\n        Generate candidate configurations that will be\n        fantasized upon.\n\n        Returns:\n            (configurations, hp_indices, hp_budgets, learning_curves): Tuple\n                A tuple of configurations, their indices in the hp list\n                and the budgets that they should be fantasized upon.\n        \"\"\"\n        hp_indices = []\n        hp_budgets = []\n        learning_curves = []\n\n        for hp_index in range(0, self.hp_candidates.shape[0]):\n\n            if hp_index in self.examples:\n                budgets = self.examples[hp_index]\n                # Take the max budget evaluated for a certain hpc\n                max_budget = max(budgets)\n                next_budget = max_budget + self.fantasize_step\n                # take the learning curve until the point we have evaluated so far\n                curve = self.performances[hp_index][:max_budget]\n                # if the curve is shorter than the length of the kernel size,\n                # pad it with zeros\n                difference_curve_length = self.surrogate_config['cnn_kernel_size'] - len(curve)\n                if difference_curve_length > 0:\n                    curve.extend([0.0] * difference_curve_length)\n            else:\n                # The hpc was not evaluated before, so fantasize its\n                # performance\n                next_budget = self.fantasize_step\n                curve = [0, 0, 0]\n\n            # this hyperparameter configuration is not evaluated fully\n            if next_budget <= self.max_benchmark_epochs:\n                hp_indices.append(hp_index)\n                hp_budgets.append(next_budget)\n                learning_curves.append(curve)\n\n        configurations = self.prepare_examples(hp_indices)\n\n        return configurations, hp_indices, hp_budgets, learning_curves\n\n    def history_configurations(\n        self,\n    ) -> Tuple[List, List, List, List]:\n        \"\"\"\n        Generate the configurations, labels, budgets and curves based on\n        the history of evaluated configurations.\n\n        Returns:\n            (train_examples, train_labels, train_budgets, train_curves):\n                A tuple of examples, labels, budgets and curves for the\n                configurations evaluated so far.\n        \"\"\"\n        train_examples = []\n        train_labels = []\n        train_budgets = []\n        train_curves = []\n\n        for hp_index in self.examples:\n            budgets = self.examples[hp_index]\n            performances = self.performances[hp_index]\n            example = self.hp_candidates[hp_index]\n\n            for budget, performance in zip(budgets, performances):\n                train_examples.append(example)\n                train_budgets.append(budget)\n                train_labels.append(performance)\n                train_curve = performances[:budget - 1] if budget > 1 else [0.0]\n                difference_curve_length = self.surrogate_config['cnn_kernel_size'] - len(train_curve)\n                if difference_curve_length > 0:\n                    train_curve.extend([0.0] * difference_curve_length)\n\n                train_curves.append(train_curve)\n\n        return train_examples, train_labels, train_budgets, train_curves\n\n    def acq(\n        self,\n        best_value: float,\n        mean: float,\n        std: float,\n        explore_factor: Optional[float] = 0.25,\n        acq_fc: str = 'ei',\n    ) -> float:\n        \"\"\"\n        The acquisition function that will be called\n        to evaluate the score of a hyperparameter configuration.\n\n        Parameters\n        ----------\n        best_value: float\n            Best observed function evaluation. Individual per fidelity.\n        mean: float\n            Point mean of the posterior process.\n        std: float\n            Point std of the posterior process.\n        explore_factor: float\n            The exploration factor for when ucb is used as the\n            acquisition function.\n        ei_calibration_factor: float\n            The factor used to calibrate expected improvement.\n        acq_fc: str\n            The type of acquisition function to use.\n\n        Returns\n        -------\n        acq_value: float\n            The value of the acquisition function.\n        \"\"\"\n        if acq_fc == 'ei':\n            if std == 0:\n                return 0\n            z = (mean - best_value) / std\n            acq_value = (mean - best_value) * norm.cdf(z) + std * norm.pdf(z)\n        elif acq_fc == 'ucb':\n            acq_value = mean + explore_factor * std\n        elif acq_fc == 'thompson':\n            acq_value = np.random.normal(mean, std)\n        elif acq_fc == 'exploit':\n            acq_value = mean\n        else:\n            raise NotImplementedError(\n                f'Acquisition function {acq_fc} has not been'\n                f'implemented',\n            )\n\n        return acq_value\n\n    def find_suggested_config(\n        self,\n        mean_predictions: np.ndarray,\n        mean_stds: np.ndarray,\n        budgets: List,\n    ) -> int:\n        \"\"\"\n        Find the hyperparameter configuration that has the highest score\n        with the acquisition function.\n\n        Args:\n            mean_predictions: The mean predictions of the posterior.\n            mean_stds: The mean standard deviations of the posterior.\n            budgets: The next budgets that the hyperparameter configurations\n                will be evaluated for.\n\n        Returns:\n            best_index: The index of the hyperparameter configuration with the\n                highest score.\n        \"\"\"\n        highest_acq_value = np.NINF\n        best_index = -1\n\n        index = 0\n        for mean_value, std in zip(mean_predictions, mean_stds):\n            budget = int(budgets[index])\n            best_value = self.calculate_fidelity_ymax(budget)\n            acq_value = self.acq(best_value, mean_value, std, acq_fc='ei')\n            if acq_value > highest_acq_value:\n                highest_acq_value = acq_value\n                best_index = index\n\n            index += 1\n\n        return best_index\n\n    def calculate_fidelity_ymax(self, fidelity: int):\n        \"\"\"\n        Find ymax for a given fidelity level.\n\n        If there are hyperparameters evaluated for that fidelity\n        take the maximum from their values. Otherwise, take\n        the maximum from all previous fidelity levels for the\n        hyperparameters that we have evaluated.\n\n        Args:\n            fidelity: The fidelity of the hyperparameter\n                configuration.\n\n        Returns:\n            best_value: The best value seen so far for the\n                given fidelity.\n        \"\"\"\n        exact_fidelity_config_values = []\n        lower_fidelity_config_values = []\n\n        for example_index in self.examples.keys():\n            try:\n                performance = self.performances[example_index][fidelity - 1]\n                exact_fidelity_config_values.append(performance)\n            except IndexError:\n                learning_curve = self.performances[example_index]\n                # The hyperparameter was not evaluated until fidelity, or more.\n                # Take the maximum value from the curve.\n                lower_fidelity_config_values.append(max(learning_curve))\n\n        if len(exact_fidelity_config_values) > 0:\n            # lowest error corresponds to best value\n            best_value = max(exact_fidelity_config_values)\n        else:\n            best_value = max(lower_fidelity_config_values)\n\n        return best_value\n\n    def update_info_dict(\n        self,\n        hp_index: int,\n        budget: int,\n        performance: float,\n        overhead: float,\n    ):\n        \"\"\"\n        Update the info dict with the current HPO iteration info.\n\n        Dump a new json file that will update with additional information\n        given the current HPO iteration.\n\n        Args:\n            hp_index: The index of the hyperparameter configuration.\n            budget: The budget of the hyperparameter configuration.\n            performance:  The performance of the hyperparameter configuration.\n            overhead: The total overhead (in seconds) of the iteration.\n        \"\"\"\n        hp_index = int(hp_index)\n        if 'hp' in self.info_dict:\n            self.info_dict['hp'].append(hp_index)\n        else:\n            self.info_dict['hp'] = [hp_index]\n\n        if 'scores' in self.info_dict:\n            self.info_dict['scores'].append(performance)\n        else:\n            self.info_dict['scores'] = [performance]\n\n        if 'curve' in self.info_dict:\n            self.info_dict['curve'].append(self.best_value_observed)\n        else:\n            self.info_dict['curve'] = [self.best_value_observed]\n\n        if 'epochs' in self.info_dict:\n            self.info_dict['epochs'].append(budget)\n        else:\n            self.info_dict['epochs'] = [budget]\n\n        if 'overhead' in self.info_dict:\n            self.info_dict['overhead'].append(overhead)\n        else:\n            self.info_dict['overhead'] = [overhead]\n\n        with open(os.path.join(self.output_path, f'{self.dataset_name}_{self.seed}.json'), 'w') as fp:\n            json.dump(self.info_dict, fp)\n\n    def preprocess_hp_candidates(self) -> List:\n        \"\"\"\n        Preprocess the list of all hyperparameter candidates\n        by  performing a log transform for the hyperparameters that\n        were log sampled.\n\n        Returns:\n            log_hp_candidates: The list of all hyperparameter configurations\n                where hyperparameters that were log sampled are log transformed.\n        \"\"\"\n        log_hp_candidates = []\n\n        for hp_candidate in self.hp_candidates:\n            new_hp_candidate = []\n            for index, hp_value in enumerate(hp_candidate):\n                new_hp_candidate.append(math.log(hp_value) if self.log_indicator[index] else hp_value)\n\n            log_hp_candidates.append(new_hp_candidate)\n\n        log_hp_candidates = np.array(log_hp_candidates)\n        # scaler for the hp configurations\n\n        log_hp_candidates = self.scaler.fit_transform(log_hp_candidates)\n\n        return log_hp_candidates\n\n    @staticmethod\n    def patch_curves_to_same_length(curves):\n        \"\"\"\n        Patch the given curves to the same length.\n\n        Finds the maximum curve length and patches all\n        other curves that are shorter in length with zeroes.\n\n        Args:\n            curves: The given hyperparameter curves.\n\n        Returns:\n            curves: The updated array where the learning\n                curves are of the same length.\n        \"\"\"\n        max_curve_length = 0\n        for curve in curves:\n            if len(curve) > max_curve_length:\n                max_curve_length = len(curve)\n\n        for curve in curves:\n            difference = max_curve_length - len(curve)\n            if difference > 0:\n                curve.extend([0.0] * difference)\n\n        return curves\n\nclass FeatureExtractor(nn.Module):\n    \"\"\"\n    The feature extractor that is part of the deep kernel.\n    \"\"\"\n    def __init__(self, configuration):\n        super(FeatureExtractor, self).__init__()\n\n        self.configuration = configuration\n\n        self.nr_layers = configuration['nr_layers']\n        self.act_func = nn.LeakyReLU()\n        # adding one to the dimensionality of the initial input features\n        # for the concatenation with the budget.\n        initial_features = configuration['nr_initial_features'] + 1\n        self.fc1 = nn.Linear(initial_features, configuration['layer1_units'])\n        self.bn1 = nn.BatchNorm1d(configuration['layer1_units'])\n        for i in range(2, self.nr_layers):\n            setattr(\n                self,\n                f'fc{i + 1}',\n                nn.Linear(configuration[f'layer{i - 1}_units'], configuration[f'layer{i}_units']),\n            )\n            setattr(\n                self,\n                f'bn{i + 1}',\n                nn.BatchNorm1d(configuration[f'layer{i}_units']),\n            )\n\n\n        setattr(\n            self,\n            f'fc{self.nr_layers}',\n            nn.Linear(\n                configuration[f'layer{self.nr_layers - 1}_units'] +\n                configuration['cnn_nr_channels'],  # accounting for the learning curve features\n                configuration[f'layer{self.nr_layers}_units']\n            ),\n        )\n        self.cnn = nn.Sequential(\n            nn.Conv1d(in_channels=1, kernel_size=(configuration['cnn_kernel_size'],), out_channels=4),\n            nn.AdaptiveMaxPool1d(1),\n        )\n\n    def forward(self, x, budgets, learning_curves):\n\n        # add an extra dimensionality for the budget\n        # making it nr_rows x 1.\n        budgets = torch.unsqueeze(budgets, dim=1)\n        # concatenate budgets with examples\n        x = cat((x, budgets), dim=1)\n        x = self.fc1(x)\n        x = self.act_func(self.bn1(x))\n\n        for i in range(2, self.nr_layers):\n            x = self.act_func(\n                getattr(self, f'bn{i}')(\n                    getattr(self, f'fc{i}')(\n                        x\n                    )\n                )\n            )\n\n        # add an extra dimensionality for the learning curve\n        # making it nr_rows x 1 x lc_values.\n        learning_curves = torch.unsqueeze(learning_curves, 1)\n        lc_features = self.cnn(learning_curves)\n        # revert the output from the cnn into nr_rows x nr_kernels.\n        lc_features = torch.squeeze(lc_features, 2)\n\n        # put learning curve features into the last layer along with the higher level features.\n        x = cat((x, lc_features), dim=1)\n        x = self.act_func(getattr(self, f'fc{self.nr_layers}')(x))\n\n        return x\n\n\nclass GPRegressionModel(gpytorch.models.ExactGP):\n    \"\"\"\n    A simple GP model.\n    \"\"\"\n    def __init__(\n        self,\n        train_x: torch.Tensor,\n        train_y: torch.Tensor,\n        likelihood: gpytorch.likelihoods.GaussianLikelihood,\n    ):\n        \"\"\"\n        Constructor of the GPRegressionModel.\n\n        Args:\n            train_x: The initial train examples for the GP.\n            train_y: The initial train labels for the GP.\n            likelihood: The likelihood to be used.\n        \"\"\"\n        super(GPRegressionModel, self).__init__(train_x, train_y, likelihood)\n\n        self.mean_module = gpytorch.means.ConstantMean()\n        self.covar_module = gpytorch.kernels.ScaleKernel(gpytorch.kernels.RBFKernel())\n\n    def forward(self, x):\n\n        mean_x = self.mean_module(x)\n        covar_x = self.covar_module(x)\n\n        return gpytorch.distributions.MultivariateNormal(mean_x, covar_x)\n\n\nclass DyHPO:\n    \"\"\"\n    The DyHPO DeepGP model.\n    \"\"\"\n    def __init__(\n        self,\n        configuration: Dict,\n        device: torch.device,\n        dataset_name: str = 'unknown',\n        output_path: str = '.',\n        seed: int = 11,\n    ):\n        \"\"\"\n        The constructor for the DyHPO model.\n\n        Args:\n            configuration: The configuration to be used\n                for the different parts of the surrogate.\n            device: The device where the experiments will be run on.\n            dataset_name: The name of the dataset for the current run.\n            output_path: The path where the intermediate/final results\n                will be stored.\n            seed: The seed that will be used to store the checkpoint\n                properly.\n        \"\"\"\n        super(DyHPO, self).__init__()\n        self.feature_extractor = FeatureExtractor(configuration)\n        self.batch_size = configuration['batch_size']\n        self.nr_epochs = configuration['nr_epochs']\n        self.early_stopping_patience = configuration['nr_patience_epochs']\n        self.refine_epochs = 50\n        self.dev = device\n        self.seed = seed\n        self.model, self.likelihood, self.mll = \\\n            self.get_model_likelihood_mll(\n                configuration[f'layer{self.feature_extractor.nr_layers}_units']\n            )\n\n        self.model.to(self.dev)\n        self.likelihood.to(self.dev)\n        self.feature_extractor.to(self.dev)\n\n        self.optimizer = torch.optim.Adam([\n            {'params': self.model.parameters(), 'lr': configuration['learning_rate']},\n            {'params': self.feature_extractor.parameters(), 'lr': configuration['learning_rate']}],\n        )\n\n        self.configuration = configuration\n        # the number of initial points for which we will retrain fully from scratch\n        # This is basically equal to the dimensionality of the search space + 1.\n        self.initial_nr_points = 10\n        # keeping track of the total hpo iterations. It will be used during the optimization\n        # process to switch from fully training the model, to refining.\n        self.iterations = 0\n        # flag for when the optimization of the model should start from scratch.\n        self.restart = True\n\n        self.logger = logging.getLogger(__name__)\n\n        self.checkpoint_path = os.path.join(\n            output_path,\n            'checkpoints',\n            f'{dataset_name}',\n            f'{self.seed}',\n        )\n\n        os.makedirs(self.checkpoint_path, exist_ok=True)\n\n        self.checkpoint_file = os.path.join(\n            self.checkpoint_path,\n            'checkpoint.pth'\n        )\n\n    def restart_optimization(self):\n        \"\"\"\n        Restart the surrogate model from scratch.\n        \"\"\"\n        self.feature_extractor = FeatureExtractor(self.configuration).to(self.dev)\n        self.model, self.likelihood, self.mll = \\\n            self.get_model_likelihood_mll(\n                self.configuration[f'layer{self.feature_extractor.nr_layers}_units'],\n            )\n\n        self.optimizer = torch.optim.Adam([\n            {'params': self.model.parameters(), 'lr': self.configuration['learning_rate']},\n            {'params': self.feature_extractor.parameters(), 'lr': self.configuration['learning_rate']}],\n        )\n\n    def get_model_likelihood_mll(\n        self,\n        train_size: int,\n    ) -> Tuple[GPRegressionModel, gpytorch.likelihoods.GaussianLikelihood, gpytorch.mlls.ExactMarginalLogLikelihood]:\n        \"\"\"\n        Called when the surrogate is first initialized or restarted.\n\n        Args:\n            train_size: The size of the current training set.\n\n        Returns:\n            model, likelihood, mll -
