    "Title": "Federated Hyperparameter Tuning: Challenges, Baselines, and Connections to Weight-Sharing",
    "Main Contributions": "The paper investigates federated hyperparameter tuning, identifying key challenges: federated validation data, extreme resource limitations, and costly personalization evaluation. It proposes baselines by adapting standard non-federated algorithms (Random Search, Successive Halving). The main contribution is FedEx, a novel method that accelerates federated hyperparameter tuning by connecting it to the neural architecture search technique of weight-sharing. FedEx is applicable to widely-used federated optimization methods like FedAvg, FedProx, and SCAFFOLD. Theoretically, a FedEx variant is shown to correctly tune on-device learning rates in online convex optimization. Empirically, FedEx outperforms natural baselines by several percentage points on Shakespeare, FEMNIST, and CIFAR-10 benchmarks, achieving higher accuracy with the same training budget.",
    "Methodology": "The problem of hyperparameter optimization in Federated Learning (FL) is formalized, distinguishing between global and personalized objectives. FL algorithms are decomposed into local training (Locc) and aggregation (Aggb) subroutines. Baselines adapt Random Search (RS) and Successive Halving (SHA) for FL, making elimination decisions based on noisy validation signals from a single communication round. FedEx addresses these challenges by applying a Neural Architecture Search (NAS)-like stochastic relaxation from weight-sharing to tune local FL hyperparameters. It replaces the personalized objective with a single-level function of shared weights (w) and local hyperparameters (c), then solves a relaxed objective min Ec~Dθ L(w,c). A categorical distribution Dθ over k random samples of local hyperparameters is used, and θ is updated via exponentiated gradient, alternating with standard SGD updates for w. FedEx is wrapped by RS or SHA to tune server hyperparameters and FedEx's own parameters. A local perturbation scheme is used to select initial configurations c1,...,ck for FedEx. Theoretical analysis employs the ARUBA framework, using bandit exponentiated gradient to tune the client step-size in an online convex optimization setting.",
    "Experimental Setup": "FedEx is instantiated to tune hyperparameters of FedAvg, FedProx, and Reptile. The experiments involve tuning server-side hyperparameters (learning rate schedule, momentum) and client-side hyperparameters (learning rate, momentum, weight-decay, local epochs, batch-size, dropout, proximal regularization). Evaluations are performed on three standard FL benchmarks: Shakespeare (next-character prediction), FEMNIST (image classification), and CIFAR-10 (image classification). Two data partitioning schemes are used: i.i.d. (randomly shuffled local data) and non-i.i.d. (actor-based temporal split for Shakespeare, digit writer-based random split for FEMNIST; CIFAR-10 is only i.i.d.). Backbone models follow FedAvg evaluation for Shakespeare/CIFAR-10 and LEAF for FEMNIST, with communication round budgets of 4K for Shakespeare/CIFAR-10 and 2K for FEMNIST. Performance is measured by final test error and online evaluation (test error vs. communication rounds). The wrapper algorithms (RS and SHA) use an elimination rate η=3 and R=3 elimination rounds. FedEx uses a local perturbation factor ε=0.1 and an 'aggressive' step-size schedule for its exponentiated gradient updates.",
    "Limitations": "FedEx is primarily applicable to FL algorithms that can be decomposed into local fine-tuning and aggregation routines; those that cannot require standard baselines. FedEx itself relies on wrapper algorithms (like SHA or RS) to tune its own internal hyperparameters (e.g., baseline computation) and certain FL server hyperparameters (e.g., server learning rate). The local perturbation scheme, while improving stability, inherently limits the search space explored by each instance of FedEx. The theoretical guarantees for tuning the step-size are derived for a simplified variant of FedEx in an online convex optimization setting with one client per round, which may not fully capture the complexity of real-world FL, and the bound incurs an additional m^(1/3) factor compared to full-information settings. The paper also acknowledges that it does not address the time-dependency of federated evaluation.",
    "Future Research Directions": "The authors suggest that FedEx could be extended to tune actual architectural hyperparameters, effectively performing federated Neural Architecture Search (NAS). Another direction is to apply FedEx to tune initialization-based meta-learning algorithms such as Reptile and MAML. The paper also broadly mentions that any work on FL involves privacy and fairness risks, implying future research should consider integrating tools developed by the community for mitigating such issues in the application of this work.",
    "Experiment Code": "def discounted_mean(trace, factor=1.0):\n\n    weight = factor ** np.flip(np.arange(len(trace)), axis=0)\n\n    return np.inner(trace, weight) / weight.sum()\n\n\nclass FedEx:\n    '''runs hyperparameter optimization given a federated learning server'''\n\n    def entropy(self):\n\n        entropy = 0.0\n        for probs in product(*(theta[theta>0.0] for theta in self._theta)):\n            prob = np.prod(probs)\n            entropy -= prob * np.log(prob)\n        return entropy\n\n    def mle(self):\n    \n        return np.prod([theta.max() for theta in self._theta])\n\n    def __init__(\n                 self, \n                 server, \n                 configs, \n                 eta0='auto', \n                 sched='auto', \n                 cutoff=0.0, \n                 baseline=0.0, \n                 diff=False,\n                 ):\n        '''\n        Args:\n            server: Object that implements two methods, 'communication_round' and 'full_evaluation'\n                    taking as input a single argument, 'get_config', itself a function that takes \n                    no inputs and outputs an element of the provided list 'configs'. \n                    - 'communication_round' samples a batch of clients, assigns a config to each \n                    using 'get_config', and runs local training using that config. It then \n                    aggregates the local models to to take a training step and returns three lists \n                    or arrays: a list of each client's validation error before local training, a \n                    list of each client's validation error after local training, and a list of each \n                    client's weight (e.g. size of its validation set). \n                    - 'full_evaluation' assigns a config to each client using 'get_config' and runs\n                    local training using that config. It then returns three lists or arrays: a list\n                    of each client's test error before local training, a list of each client's test\n                    error after local training, and a list of each client's weight (e.g. size of \n                    its test set).\n            configs: list of configs used for local training and testing by 'server' \n                     OR dict of (string, list) pairs denoting a grid of configs\n            eta0: base exponentiated gradient step size; if 'auto' uses sqrt(2*log(len(configs)))\n            sched: learning rate schedule for exponentiated gradient:\n                    - 'adaptive': uses eta0 / sqrt(sum of squared gradient l-infinity norms)\n                    - 'aggressive': uses eta0 / gradient l-infinity norm\n                    - 'auto': uses eta0 / sqrt(t) for t the number of rounds\n                    - 'constant': uses eta0\n                    - 'scale': uses sched * sqrt(2 * log(len(configs)))\n            cutoff: entropy level below which to stop updating the config probability and use MLE\n            baseline: discount factor when computing baseline; 0.0 is most recent, 1.0 is mean\n            diff: if True uses performance difference; otherwise uses absolute performance\n        '''\n\n        self._server = server\n        self._configs = configs\n        self._grid = [] if type(configs) == list else sorted(configs.keys())\n\n        sizes = [len(configs[param]) for param in self._grid] if self._grid else [len(configs)]\n        self._eta0 = [np.sqrt(2.0 * np.log(size)) if eta0 == 'auto' else eta0 for size in sizes]\n        self._sched = sched\n        self._cutoff = cutoff\n        self._baseline = baseline\n        self._diff = diff\n        self._z = [np.full(size, -np.log(size)) for size in sizes]\n        self._theta = [np.exp(z) for z in self._z]\n\n        self._store = [0.0 for _ in sizes]\n        self._stopped = False\n        self._trace = {'global': [], 'refine': [], 'entropy': [self.entropy()], 'mle': [self.mle()]}\n\n    def stop(self):\n\n        self._stopped = True\n\n    def sample(self, mle=False, _index=[]):\n        '''samples from configs using current probability vector'''\n\n        if mle or self._stopped:\n            if self._grid:\n                return {param: self._configs[param][theta.argmax()] \n                        for theta, param in zip(self._theta, self._grid)}\n            return self._configs[self._theta[0].argmax()]\n        _index.append([np.random.choice(len(theta), p=theta) for theta in self._theta])\n\n        if self._grid:\n            return {param: self._configs[param][i] for i, param in zip(_index[-1], self._grid)}\n        return self._configs[_index[-1][0]]\n\n    def settings(self):\n        '''returns FedEx input settings'''\n\n        output = {'configs': deepcopy(self._configs)}\n        output['eta0'], output['sched'] = self._eta0, self._sched\n        output['cutoff'], output['baseline'] = self._cutoff, self._baseline \n        if self._trace['refine']:\n            output['theta'] = self.theta()\n        return output\n\n    def step(self):\n        '''takes exponentiated gradient step (calls 'communication_round' once)'''\n\n        index = []\n        before, after, weight = self._server.communication_round(lambda: self.sample(_index=index))        \n        before, after = np.array(before), np.array(after)\n        weight = np.array(weight, dtype=np.float64) / sum(weight)\n\n        if self._trace['refine']:\n            trace = self.trace('refine')\n            if self._diff:\n                trace -= self.trace('global')\n            baseline = discounted_mean(trace, self._baseline)\n        else:\n            baseline = 0.0\n        self._trace['global'].append(np.inner(before, weight))\n        self._trace['refine'].append(np.inner(after, weight))\n        if not index:\n            self._trace['entropy'].append(0.0)\n            self._trace['mle'].append(1.0)\n            return\n\n        for i, (z, theta) in enumerate(zip(self._z, self._theta)):\n            grad = np.zeros(len(z))\n            for idx, s, w in zip(index, after-before if self._diff else after, weight):\n                grad[idx[i]] += w * (s - baseline) / theta[idx[i]]\n            if self._sched == 'adaptive':\n                self._store[i] += norm(grad, float('inf')) ** 2\n                denom = np.sqrt(self._store[i])\n            elif self._sched == 'aggressive':\n                denom = 1.0 if np.all(grad == 0.0) else norm(grad, float('inf'))\n            elif self._sched == 'auto':\n                self._store[i] += 1.0\n                denom = np.sqrt(self._store[i])\n            elif self._sched == 'constant':\n                denom = 1.0\n            elif self._sched == 'scale':\n                denom = 1.0 / np.sqrt(2.0 * np.log(len(grad))) if len(grad) > 1 else float('inf')\n            else:\n                raise NotImplementedError\n            eta = self._eta0[i] / denom\n            z -= eta * grad\n            z -= logsumexp(z)\n            self._theta[i] = np.exp(z)\n\n        self._trace['entropy'].append(self.entropy())\n        self._trace['mle'].append(self.mle())\n        if self._trace['entropy'][-1] < self._cutoff:\n            self.stop()\n\n    def test(self, mle=False):\n        '''evaluates found config (calls 'full_evaluation' once)\n        Args:\n            mle: use MLE config instead of sampling\n        Returns:\n            output of 'full_evaluation'\n        '''\n\n        before, after, weight = self._server.full_evaluation(lambda: self.sample(mle=mle))\n        return {'global': np.inner(before, weight) / weight.sum(),\n                'refine': np.inner(after, weight) / weight.sum()}\n\n    def theta(self):\n        '''returns copy of config probability vector'''\n\n        return deepcopy(self._theta)\n\n    def trace(self, key):\n        '''returns trace of one of three tracked quantities\n        Args:\n            key: 'entropy', 'global', or 'refine'\n        Returns:\n            numpy vector with length equal to number of calls to 'step'\n        '''\n\n        return np.array(self._trace[key])\n\n\nclass Server:\n    '''object for federated training implementing methods required by FedEx'''\n\n    def _set_test_state(self):\n\n        state = (np.random.get_state(), torch.get_rng_state(), torch.cuda.get_rng_state())\n        if self._state is None:\n            self._state = state\n        else:\n            np.random.set_state(self._state[0])\n            torch.set_rng_state(self._state[1])\n            torch.cuda.set_rng_state(self._state[2])\n        return state\n\n    def _reset_state(self, state):\n\n        np.random.set_state(state[0])\n        torch.set_rng_state(state[1])\n        torch.cuda.set_rng_state(state[2])\n\n    def __init__(\n                 self, \n                 model, \n                 clients, \n                 train, \n                 test, \n                 lr=1.0, \n                 momentum=0.0, \n                 step=1, \n                 gamma=1.0, \n                 batch=10,\n                 state=None,\n                 ):\n        '''\n        Args:\n            model: PyTorch model\n            clients: list of clients, each a function that takes one or more strings 'train',\n                     'val', 'test' and returns, as one tuple, input and output tensors for each\n            train: method that takes as argument a PyTorch model, an input tensor, an output\n                   tensor, and optional kwargs and returns the same PyTorch model\n            test: method that takes as argument a PyTorch model, an input tensor, and an output\n                  tensor and returns the model's error\n            lr: server learning rate\n            momentum: server momentum\n            step: server learning rate decay interval\n            gamma: server learning rate decay factor\n            batch: number of clients to sample per communication round\n            state: np.random, torch, torch.cuda random state tuple; if None uses current states\n        '''\n\n        self._model = model\n        self._clients = clients\n        self._train = train\n        self._test = test\n        self._opt = optim.SGD(self._model.parameters(), lr=lr, momentum=momentum)\n        self._sched = optim.lr_scheduler.StepLR(self._opt, step, gamma=gamma)\n        self._batch = batch\n        self._state = state\n        self._reset_state(self._set_test_state())\n\n    def communication_round(self, get_config):\n        '''runs one step of local training and model aggregation\n        Args:\n            get_config: returns kwargs for 'train' as a dict\n        Returns:\n            np.array objects for global val error, local val error, and val size of each client\n        '''\n\n        self._model.cuda()\n        before, after, weight = [np.zeros(self._batch) for _ in range(3)]\n        total = 0.0\n\n        for i in range(self._batch):\n            Xtrain, Ytrain, Xval, Yval = random.choice(self._clients)('train', 'val')\n            before[i] = self._test(self._model, Xval, Yval)\n            model = self._train(deepcopy(self._model), Xtrain, Ytrain, **get_config())\n            after[i] = self._test(model, Xval, Yval)\n            weight[i] = len(Yval)\n            total += len(Ytrain)\n            if i:\n                for agg, param in zip(aggregate.parameters(), model.parameters()):\n                    agg.data += len(Ytrain) * param.data\n            else:\n                for param in model.parameters():\n                    param.data *= len(Ytrain)\n                aggregate = model\n\n        self._opt.zero_grad()\n        for agg, param in zip(aggregate.parameters(), self._model.parameters()):\n            param.grad = param.data - agg / total\n        self._opt.step()\n        self._opt.zero_grad()\n        self._sched.step()\n        self._model.cpu()\n        return before, after, weight\n\n    def full_evaluation(self, get_config):\n        '''evaluates personalization on each client\n        Args:\n            get_config: returns kwargs for 'train' as a dict\n        Returns:\n            np.array objects for global test error, local test error, and test size of each client\n        '''\n\n        state = self._set_test_state()\n        self._model.cuda()\n        before, after, weight = [np.zeros(len(self._clients)) for _ in range(3)]\n        for i, client in enumerate(self._clients):\n            Xtrain, Ytrain, Xtest, Ytest = client('train', 'test')\n            before[i] = self._test(self._model, Xtest, Ytest)\n            after[i] = self._test(self._train(deepcopy(self._model), \n                                              Xtrain, Ytrain, **get_config()), \n                                  Xtest, Ytest)\n            weight[i] = len(Ytest)\n            print('\\r\\tEvaluated client', frac(i+1, len(self._clients)),\n                  '    global error:', round(np.inner(before, weight) / weight.sum(), 4),\n                  '    refine error:', round(np.inner(after, weight) / weight.sum(), 4), \n                  end=32*' ')\n        self._model.cpu()\n        self._reset_state(state)\n        return before, after, weight\n\n\ndef random_search(max_resources=500, total_resources=2000):\n    '''returns a random search rate and schedule for use by 'successive_elimination'\n    Args:\n        max_resources: most resources (steps) assigned to single arm\n        total_resources: overall resource limit\n    Returns:\n        elimination rate as an int, elimination schedule as a list\n    '''\n\n    assert max_resources > 0, \"max_resources must be positive\"\n    return int(total_resources / max_resources), [max_resources]\n\n\ndef get_schedule(\n                 max_resources=500, \n                 total_resources=2000, \n                 elim_rate=3, \n                 num_elim=0, \n                 num_eval=1,\n                 ):\n    '''returns rate and schedule for use by 'successive_elimination'\n    Args:\n        max_resources: most resources (steps) assigned to single arm\n        total_resources: overall resource limit\n        elim_rate: multiplicative elimination rate\n        num_elim: number of elimination rounds; if 0 runs random search\n        num_eval: number of evaluation rounds\n    Returns:\n        elimination rate as an int, elimination schedule as a list, evaluation schedule as a list\n    '''\n\n    assert max_resources <= total_resources, \"max_resources cannot be greater than total_resources\"\n    assert elim_rate > 1, \"elim_rate must be greater than 1\"\n    assert num_eval <= total_resources, \"num_eval cannot be greater than total_resources\"\n\n    if num_elim:\n        diff = total_resources - max_resources\n        geos = (elim_rate**(num_elim+1) - 1) / (elim_rate-1)\n        u = int(diff / (geos-num_elim-1))\n        resources = 0\n        v = lambda i: 1 + ceil((diff+(num_elim-geos+elim_rate**i)*u) / (elim_rate**i-1))\n        for opt in product(*(range(u, v(i)) for i in reversed(range(1, num_elim+1)))):\n            used = max_resources + sum((elim_rate**i-1)*r \n                                       for i, r in zip(reversed(range(1, num_elim+1)), opt))\n            if resources <= used <= total_resources:\n                best, resources = opt, used\n        assert not 0 in best, \"invalid: use more resources or fewer eliminations, or increase rate\"\n        elim_sched = list(np.cumsum(best)) + [max_resources]\n    else:\n        elim_rate, elim_sched = random_search(max_resources=max_resources, \n                                              total_resources=total_resources)\n\n    eval_sched = [int(step) for step in np.linspace(0, total_resources, num_eval+1)[1:]]\n    return elim_rate, elim_sched, eval_sched\n\n\n\ndef successive_elimination(\n                           sampler, \n                           eval_traces, \n                           logdir=None, \n                           val_discount=0.0, \n                           elim_rate=3, \n                           elim_sched=[1], \n                           eval_sched=[-1], \n                           traces=[], \n                           last_round=None,\n                           eval_global=False,\n                           **kwargs,\n                           ):\n    '''runs successive elimination according to provided schedule\n    Args:\n        sampler: function of n returning an iterable of n objects with methods 'step' and 'trace'\n        eval_traces: list of strings of traces measuring performance; element 0 used for elimination\n        logdir: directory to store tensorboard logs; if None does not log anything\n        val_discount: discount factor when computing score for a trace; 0.0 is most recent, 1.0 is mean\n        elim_rate: multiplicative elimination rate\n        elim_sched: list of steps at which to run an elimination\n        eval_sched: list of steps at which to call 'test' method of the best config\n        traces: list of strings of traces to collect\n        last_round: str name of function that last config executes before final round\n        kwargs: passed to 'test' method of each config\n    Returns:\n        best config;\n        also dumps tensorboard logs and results.pkl to folder 'logdir', if specified\n    '''\n    assert len(elim_sched) > 0, \"'elim_sched' must be a list of positive length\"\n    assert type(elim_rate) == int, \"'elim_rate' must be an int\"\n    logger = False if logdir is None else SummaryWriter(logdir)\n    traces, eval_sched = deepcopy(traces), deepcopy(eval_sched)\n    for trace in eval_traces:\n        if not trace in traces:\n            traces.append(trace)\n    #each config is (index, config_settings())\n    configs = list(enumerate(sampler(elim_rate ** max(1, len(elim_sched)-1))))\n    output = {index: {'settings': config.settings()} for index, config in configs}\n    for trace in eval_traces:\n        output[trace+' val'] = []\n        output[trace+' test'] = []\n    output['eval step'] = []\n    changed = {index: True for index, _ in configs}\n\n    #evaluate fedex object with best score across all time\n    best_score = 100.0\n    best_config = None \n    best_config_idx = None\n\n    start, last_start, used = 0, 0, 0\n    for i, stop in enumerate(elim_sched):\n        if len(configs) == 1 and not last_round is None:\n            getattr(configs[0][1], last_round)()\n        scores = []\n        for j, (index, config) in enumerate(configs):\n            scores.append(float('inf'))\n            for k in range(start, stop):\n                changed[index] = True\n                print('\\r\\tRound', frac(i+1, len(elim_sched)), \n                      'config', frac(j+1, len(configs)), \n                      'step', frac(k+1, stop), end=4*' ')\n                config.step()\n                for trace in traces:\n                    output[index][trace] = config.trace(trace)\n                    print(trace+':', round(output[index][trace][-1], 4), end=4*' ')\n                    if logger:\n                        logger.add_scalars(trace, {str(index): output[index][trace][-1]}, k+1)\n                #use refine error, if eval_global use global error\n                if eval_global:\n                    scores[-1] = discounted_mean(output[index][eval_traces[1]][start:],val_discount)\n                else:\n                    scores[-1] = discounted_mean(output[index][eval_traces[0]][start:], val_discount)\n                used += 1\n                current_best, score = min(enumerate(scores), key=itemgetter(1))\n\n                #check if val score beats best score so far\n                if score < best_score: \n                    best_config_idx = configs[current_best][0]\n                    best_config = deepcopy(configs[current_best][1])\n                    best_score = score \n\n                print('best:', round(best_score, 4), end=8*' ')\n\n                for trace in eval_traces:\n                    if len(output[best_config_idx][trace][start:])==0:\n                        val = discounted_mean(output[best_config_idx][trace][last_start:], val_discount)\n                    else:\n                        val = discounted_mean(output[best_config_idx][trace][start:], val_discount)\n                    output[trace+' val'].append(val)\n                    if logger:\n                        logger.add_scalar(trace+' val', val, used)\n                if used in eval_sched:\n                    if changed[best_config_idx]:\n                        results = best_config.test(**kwargs)\n                        changed[best_config_idx] = False\n                    print('\\r\\tStep', used, 'test error', end='')\n                    for trace in reversed(eval_traces):\n                        output[trace+' test'].append(results[trace])\n                        if logger:\n                            logger.add_scalar(trace+' test', results[trace], used)\n                        print('    '+trace, round(results[trace], 4), end='')\n                    print(64*' ')\n                    output['eval step'].append(eval_sched.pop(eval_sched.index(used)))\n        if len(configs) == 1:\n            break\n\n        #select top n configs, index of each config is preserved\n        _, configs = zip(*nsmallest(int(len(configs) / elim_rate), \n                                    zip(scores, configs), \n                                    key=itemgetter(0)))\n        last_start = start \n        start = stop\n\n    #best, config = configs[0]\n    best, config = best_config_idx, best_config \n    output['best'], output[best]['settings'] = best, config.settings()\n    if eval_sched:\n        if changed[best]:\n            results = config.test(**kwargs)\n        print('\\r\\tStep', used, 'test error', end='')\n        for trace in reversed(eval_traces):\n            output[trace+' test'].append(results[trace])\n            if logger:\n                logger.add_scalar(trace+' test', results[trace], used)\n            print('    '+trace, round(results[trace], 4), end='')\n        print(64*' ')\n        output['eval step'].append(used)\n\n    if logger:\n        with open(os.path.join(logdir, 'results.pkl'), 'wb') as f:\n            pickle.dump(output, f)\n        try:\n            logger.flush()\n        except AttributeError:\n            pass\n    return config\n\n\ndef wrapped_fedex(\n                  get_server,\n                  get_client,\n                  num_configs=1,\n                  prod=False,\n                  stepsize_init='auto', \n                  stepsize_sched='aggressive', \n                  cutoff=1E-4, \n                  baseline_discount=-1.0, \n                  diff=False,\n                  mle=False, \n                  logdir=None,\n                  val_discount=0.0, \n                  last_stop=False,\n                  eval_global=False,\n                  **kwargs,\n                  ):\n    '''evaluates FedEx wrapped with successive elimination algorithm;\n       uses FedAvg when num_configs = 1 and prod = False\n    Args:\n        get_server: function that takes no input and returns an object that can be passed as the \n                    first argument to FedEx.__init__, e.g. a Server object\n        get_client: function that takes no input and returns a dict of local training configs, a\n                    list of which is passed as the second argument to 'FedEx.__init__'; can also\n                    return a dict of (string, list) pairs to be passed directly to 'FedEx.__init__'\n        num_configs: determines number of configs in the list passed to 'FedEx.__init__':\n                     - >0: use this value directly\n                     - =0: value drawn at random from Unif[1, number of arms given by the wrapper]\n                     - =-1: use the number of arms given by the wrapper\n                     - else: value drawn at random from Unif{1, ..., abs(num_configs)}\n        prod: run FedEx over a product set of single-parameter grids; must be 'True' in the case\n                  when 'get_client' returns an object to be passed directly to 'FedEx.__init__'\n        stepsize_init: passed to 'eta0' kwarg of 'FedEx.__init__'\n        stepsize_sched: passed to 'sched' kwarg of 'FedEx.__init__'\n        baseline_discount: determines 'baseline' kwarg of 'FedEx.__init__':\n                           - >0.0: use this value directly\n                           - else: value drawn at random from Unif[0.0, abs(baseline_discount)]\n        diff: passed to 'diff' kwarg of 'FedEx.__init__'\n        mle: passed to 'mle' kwarg of 'FedEx.test' via the kwargs of 'successive_elimination'\n        logdir: passed to 'logdir' kwarg of 'successive_elimination'\n        val_discount: passed to 'val_discount' kwarg of 'successive_elimination'\n        last_stop: if True sets 'last_round' kwarg of 'successive_elimination' to 'stop'\n        kwargs: passed to 'get_schedule'\n    Returns:\n        FedEx object\n    '''\n\n    elim_rate, elim_sched, eval_sched = get_schedule(**kwargs)\n    print('Wrapping with', 'random search' if len(elim_sched) == 1 else 'successive elimination')\n\n    if num_configs < -1:\n        samples = lambda n: random.randint(1, -num_configs)\n    elif num_configs == -1:\n        samples = lambda n: n\n    elif num_configs == 0:\n        samples = lambda n: random.randint(1, n)\n    else:\n        samples = lambda n: num_configs\n\n    if baseline_discount < 0.0:\n        baseline = lambda: random.uniform(0.0, -baseline_discount)\n    else:\n        baseline = lambda: baseline_discount\n\n    def sampler(n):\n\n        for _ in range(n):\n            yield FedEx(\n                        get_server(), \n                        get_client() if prod else get_client(samples(n)),\n                        eta0=stepsize_init, \n                        sched=stepsize_sched, \n                        cutoff=cutoff, \n                        baseline=baseline(),\n                        diff=diff,\n                        )\n\n    return successive_elimination(\n                                  sampler, \n                                  ['refine', 'global'], \n                                  logdir=logdir, \n                                  val_discount=val_discount,\n                                  elim_rate=elim_rate, \n                                  elim_sched=elim_sched, \n                                  eval_sched=eval_sched,\n                                  traces=['entropy', 'mle', 'global', 'refine'], \n                                  last_round='stop' if last_stop else None,\n                                  mle=mle,\n                                  eval_global=eval_global,\n                                  )\n\nSERVER = lambda: {\n                  'lr': 10.0 ** np.random.uniform(low=-1.0, high=1.0),\n                  'momentum': np.random.choice([0.0, 0.9]),\n                  'step': 1,\n                  'gamma': 1.0 - 10.0 ** np.random.uniform(low=-4.0, high=-2.0),\n                  }\nCLIENT = lambda: {\n                  'lr': 10.0 ** np.random.uniform(low=-4.0, high=0.0),\n                  'momentum': np.random.uniform(low=0.0,high=1.0),\n                  'weight_decay': 10.0 ** np.random.uniform(low=-5.0, high=-1.0),\n                  'epochs': np.random.choice(np.arange(1, 6)), \n                  'batch': 2 ** np.random.choice(np.arange(3, 8)),\n                  'mu': 10.0 ** np.random.uniform(low=-5.0, high=0.0),\n                  'dropout': np.random.uniform(low=0.0, high=0.5),\n                  }\n\ndef get_client(n_clients=1):\n        '''performs local tuning for each hyperparameter'''\n        if args.lr_only:\n            return [SIMPLE_CLIENT()]\n\n        initial_client = CLIENT()\n        client_arr = [initial_client]\n        eps = args.eps\n\n        for i in range(n_clients-1):\n            other_client = deepcopy(initial_client)\n            \n            log_lr = np.log10(other_client['lr'])\n            other_client['lr'] = 10 ** np.clip(log_lr + np.random.uniform(4*-eps, 4*eps), -4.0, 0.0)\n            \n            other_client['momentum'] = np.clip(initial_client['momentum'] + np.random.uniform(-eps, eps), 0, 1.0)\n            \n            log_wd = np.log10(other_client['weight_decay'])\n            other_client['weight_decay'] = 10 ** np.clip(log_wd + np.random.uniform(4*-eps, 4*eps),-5.0, -1.0)\n            \n            epochs_range = math.ceil(eps * 4)\n            other_client['epochs'] = np.clip(np.random.choice(np.arange(initial_client['epochs']-epochs_range, initial_client['epochs']+epochs_range+1)), 1, 5)\n\n            log_batch = int(np.log2(other_client['batch']))\n            batch_range = math.ceil(eps * 4)\n            other_client['batch'] = 2 ** np.clip(np.random.choice(np.arange(log_batch-batch_range, log_batch+batch_range+1)), 3, 7)\n\n            \n            log_mu = np.log10(other_client['mu'])\n            other_client['mu'] = 10 ** np.clip(log_mu + np.random.uniform(5*-eps, 5*eps), -5.0 , 0.0)\n            \n            other_client['dropout'] = np.clip(initial_client['dropout'] + np.random.uniform(0.5*-eps, 0.5*eps),0, 0.5)\n\n            client_arr.append(other_client)\n\n        return [UNIFORM()] if args.uniform else [RANDOM()] if args.random else client_arr",
    "Experiment Result": "Wrapper algorithm settings:\n- `rounds` (max_resources): Default 800 (Cifar/Shakespeare), 50 (Femnist)\n- `total` (total_resources): Default 4000 (Cifar/Shakespeare), 450 (Femnist)\n- `rate` (elimination rate): Default 3\n- `elim` (number of elimination rounds): Default 0 (random search)\n- `eval` (number of evaluation rounds): Default 1\n- `discount` (discount factor for validation score): Default 0.0\n\nFedEx settings:\n- `batch` (number of tasks per round): Default 10\n- `configs` (number of configurations for FedEx): Default 1 (FedAvg), can be set to sample a random number or match wrapper's arms\n- `lr_only`: Flag to tune only learning rate as a hyperparameter\n- `eps`: Multiplicative perturbation to client config, default 0.0 (FedAvg) or 0.1 (Shakespeare)\n- `uniform`: Flag to run FedEx over a product set of single-parameter uniform grids\n- `random`: Flag to run FedEx over a product set of single-parameter random grids\n- `eta0` (FedEx initial step size): Default 0.0 (uses FedEx default 'auto')\n- `sched` (FedEx step size schedule): Default 'aggressive'\n- `cutoff` (entropy cutoff to stop updating config distribution): Default 0.0\n- `baseline` (discount factor for computing baseline): Default -1.0 (samples from [0.0, 1.0))\n- `diff`: Flag to use difference between refine and global as FedEx objective\n- `stop`: Flag to stop updating FedEx config distribution after last elimination\n\nEvaluation settings:\n- `mle`: Flag to use MLE config at test time\n- `loss`: Flag to use loss instead of error as evaluation metric\n- `eval_global`: Flag to use global error as elimination metric instead of refine\n\nData settings (Cifar10 example):\n- `val`: Proportion of training set for validation, default 0.2\n- `num-clients`: Number of clients, default 500\n\nServer hyperparameters search space:\n- `lr`: Uniformly sampled in `[10^-1, 10^1]` (log scale)\n- `momentum`: Randomly chosen from `{0.0, 0.9}`\n- `step`: Fixed at 1\n- `gamma`: Uniformly sampled `1 - 10^[-4, 10^-2]` (log scale)\n\nClient hyperparameters search space (for FedEx):\n- `lr`: Uniformly sampled in `[10^-4, 10^0]` (log scale)\n- `momentum`: Uniformly sampled in `[0.0, 1.0]`\n- `weight_decay`: Uniformly sampled in `[10^-5, 10^-1]` (log scale)\n- `epochs`: Randomly chosen integer from `[1, 5]`\n- `batch`: Randomly chosen from powers of 2 in `[2^3, 2^7]`\n- `mu` (proximal term): Uniformly sampled in `[10^-5, 10^0]` (log scale)\n- `dropout`: Uniformly sampled in `[0.0, 0.5]`\n\nInitial client configuration perturbation scheme (illustrated by `get_client`):\n- For `n_clients-1` additional clients, hyperparameters are perturbed multiplicatively or additively around an `initial_client`'s values based on an `eps` parameter.\n- Log-transformed hyperparameters (`lr`, `weight_decay`, `mu`) are perturbed by `np.random.uniform(C*-eps, C*eps)` where `C` is a scaling factor (e.g., 4 or 5).\n- Linearly scaled hyperparameters (`momentum`, `dropout`) are perturbed by `np.random.uniform(-eps, eps)` or `np.random.uniform(-eps*0.5, eps*0.5)`.\n- Discrete hyperparameters (`epochs`, `batch`) are perturbed by adding/subtracting integer ranges (`math.ceil(eps * 4)`) or discrete choices around the initial value, with clipping to valid ranges."
}{
    "Title": "PASHA: Efficient HPO and NAS with Progressive Resource Allocation",
    "Main Contributions": "The paper introduces PASHA, a novel approach designed to address the high computational cost of Hyperparameter Optimization (HPO) and Neural Architecture Search (NAS), especially when dealing with large datasets and models. PASHA extends the ASHA algorithm by dynamically allocating the maximum amount of resources needed for the tuning procedure, unlike ASHA's fixed resource budget. This dynamic allocation allows PASHA to significantly reduce computational resources while identifying well-performing configurations and architectures with similar predictive performance to conventional ASHA after full retraining. The approach also demonstrates successful integration with sample-efficient strategies like Bayesian Optimization, highlighting its generality.",
    "Methodology": "PASHA is an extension of ASHA, drawing inspiration from the 'doubling trick'. It operates by starting with a small initial amount of resources and progressively increasing them only if the ranking of configurations in the top two rungs (rounds of promotion) has not stabilized. The core of PASHA's early stopping mechanism relies on a 'soft ranking' system, where configurations are considered equivalent if their performance difference is below a dynamically estimated threshold, ϵ. This ϵ value is automatically estimated by observing noise in rankings, specifically by identifying configurations that repeatedly swap their relative ranks across different resource levels (epochs), indicative of similar underlying performance. When the soft ranking stabilizes, PASHA stops allocating further resources, thereby saving computation.",
    "Experimental Setup": "The method's performance was evaluated in two phases: first, running the hyperparameter optimizer until 256 candidate configurations were evaluated; second, retraining the best identified configuration from scratch using only the training set for most benchmarks. Experiments included NAS problems using the NASBench201 benchmark (on CIFAR-10, CIFAR-100, and ImageNet16-120 datasets) and HPO problems on two large-scale tasks from the PD1 benchmark (WMT15 German-English with xformer model and ImageNet with ResNet50 model). An additional evaluation on the LCBench benchmark was performed to analyze performance with limited rungs. Baselines for comparison included ASHA, a 'one-epoch baseline' (training all configurations for one epoch), and a 'random baseline'. PASHA was also tested in combination with Bayesian Optimization using MOBSTER. Key parameters were minimum resource (r=1 epoch typically), reduction factor (η=3 by default), and maximum resources (R, dataset-dependent, e.g., 200 epochs for NASBench201). All results were averaged over multiple random seeds.",
    "Limitations": "PASHA's benefits in terms of speedup are reduced when the number of available rungs is small, as this limits opportunities for the algorithm to interrupt tuning early. This was demonstrated on benchmarks like LCBench, which often have a small maximum number of epochs (e.g., 50). Public benchmarks frequently fix the minimum resources to one epoch, which can constrain the granularity of resource allocation and thus PASHA's ability to achieve large speedups. The authors recommend having a maximum amount of resources at least 100 times larger than the minimum when using a reduction factor of η=3 to maximize PASHA's effectiveness, suggesting that using more granular resource units (e.g., gradient updates instead of epochs) could mitigate this limitation in practical scenarios.",
    "Future Research Directions": "Future research could investigate the definition of rungs and resource levels more broadly to understand their impact on multi-fidelity algorithms, including PASHA, and how to optimize these for better decision-making. Another promising direction is to explore combinations of PASHA with transfer-learning techniques specifically designed for multi-fidelity HPO and NAS problems, such as RUSH, to achieve further reductions in tuning time.",
    "Experiment Code": "class PASHARungSystem(PromotionRungSystem):\"\"\"Implements PASHA algorithm. It is very similar to ASHA, but it progressivelyextends the maximum resources if the ranking in the top two current rungs changes.A report introducing and evaluating the approach is available atTODO: add link\"\"\"def __init__(self,rung_levels,promote_quantiles,metric,mode,resource_attr,max_t,ranking_criterion,epsilon,epsilon_scaling,):super().__init__(rung_levels, promote_quantiles, metric, mode, resource_attr, max_t)self.ranking_criterion = ranking_criterion# define the index of the current top rung, starting from 1 for the lowest rung#self.current_rung_idx = 2self.rung_levels = rung_levels# initialize current maximum resourcesself.current_max_t = rung_levels[self.current_rung_idx - 1]self.epsilon = epsilonself.epsilon_scaling = epsilon_scalingif ranking_criterion == 'soft_ranking_auto':self.per_epoch_results = {}self.epoch_to_trials = {}self.current_max_epoch = -1# overriding the method in HB promotion to accomodate the increasing max resources leveldef _effective_max_t(self):return self.current_max_tdef _get_top_rungs_rankings(self, num_rungs=2):\"\"\"Look at the current top two rungs and get the rankings of the configurations.The rungs can be empty, in which case we will return a list with 0 or 1 elements.Normally the list will include rankings for both rungs.The rankings are stored as a list of tuples (trial_id, rank, value).Lower values have lower ranks, starting from zero. For example:[('0', 0, 10.0), ('1', 3, 19.6), ('2', 2, 14.3), ('3', 1, 11.6)]:param num_rungs: int describing how many top rungs to return:return: rankingsList of at most two lists with tuple(trial_id, rank, score)\"\"\"rankings = []# be careful, self._rungs is ordered with the highest resources level in the beginningrungs = [self._rungs[-self.current_rung_idx + e] for e in range(num_rungs)]for rung bool:\"\"\"Soft ranking creates groups of similarly performing configurationsand increases the resources only if a configuration goes outside ofits group.:param sorted_top_rung: list of tuple(trial_id, rank, score):param sorted_previous_rung: list of tuple(sorted_top_rung, rank, score):return: keep_current_budget\"\"\"keep_current_budget = Trueif len(sorted_previous_rung) < 2:epsilon idx2]if self.ranking_criterion == \"soft_ranking_mean_dst\":epsilon = np.mean(distances) * self.epsilon_scalingelif self.ranking_criterion == \"soft_ranking_median_dst\":epsilon = np.median(distances) epsilon:breakcurrent_rung_group.append(new_item[0])# add configurations that are before the current configurationfor idx_before in range(idx - 1, -1, -1):new_item = sorted_previous_rung[idx_before]if _update_epsilon(self):\"\"\"This function is used to automatically calculate the value of epsilon.It finds the configurations which swapped their rankings across rungsand estimates the value of epsilon as the 90th percentile of the differencebetween their performance in the previous rung.The original value of epsilon is kept if no suitable configurations were found.\"\"\"seen_pairs = set()noisy_cfg_distances = []top_epoch = min(self.current_max_epoch, self._rungs[-self.current_rung_idx].level)bottom_epoch = min(self._rungs[-self.current_rung_idx+1].level, self.current_max_epoch)for epoch in range(top_epoch, bottom_epoch, -1):if len(self.epoch_to_trials[epoch]) > 1:for pair in itertools.combinations(self.epoch_to_trials[epoch], 2):c1, c2 = pair[0], = False# now we need to check the earlier epochs to see if at any point they had a different orderfor prev_epoch in range(epoch - 1, 0, -1):pp1, pp2 = self.per_epoch_results[c1][prev_epoch], self.per_epoch_results[c2][prev_epoch]p_cond = pp1 > pp2if p_cond == (not cond):opposite_order = Trueif opposite_order and p_cond == cond:same_order_after_opposite = Truebreakif opposite_order and same_order_after_opposite:noisy_cfg_distances.append(abs(p1 - p2))if len(noisy_cfg_distances) > 0:self.epsilon = np.percentile(noisy_cfg_distances, 90)if str(self.epsilon) == 'nan':raise ValueError('Epsilon became nan')def _update_per_epoch_results(self, trial_id, result):if trial_id not in self.per_epoch_results:self.per_epoch_results[trial_id] = order also in the second list, we keep the current resourcebudget. If the rankings are different, we will increase the budget.The rankings can only be incorrect if we have rankings for both rungs.:param rankings: list of at most two lists with tuple(trial_id, rank, score):return: not keep_current_budget\"\"\"if len(rankings) == 2:sorted_top_rung, sorted_previous_rung = self._get_sorted_top_rungs(rankings)else:return Falsekeep_current_budget = self._evaluate_soft_ranking(sorted_top_rung, sorted_previous_rung)return not keep_current_budgetdef on_task_report(self, trial_id: str, result: dict, skip_rungs: int) -> dict:\"\"\"Apart from calling the superclass method, we also check the rankingsand decides if to increase the current maximum resources.\"\"\"ret_dict = super().on_task_report(trial_id, result, skip_rungs)if self.ranking_criterion == \"soft_ranking_auto\":self._update_per_epoch_results(trial_id, result)self._update_epsilon()# check the rankings and decide if to increase the current maximum resourcesrankings = self._get_top_rungs_rankings(num_rungs=2)increase_resources = self._decide_resource_increase(rankings)# we have a maximum amount of resources that PASHA can use# the resources should not increase indefinitelyif increase_resources:if self.current_rung_idx < len(self._rungs):self.current_rung_idx += 1# be careful, self.rung_levels is ordered with the highest resources level at the end# moreover, since we use rung levels for counting both from the beginning and from the end of the list# we need to remember that counting from the beginning it's zero indexedself.current_max_t = self.rung_levels[self.current_rung_idx - 1]else:self.current_max_t = self.max_treturn ret_dict",
    "Experiment Result": "PASHA extends ASHA by dynamically adjusting resource allocation based on ranking stability. The core mechanism involves a 'soft ranking' system and a dynamically estimated threshold, ϵ. Experimental settings related to PASHA are:\n- `ranking_criterion`: Determines the strategy for soft ranking. Options include: 'soft_ranking' (uses a fixed `epsilon`), 'soft_ranking_auto' (automatically estimates ϵ by observing rank swaps across resource levels), 'soft_ranking_std' (estimates ϵ based on standard deviation), 'soft_ranking_median_dst' (estimates ϵ based on median distance), 'soft_ranking_mean_dst' (estimates ϵ based on mean distance).\n- `epsilon`: The explicit performance difference threshold used in 'soft_ranking' mode.\n- `epsilon_scaling`: A scaling factor applied to the automatically estimated ϵ in auto-ranking modes.\n- `rung_levels`: The predefined discrete resource levels (e.g., epochs) at which performance is evaluated and promotion/stopping decisions are considered. Passed via HyperbandScheduler's `rung_levels` or derived from `grace_period`, `reduction_factor`, and `max_t`.\n- `metric`: The objective metric to optimize (e.g., 'metric_valid_loss', 'accuracy').\n- `mode`: Optimization direction for the metric, 'min' for minimization or 'max' for maximization.\n- `resource_attr`: The name of the attribute in reported results that signifies the current resource level (e.g., 'epoch').\n- `max_t`: The maximum possible resource level a trial can run to.\n\nKey aspects of PASHA's operation:\n- Resource levels are dynamically increased (`current_max_t`) only if the soft ranking of configurations in the top two rungs has not stabilized.\n- For `ranking_criterion='soft_ranking_auto'`, `epsilon` is estimated by tracking configurations that repeatedly swap relative ranks at different resource levels, indicating similar underlying performance.\n- When a configuration's soft ranking stabilizes (i.e., configurations remain within their 'soft' performance groups across rungs), PASHA ceases to increase resources for subsequent trials, leading to early stopping and computational savings."
}{
    "Title": "Hyperparameter Optimization through Neural Network Partitioning",
    "Main Contributions": "The paper proposes Partitioned Neural Networks, a simple and efficient method for hyperparameter optimization inspired by marginal likelihood, requiring no validation data and only a single training run. It addresses the challenges of traditional hyperparameter tuning, especially in low-data regimes and federated learning, by partitioning training data and neural network parameters into 'shards' and 'partitions' respectively. The method optimizes hyperparameters based on an 'out-of-training-sample' loss, demonstrating superior computational efficiency compared to existing marginal likelihood-based methods and achieving strong generalization performance across various tasks.",
    "Methodology": "The method optimizes a lower-bound approximation to the marginal likelihood, LML. It partitions the training dataset into C chunks (D1, ..., DC) and the neural network parameters into C partitions (w1, ..., wC). Subnetworks w(k)s are defined such that w(k)s = concat(w1, ..., wk, ŵk+1, ..., ŵC), where ŵi are default values (e.g., initialization values). Each partition wk is optimized on data chunks D1:k using its corresponding subnetwork w(k)s. Hyperparameters ψ are optimized using the 'out-of-training-sample' loss, which is the sum of log p(Dk|w(k-1)s,ψ) for k=2 to C. This involves interleaving stochastic gradient updates for parameters and hyperparameters, where hyperparameter gradients are computed on data shards unseen by the respective subnetwork used for training its parameters.",
    "Experimental Setup": "The method was evaluated on: 1) A toy input selection task with 15 informative and 15 spurious features, using MLPs. 2) Learning affine image augmentations on MNIST, CIFAR10, TinyImagenet, and their rotated variants (rotMNIST, rotCIFAR10, rotTinyImagenet), using CNNs, fixupResNet-8, fixupResNet-14, and ResNet-50. 3) Learning a feature extractor (first two stages of a Wide ResNet-20) on CIFAR10. 4) Federated Learning (FL) settings on non-i.i.d. splits of MNIST and CIFAR10 (including rotated versions), optimizing data augmentation parameters and dropout rates with a CNN for MNIST and a GroupNorm ResNet-9 for CIFAR10. Baselines included standard training (no augmentations/FedAvg), Augerino, Differentiable Laplace, and validation set optimization. Performance was measured by test accuracy, log-likelihood, and validation accuracy in FL, often with various dataset sizes and communication cost analysis.",
    "Limitations": "The method inherently requires an additional forward-backward pass to update hyperparameters, leading to increased computational costs, although significantly less than existing work. It typically requires more training iterations to converge. Partitioning the network constrains its capacity, potentially causing some loss of performance compared to a full, non-partitioned network trained with optimal hyperparameters. The partitioning strategy (number of chunks, data proportions, parameter proportions) introduces an additional hyperparameter that may need tuning for optimal performance.",
    "Future Research Directions": "Future work could explore dynamic partitioning of network parameters during training. Investigating alternative partitioning schemes could potentially reduce the computational overhead associated with separate forward-backward passes. Research into methods for alleviating the performance loss caused by network partitioning, such as adjusting training rounds or increasing initial network capacity, is also suggested. Additionally, exploring gradient accumulation schemes for hyperparameter updates is a potential area for improvement.",
    "Experiment Code": null,
    "Experiment Result": null

    "Title": "Provably Efficient Online Hyperparameter Optimization with Population-Based Bandits",
    "Main Contributions": "The paper introduces Population-Based Bandits (PB2), the first provably efficient PBT-style algorithm, addressing the lack of theoretical guarantees and computational inefficiency of existing Population-Based Training (PBT) which relies on random heuristics. PB2 uses a probabilistic model (Gaussian Process bandits) to guide hyperparameter search, enabling the discovery of high-performing configurations with significantly fewer agents. It provides the first sublinear regret bound for a PBT-style algorithm and demonstrates superior performance and computational efficiency in reinforcement learning tasks compared to PBT and other baselines.",
    "Methodology": "PB2 formulates online hyperparameter selection as batch Gaussian Process (GP) bandit optimization of a time-varying function. It models the reward function using a time-varying GP with a product form kernel (kSE ◦ ktime), where a parameter ω models how the function varies over time. Hyperparameters for parallel agents are selected by sequentially maximizing an acquisition function, which balances exploration and exploitation by updating uncertainty based on currently training agents. The algorithm periodically ranks agents, copies weights from best performers to underperformers, and then fits a GP model to accumulated data to select new hyperparameters.",
    "Experimental Setup": "Experiments focus primarily on Reinforcement Learning (RL) settings. For on-policy RL, it optimizes Proximal Policy Optimization (PPO) hyperparameters on OpenAI Gym continuous control tasks (BipedalWalker, LunarLanderContinuous, Hopper, InvertedDoublePendulum) with population sizes B={4, 8}. For off-policy RL, it optimizes IMPALA hyperparameters in Breakout and SpaceInvaders from the Arcade Learning Environment with B=4. An additional experiment evaluates PB2 for Supervised Learning, optimizing CNN hyperparameters on CIFAR-10 with B=4. Baselines include PBT, Random Search (RS), Bayesian Optimization (BO), and ASHA. Experiments are conducted for 10^6 environment timesteps (PPO), 10 million timesteps (IMPALA), and 50 epochs (CIFAR-10), with results reported as median best performance across 10 (PPO), 7 (IMPALA), or 5 (CIFAR-10) random seeds.",
    "Limitations": "The primary limitations addressed by PB2 are those of PBT: its reliance on random heuristics, the need for vast computational resources, its lack of theoretical guarantees, and the tendency for small populations to collapse to suboptimal modes. For PB2 itself, the theoretical regret bound relies on the condition that the batch size (B) is much smaller than the total timesteps (T). Additionally, the time-varying GP model introduces a new hyperparameter (ω) that needs to be optimized, adding a layer of complexity. While PB2 shows good scaling, scaling to even larger populations is suggested as a future research area, implying potential limitations in current large-scale validation.",
    "Future Research Directions": "Future research directions include updating population sizes dynamically based on the acquisition function's value, extending the search space to encompass the selection of optimization algorithms or neural network architectures using Bayesian Optimization, and further leveraging off-policy data in reinforcement learning experiments to potentially achieve additional performance gains.",
    "Experiment Code": "from GPy.kern import Kern\nfrom GPy.core import Param\nfrom sklearn.metrics import pairwise_distances\nfrom sklearn.metrics.pairwise import euclidean_distances\n\nimport numpy as np\n\nclass TV_SquaredExp(Kern):\n    def __init__(self,input_dim, variance=1.,lengthscale=1.,epsilon=0.,active_dims=None):\n        super().__init__(input_dim, active_dims, 'time_se')\n        self.variance = Param('variance', variance)\n        self.lengthscale = Param('lengthscale', lengthscale)\n        self.epsilon = Param('epsilon', epsilon)\n        self.link_parameters(self.variance, self.lengthscale, self.epsilon)\n        \n    def K(self,X,X2):\n        # time must be in the far left column\n        if self.epsilon > 0.5: # 0.5\n            self.epsilon = 0.5\n        if X2 is None: X2 = np.copy(X)\n        T1 = X[:, 0].reshape(-1, 1)\n        T2 = X2[:, 0].reshape(-1, 1)\n        dists = pairwise_distances(T1,T2, 'cityblock')\n        timekernel=(1-self.epsilon)**(0.5*dists)\n        \n        X = X[:, 1:]\n        X2 = X2[:, 1:]\n\n        RBF = self.variance*np.exp(-np.square(euclidean_distances(X,X2))/self.lengthscale)\n        \n        return RBF * timekernel\n    \n    def Kdiag(self,X):\n        return self.variance*np.ones(X.shape[0])\n    \n    def update_gradients_full(self, dL_dK, X, X2):\n        if X2 is None: X2 = self.variance * np.exp(-dist2)) * self.lengthscale**(-2)\n        n = pairwise_distances(T1,T2, 'cityblock')/2\n        deps = -n * (1-self.epsilon)**(n-1)\n    \n        self.variance.gradient = print(\"\\n**\\n\\n vs. Mean Dist: {} \\n\\n**\\n\".format(str(meandist)))\n    \n    xt = xt.astype(np.float32)\n    return(xt, lengthscale, min_new, meandist)\n\ndef explore(data, bounds, current, mutations):\n    \"\"\"Appends perturbed params to the trial name to show in the console.\"\"\"\n\n    resolved_vars = {}\n    for k in mutations.keys():\n        resolved_vars[(\"config\", k)] = config[k]\n    return \"{}@perturbed[{}]\".format(orig_tag, format_vars(resolved_vars))\n\n\nclass PB2(FIFOScheduler):\n    \"\"\"\n    Below text is from PBT... which this was adapted from.\n\n    Implements the Population Based Training (PBT) algorithm.\n\n    https://deepmind.com/blog/population-based-training-neural-networks\n\n    PBT trains a group of models (or agents) in parallel. Periodically, poorly\n    performing models clone the state of the top performers, and a random\n    mutation is applied to their hyperparameters in the hopes of\n    outperforming the current top models.\n\n    Unlike other hyperparameter search algorithms, PBT mutates hyperparameters\n    during training time. This enables very fast hyperparameter discovery and\n    also automatically discovers good annealing schedules.\n\n    This Tune PBT implementation considers all trials added as part of the\n    PBT population. If the number of trials exceeds the cluster capacity,\n    they will be time-multiplexed as to balance training progress across the\n    population. To run multiple trials, use `tune.run(num_samples=<int>)`.\n\n    In {LOG_DIR}/{MY_EXPERIMENT_NAME}/, all mutations are logged in\n    `pbt_global.txt` and individual policy perturbations are recorded\n    in pbt_policy_{i}.txt. Tune logs: [target trial tag, clone trial tag,\n    target trial iteration, clone trial iteration, old config, new config]\n    on each perturbation step.\n\n    Args:\n        time_attr (str): The training result attr to use for comparing time.\n            Note that you can pass in something non-temporal such as\n            `training_iteration` as a measure of progress, the only requirement\n            is that the attribute should increase monotonically.\n        metric (str): The training result objective value attribute. Stopping\n            procedures will use this attribute.\n        mode (str): One of {min, max}. Determines whether objective is\n            minimizing or maximizing the metric attribute.\n        perturbation_interval (float): Models will be considered for\n            perturbation at this interval of `time_attr`. Note that\n            perturbation incurs checkpoint overhead, so you shouldn't set this\n            to be too frequent.\n        hyperparam_mutations (dict): Hyperparams to mutate. The format is\n            as follows: for each key, either a list or function can be\n            provided. A list specifies an allowed set of categorical values.\n            A function specifies the distribution of a continuous parameter.\n            You must specify at least one of `hyperparam_mutations` or\n            `custom_explore_fn`.\n        quantile_fraction (float): Parameters are transferred from the top\n            `quantile_fraction` fraction of trials to the bottom\n            `quantile_fraction` fraction. Needs to be between 0 and 0.5.\n            Setting it to 0 essentially implies doing no exploitation at all.\n        resample_probability (float): The probability of resampling from the\n            original distribution when applying `hyperparam_mutations`. If not\n            resampled, the value will be perturbed by a factor of 1.2 or 0.8\n            if continuous, or changed to an adjacent value if discrete.\n        custom_explore_fn (func): You can also specify a custom exploration\n            function. This function is invoked as `f(config)` after built-in\n            perturbations from `hyperparam_mutations` are applied, and should\n            return `config` updated as needed. You must specify at least one of\n            `hyperparam_mutations` or `custom_explore_fn`.\n        log_config (bool): Whether to log the ray config of each model to\n            local_dir at each exploit. Allows config schedule to be\n            reconstructed.\n\n    Example:\n        >>> pbt = PopulationBasedTraining(\n        >>>     time_attr=\"training_iteration\",\n        >>>     metric=\"episode_reward_mean\",\n        >>>     mode=\"max\",\n        >>>     perturbation_interval=10,  # every 10 `time_attr` units\n        >>>                                # (training_iterations in this case)\n        >>>     hyperparam_mutations={\n        >>>         # Perturb factor1 by scaling it by 0.8 or 1.2. Resampling\n        >>>         # resets it to a value sampled from the lambda function.\n        >>>         \"factor_1\": lambda: random.uniform(0.0, 20.0),\n        >>>         # Perturb factor2 by changing it to an adjacent value, e.g.\n        >>>         # 10 -> 1 or 10 -> 100. Resampling will choose at random.\n        >>>         \"factor_2\": [1, 10, 100, 1000, 10000],\n        >>>     })\n        >>> tune.run({...}, num_samples=8, scheduler=pbt)\n    \"\"\"\n\n    def __init__(self, \n                 time_attr=\"time_total_s\",\n                 reward_attr=None,\n                 metric=\"episode_reward_mean\",\n                 mode=\"max\",\n                 perturbation_interval=60.0,\n                 hyperparam_mutations={},\n                 quantile_fraction=0.25,\n                 resample_probability=0.25,\n                 custom_explore_fn=None,\n                 log_config=True):\n        if not hyperparam_mutations and not custom_explore_fn:\n            raise TuneError(\n                \"You must specify at least one of `hyperparam_mutations` or \"\n                \"`custom_explore_fn` to use PBT.\")\n\n        if quantile_fraction > 0.5 or quantile_fraction < 0:\n            raise TuneError(\n                \"You must set `quantile_fraction` to a value between 0 and\"\n                \"0.5.     "Title": "Differentiable Expected Hypervolume Improvement for Parallel Multi-Objective Bayesian Optimization",
    "Main Contributions": "The paper introduces q-Expected Hypervolume Improvement (qEHVI), a novel acquisition function for parallel, constrained multi-objective Bayesian optimization. Key contributions include: 1) an exact formulation of qEHVI for batch, constrained settings using Monte-Carlo (MC) integration, 2) computation of exact gradients of the MC estimator via auto-differentiation, enabling efficient gradient-based optimization, 3) theoretical convergence guarantees under the Sample Average Approximation (SAA) approach, 4) demonstration of computational tractability and superior performance on synthetic and real-world benchmarks at a fraction of the wall time of state-of-the-art methods, and 5) extension of qEHVI to support auxiliary outcome constraints and a proper sequential greedy optimization approach.",
    "Methodology": "The methodology leverages recent advances in programming models and hardware acceleration. It extends the Hypervolume Improvement (HVI) computation to 'q' new candidate points using the inclusion-exclusion principle over disjoint axis-parallel box decompositions of the non-dominated space. Expected q-Hypervolume Improvement (qEHVI) is then estimated via Monte-Carlo integration using samples from the joint Gaussian Process (GP) posterior, with randomized quasi-MC methods employed for variance reduction. Exact gradients of the MC estimator are computed using the re-parameterization trick and auto-differentiation, which allows for efficient optimization with deterministic, higher-order methods via the Sample Average Approximation (SAA) approach. The method is extended to handle outcome constraints by feasibility-weighting on the sample level using a differentiable sigmoid approximation for the indicator function. The formulation is embarrassingly parallel.",
    "Experimental Setup": "qEHVI was empirically evaluated on four benchmark problems and compared against state-of-the-art multi-objective BO algorithms: SMS-EGO, PESMO, TS-TCH, analytic EHVI (with gradients), and a novel extension of ParEGO called qPAREGO, plus a quasi-random baseline. Benchmarks included synthetic problems (Branin-Currin, C2-DTLZ2, DTLZ2 for 3 and 4 objectives, and a noisy Branin-Currin) and real-world problems (Vehicle Crash Safety with 3 objectives and Adaptive Bitrate Control for video streaming with 2 objectives). Performance was measured by log hypervolume difference or hypervolume indicator. All methods used independent Gaussian Processes with Matern 5/2 ARD kernels. qEHVI and qPAREGO used N=128 QMC samples. Acquisition functions were optimized with L-BFGS-B (exact gradients for qEHVI, EHVI, qPAREGO; finite differences for others) or CMA-ES for gradient-free cases. Experiments were conducted on a CPU (2x Intel Xeon E5-2680 v4) and a GPU (Tesla V100-SXM2-16GB).",
    "Limitations": "The current approach assumes noiseless observations, which is a common limitation among all known formulations of EHVI. Another limitation is that qEHVI's scalability is restricted by the performance of the underlying partitioning algorithm for the non-dominated space, which can preclude its efficient use in high-dimensional objective spaces (e.g., M>=4). Furthermore, for very large numbers of objectives (M) and batch sizes (q) on GPUs, memory load can become a practical concern.",
    "Future Research Directions": "Future research directions include: 1) integrating over the uncertainty around previous observations to account for noise in experiments, potentially by using MC samples over new candidates and training points, 2) exploring more scalable partitioning algorithms (either approximate or more efficient exact ones) that result in fewer disjoint hyper-rectangles to improve qEHVI's scalability and computation time in higher-dimensional objective spaces, 3) deriving convergence rates for the optimizer, similar to existing work, and 4) extending convergence results to randomized quasi Monte-Carlo methods for sample generation. The authors also encourage broader research into applying modern computational paradigms and tooling to Bayesian optimization.",

None) -> Tensor:\n        obj = self.objective(samples, X=X)\n        q = obj.shape[-2]\n        if self.constraints is not None:\n            feas_weights = compute_smoothed_feasibility_indicator(\n                constraints=self.constraints,\n                samples=samples,\n                eta=self.eta,\n                fat=self.fat,\n            )\n        device = self.ref_point.device\n       )\n    else:\n        eta = torch.full(\n            (len(constraints),),\n            eta,\n            dtype=samples.dtype,\n            device=samples.device,\n        )\n    log_sigmoid_fn =     "Experiment Result": "The methodology computes Expected q-Hypervolume Improvement (qEHVI) using Monte-Carlo (MC) integration. Randomized quasi-Monte Carlo (QMC) methods, specifically Sobol sequences via `SobolQMCNormalSampler`, are employed for variance reduction. Exact gradients of the MC estimator are derived through the re-parameterization trick and auto-differentiation, enabling efficient optimization via the Sample Average Approximation (SAA) approach. Outcome constraints are handled by feasibility-weighting on the sample level, approximated using a differentiable sigmoid function (from `compute_smoothed_feasibility_indicator` with an `eta` parameter). The core HVI computation is based on the inclusion-exclusion principle over disjoint axis-parallel box decompositions of the non-dominated space, implemented by `NondominatedPartitioning`. Numerical stability for Gaussian Process (GP) computations is enhanced by modifying GPyTorch and Linear Operator settings: disabling fast covariance root decomposition (`linear_operator.settings._fast_covar_root_decomposition._default = False`), fast log probability (`linear_operator.settings._fast_log_prob._default = False`), and fast solves (`linear_operator.settings._fast_solves._default = False`), while increasing `cholesky_max_tries` to 6, `max_cholesky_size` to 4096, and `max_eager_kernel_size` to 4096."

    "Title": "Bayesian Optimization for Iterative Learning",
    "Main Contributions": "The paper introduces Bayesian Optimization for Iterative Learning (BOIL), an approach for efficient hyperparameter tuning of deep (reinforcement) learning systems. It addresses the high computational cost and issues with noisy, iterative training by proposing to compress entire learning curves into a single numeric score based on training success and stability, rather than relying solely on final performance. A key contribution is a data augmentation technique that selectively includes intermediate scores to improve sample-efficiency and prevent GP model ill-conditioning. BOIL balances the benefit of extended training steps against their computational cost, demonstrating superior performance over baselines in identifying optimal hyperparameters in minimal wall-clock time.",
    "Methodology": "BOIL employs a Bayesian Optimization framework that models the joint space of input hyperparameters (x) and training iterations (t) using a Gaussian Process (GP) surrogate. It uses a product kernel and approximates training time cost with a linear regressor. The objective function is defined by compressing the full learning curve into a numeric score using a Sigmoid (Logistic) preference function, whose parameters (growth and middle point) are learned by maximizing the GP marginal likelihood. To enhance sample efficiency and model robustness, the method selectively augments training data by choosing intermediate points from the learning curve that maximize GP predictive uncertainty, while ensuring the GP covariance matrix remains well-conditioned (condition number below a threshold). An acquisition function based on Expected Improvement (EI) is used to select the next evaluation point, balancing utility, uncertainty, and cost.",
    "Experimental Setup": "The model was assessed by tuning hyperparameters for Dueling DQN on the CartPole-v0 environment, Advantage Actor Critic (A2C) on InvertedPendulum-v2 and Reacher-v2 (Mujoco), and Convolutional Neural Networks (CNNs) on SVHN and CIFAR10 datasets. Experiments were averaged over 20 independent runs on an NVIDIA 1080 GTX GPU using TensorFlow-gpu, OpenAI Gym, and OpenAI Baselines. Baselines included Hyperband, Continuous Multi-Task/Multi-Fidelity BO (CM-T/F-BO), vanilla BO, and BO-L (BO with curve compression). Ablation studies were conducted to evaluate the impact of curve compression, robustness across different preference functions (Sigmoid, Log, Average), and the use of a Freeze-Thaw kernel for the time component.",
    "Limitations": "Existing early stopping criteria (e.g., exponential decay in Freeze-thaw BO) are often unsuitable for DRL due to unpredictable fluctuations and noise in reward curves, potentially leading to premature termination of promising training runs. Naive data augmentation by adding full learning curves can lead to redundant information and ill-conditioning of the GP covariance matrix. Hyperband can be misled by noisy early performance in DRL, hindering efficient resource allocation. The study did not compare various acquisition functions or kernel choices beyond the square-exponential, and Fabolas was not directly compared as it focuses on dataset size rather than iteration numbers. A broader concern is that increased automation via such methods might further detach humans from the modeling process, making critical failure detection harder and increasing model opacity.",
    "Future Research Directions": "The proposed Bayesian optimization framework is not limited to machine learning and can be applied to any iterative process that can benefit from exploiting its step-wise structure, such as optimizing manufacturing pipelines to improve productivity. Furthermore, the paper implicitly suggests future work in ensuring responsible deployment of ML models trained with such automated systems, advocating for the integration of interpretability methods to rigorously analyze final training outcomes and address concerns about growing model opacity and potential biases.",

    "Experiment Result": "The BOIL method employs the following experimental settings:\n- **Gaussian Process (GP) Surrogate:**\n    - Kernel: Product of RBF kernels for input hyperparameters (x) and training iterations (t).\n    - Noise parameter (`noise_delta`): Initialized to 5e-4 in `ProductGaussianProcess`.\n    - Initial lengthscale for x (`lengthscale_x`): 0.02.\n    - Initial lengthscale for t (`lengthscale_t`): 0.2.\n    - Optimization bounds for lengthscales during hyperparameter learning:\n        - `lengthscale_x`: [0.03, 0.3]\n        - `lengthscale_t`: [0.3, 0.6] (derived from `[10*SearchSpace_l_min, 2*SearchSpace_l_max]` with `SearchSpace_l_min=0.03`, `SearchSpace_l_max=0.3`).\n    - Hyperparameter optimization frequency: Every `2 * dim` iterations.\n    - Optimizer for hyperparameters: L-BFGS-B with `maxiter=30*dim` and `maxfun=30*dim`.\n- **Sigmoid (Logistic) Preference Function:**\n    - Initial midpoint: 0.0.\n    - Initial growth: 1.0.\n    - Optimization bounds for midpoint: `[-2, 3]`.\n    - Optimization bounds for growth: `[0.5, 2]`.\n    - Parameters are learned jointly with GP hyperparameters by maximizing GP marginal likelihood.\n- **Cost Model:**\n    - Training time cost is approximated by `sklearn.linear_model.LinearRegression`.\n- **Acquisition Function:**\n    - Type: Expected Improvement (EI), specifically `ei_mu_max`, which uses the maximum of the GP predictive mean as the incumbent (`mu_max`).\n    - Optimization method: Multi-start L-BFGS-B (via `acq_min_scipy_kwargs` in `acquisition_maximization.py`).\n    - Optimizer settings: `maxiter=10*dim`, `maxfun=20*dim`.\n    - Initial points for multi-start: `100*dim` random uniform samples within the search space.\n    - Balancing: The acquisition function is optimized to minimize `-log(utility) + log(cost)`, effectively maximizing `log(utility/cost)`.\n- **Data Augmentation:**\n    - Initial real observations: `n_init_points=3` (default).\n    - Method: Selects intermediate points from the learning curve within the range `[0, t_max]` for the selected hyperparameter `x_max`.\n    - Selection criteria: Maximizes GP predictive uncertainty using the `pure_exploration` acquisition function.\n    - Maximum number of augmented points: `max_n_augmentation=10` per new real observation, also capped by `t_max`.\n    - Robustness control: Augmentation stops if the natural logarithm of the GP covariance matrix condition number exceeds a threshold of `15` (`threshold_cond=15`), or if the predictive uncertainty falls below `(self.gp.noise_delta + 1e-3)`."
