    "Title": "Conformal Inference is (almost) Free for Neural Networks Trained with Early Stopping",
    "Main Contributions": "The paper introduces Conformalized Early Stopping (CES), a novel method that efficiently combines early stopping with conformal calibration using the same hold-out data. This approach addresses the limitation of neural networks trained with early stopping, which often lack precise statistical guarantees and suffer from overconfidence. CES provides models that are both accurate and capable of offering exact predictive inferences without requiring multiple data splits or overly conservative adjustments, thereby yielding rigorous predictive inferences at no additional data cost compared to standard early stopping. Practical implementations are developed and demonstrated for outlier detection, multi-class classification, and regression tasks, showing competitive performance on real-world data.",
    "Methodology": "CES operates by performing a two-way data split into a training set (Dtrain) and a combined early stopping and calibration set (Des-cal). A neural network is trained on Dtrain for a maximum number of epochs, storing intermediate models at predefined intervals. For a given test point, a model `ˆMces` is selected by minimizing an augmented loss function (`L+1 es-cal`) on the Des-cal set, which includes the test point as a placeholder, ensuring its involvement in the early stopping rule. Conformal p-values or prediction sets are then computed using nonconformity scores derived from the selected `ˆMces` and the Des-cal set. Specific implementations include: for outlier detection, a one-class classifier and corresponding loss; for multi-class classification, a K-class classifier with cross-entropy loss, where models are selected for each possible label using adaptive nonconformity scores; for regression, a neural network with squared-error loss, where model selection across continuous outcomes is solved efficiently by finding the lower envelope of parabolas (`O(T log T)` computational cost); and an extension to quantile regression (CQR) employing pinball loss and a similar lower envelope algorithm for shifted pinball loss functions. To prevent empty prediction sets in regression, a fallback to a naive conformal prediction interval is implemented.",
    "Experimental Setup": "The effectiveness of CES is demonstrated across several learning tasks. For outlier detection and multi-class classification, experiments are conducted using the CIFAR10 dataset, employing a convolutional neural network with ReLU activation and cross-entropy loss. For standard regression, evaluations are performed on three UCI datasets: 'bio', 'bike', and 'concrete', utilizing a neural network with two hidden layers and ReLU. For quantile regression, seven UCI datasets are used: 'MEPS 21', 'blog data', 'STAR', 'community', 'bio', 'homes', and 'bike', with a neural network featuring three hidden layers, ReLU, pinball loss, and Adam optimizer. Training involves a maximum of 50 epochs for CIFAR10, 1000 for standard regression, and 2000 for quantile regression, with intermediate models saved at specified periods. Data allocation for CES involves 75% for training and 25% for early stopping and calibration. Test sets typically consist of 100 or 1000 independent data points. Performance is measured by False Positive Rate (FPR) and True Positive Rate (TPR) for outlier detection; average cardinality and marginal coverage for classification; and marginal coverage, worst-slab conditional coverage, and prediction interval width for regression tasks (targeting 90% marginal coverage). CES is benchmarked against naive early stopping with theoretical correction, a data splitting approach (50% train, 25% early stop, 25% calibration), and full training. All results are averaged over multiple independent trials (100 for most, 25 for quantile regression).",
    "Limitations": "The limitations include increased memory cost due to storing all intermediate candidate models, which, while feasible in many applications, is higher than standard early stopping. For regression tasks, the `O(T log T)` computational cost of the lower envelope algorithm might become a barrier if the number of candidate models (`T`) is very large, suggesting the use of moderate `T` values. The core regression algorithms (Algorithms 3 and 4) may sometimes produce empty prediction sets, requiring a fallback mechanism to a naive conformal prediction interval, indicating a potential weakness in the base method. Furthermore, achieving perfect feature-conditional coverage is noted as extremely difficult, leading the classification method to primarily guarantee weaker forms of coverage (label-conditional or marginal). The initial regression CES is specific to squared-error loss, limiting its direct efficiency for heteroscedastic data without extensions. Lastly, while the paper notes that a naive benchmark (using the same hold-out data twice) often performs similarly to CES in practice, it lacks theoretical validity without conservative corrections, and even these theoretical corrections prove overly conservative.",
    "Future Research Directions": "Future research could extend the core methodology of integrating data-driven model selection with conformal calibration beyond just early stopping, applying it to tune hyper-parameters or select models from arbitrary machine learning toolboxes for multi-class classification and regression problems. The proposed CES method could also be combined with existing research on improving the robustness of conformal inferences to distribution shifts and failures of the data exchangeability assumption. Further exploration is possible in accommodating diverse nonconformity scores for regression beyond classical absolute residuals, such as those based on conditional distributions or conditional histograms, to handle heteroscedastic data more efficiently. Additionally, investigating the empirical success of the naive benchmark (without theoretical corrections) could provide insights for developing computationally less demanding alternatives when CES's memory costs are prohibitive.",

    "Experiment Result": null

    "Title": "Hyperparameter Optimization through Neural Network Partitioning",
    "Main Contributions": "The paper proposes Partitioned Neural Networks, a novel and efficient method for hyperparameter optimization (HO) that is inspired by marginal likelihood. It allows optimizing a variety of hyperparameters within a single training run, without requiring a separate validation set. This approach partitions the training data into K shards and the neural network model into K parameter partitions, associating each partition with specific data shards for optimization. The 'out-of-training-sample' loss of subnetworks (loss on unseen data shards) serves as the objective for HO. The method is significantly computationally cheaper and more scalable than previous marginal likelihood approximations, avoiding the need to compute or invert Hessians. It also demonstrates particular utility and reduced communication overhead in Federated Learning settings.",
    "Methodology": "The core methodology involves optimizing a lower-bound approximation to the marginal likelihood, denoted as LML(D,ψ), with respect to hyperparameters ψ. This is achieved by: 1) **Parameter Partitioning**: The neural network weights (w) are divided into C partitions (w1, ..., wC). C subnetworks w(k)s are defined, where w(k)s consists of the first k learned partitions and subsequent parameters are fixed to default values (e.g., initialization values or zero). This approximates a model trained on subsets of data D1:k. 2) **Partitioned Training**: The training dataset D is split into C chunks (D1, ..., DC). Each parameter partition wk is updated using gradients computed from the negative log-likelihood on data chunks D1:k via its respective subnetwork w(k)s. Hyperparameters ψ are updated using the LML objective, which sums the 'out-of-sample' losses of subnetworks w(k−1)s on the currently unseen data chunks Dk. The common partitioning scheme used is 'random weight partitioning', where a fixed proportion of weights in each layer is randomly assigned to a partition. For Federated Learning, clients are assigned to data chunks, computing gradients for relevant parameter partitions and hyperparameters locally, and only updated partitions are communicated to the server, reducing upload costs.",
    "Experimental Setup": "The method was evaluated on several tasks: toy input selection (both non-differentiable and differentiable mask learning), learning invariances through affine data augmentations, optimizing general feature extractors, and hyperparameter optimization in federated learning. Datasets included a synthetic toy dataset, MNIST, RotMNIST, CIFAR10, RotCIFAR10, TinyImagenet, and RotTinyImagenet, including non-i.i.d. federated learning splits. Architectures varied from fully connected NNs for toy tasks to fixupResNets (8, 14, 50 layers), Wide ResNet-20, and custom CNNs for MNIST. Baselines included Post-hoc Diagonal Laplace, standard training (with/without augmentations), Augerino, Differentiable Laplace, Last-layer marginal-likelihood, and traditional training/validation split with finetuning. For FL, FedAvg and FedAvg+Augerino were used. Validation was performed using test accuracy, log-likelihood, and empirical timing/memory complexity analysis. Sensitivity to partitioning schemes was investigated via grid-search, and the quality of found hyperparameters was validated by retraining vanilla models. Training details involved Adam/SGD optimizers, various learning rates, cosine annealing, and a concrete Bernoulli distribution for learnable masks/dropout, with specific chunk proportions (e.g., [80%,10%,10%] for MNIST/CIFAR).",
    "Limitations": "The proposed method has several limitations: 1) **Computational Complexity**: It inherently requires an additional forward-backward pass for hyperparameter updates, leading to some overhead compared to standard training, though still significantly less than prior marginal likelihood-based methods. 2) **Convergence Speed**: Empirically, partitioned networks tend to require more training iterations to converge. 3) **Performance**: Partitioning the network can constrain its capacity, potentially leading to a slight performance loss compared to a full, non-partitioned network trained with perfectly tuned hyperparameters. 4) **Partitioning Strategy**: The method introduces a new set of hyperparameters related to the partitioning scheme itself (e.g., number of chunks, relative proportions of data and parameters), which might require additional tuning to achieve optimal performance.",
    "Future Research Directions": "Future research could explore: 1) **Alternative Partitioning Schemes**: Developing methods that side-step the need for separate forward-backward passes for hyperparameter updates or allow for dynamic partitioning of network parameters during training. 2) **Optimized Update Schedules**: Investigating different sequential updating schemes for Federated Learning clients and exploring strategies for accumulating gradients from multiple chunks or interleaving hyperparameter updates at less frequent intervals to reduce computational overhead. 3) **Performance Enhancement**: Researching ways to alleviate the performance loss associated with network partitioning, possibly through adjusting training rounds or increasing network capacity. 4) **Broader Hyperparameter Applications**: Applying the method to more complex hyperparameter settings, such as learning data augmentation transformations parameterized by a neural network, where devising adequate regularizers is non-trivial for existing methods.",
    "Experiment Code": null,
    "Experiment Result": null

    "Title": "Meta-learning to Improve Pre-training",
    "Main Contributions": "The paper addresses the challenge of optimizing high-dimensional pre-training (PT) hyperparameters, or meta-parameters, in the two-stage pre-training and fine-tuning (PT&FT) paradigm. It formalizes 'Meta-Parameterized Pre-Training' and proposes an efficient, scalable, gradient-based algorithm to meta-learn these meta-parameters. The key innovation is a novel gradient estimation method that combines a constant-memory implicit differentiation approximation for the longer PT stage and exact backpropagation through unrolled optimization for the shorter FT stage. The method is shown to improve predictive performance in two real-world domains: multitask PT on protein-protein interaction graphs (up to 3.9% AUROC improvement) and self-supervised PT with SimCLR on electrocardiography data (up to 1.9% AUROC improvement).",
    "Methodology": "The proposed method is a gradient-based algorithm that optimizes meta-parameters φ by minimizing a meta-PT optimization objective, which is the final fine-tuning (FT) loss. It uses a novel gradient estimator composed of two parts: (1) For the PT best response Jacobian (∂AlgPT/∂φ), it uses the Implicit Function Theorem (IFT), approximating the inverse Hessian with a truncated Neumann series (typically 1 step) to handle the long PT optimization horizon and large model sizes. (2) For the FT best response Jacobian (∂AlgFT/∂θPT), it uses exact backpropagation through a small number of unrolled optimization steps (K steps, typically K=1) during FT, leveraging the shorter optimization horizon of FT. The algorithm allows for online updates of PT, FT, and meta-parameters, with warm-up iterations. It is designed to scale to high-dimensional meta-parameters and large encoder models, demonstrating computational tractability where meta-parameterized PT has less than twice the time cost of standard PT.",
    "Experimental Setup": "The method was validated through synthetic and real-world experiments. For synthetic validation, MNIST datasets were used to optimize low-dimensional meta-parameters (1-D mean of a Normal distribution for rotation augmentations) and high-dimensional meta-parameters (neural network weights for per-example weighting in the presence of noisy labels). Real-world experiments included: (1) Multitask Pre-Training for Graph Neural Networks, using protein-protein interaction graphs (88K graphs) for predicting 5000 PT protein functions and 40 FT protein functions. Meta-parameters were 5000 task weights for the PT loss. A Graph Isomorphism Network (GIN) architecture was used, trained for 100 PT epochs and 50 FT epochs. Baselines included No PT, Graph Supervised PT, CoTrain, and CoTrain + PCGrad. (2) Semi-Supervised Learning with SimCLR on Electrocardiography (ECG) data (PTB-XL dataset). Input signals were 1000x12 ECGs, with 5 binary classification tasks for pathologies. Meta-parameters were weights of a neural network determining the warp strength for temporal data augmentation. A 1D CNN based on ResNet-18 was used, trained for 50 PT epochs and 200 FT epochs. Baselines included No PT, SimCLR (fixed augmentations), and SimCLR + Optimized Supervised Learning Augmentations. Both real-world settings explored Full FT Access and Partial FT Access scenarios, evaluating performance using AUROC.",
    "Limitations": "The algorithm is applicable when differentiable PT meta-parameters are to be optimized and access to a model, PT data, and FT data is available. It is not applicable if no FT data is available at meta-PT time, or if the model/PT data is unavailable. The meta-parameters must be differentiable, and the method cannot directly optimize meta-parameters that do not affect the PT optimization landscape (e.g., PT learning rates). The paper acknowledges a potential for short-horizon bias in online optimization, particularly for hyperparameters like learning rates, but argues it's not a significant concern for the meta-parameters considered. Furthermore, for ECG data, the observed gains were modest with simple augmentation policies, suggesting that more complex policies might be needed for larger improvements.",
    "Future Research Directions": "Future work could involve applying the proposed method to learn other types of meta-parameters, such as learned auxiliary tasks or noise models. Given the use of healthcare data in experiments, it is also recommended that the method undergo further validation in retrospective and prospective studies to detect failure modes and potential harm before deployment in real-world clinical settings.",
    "Experiment Code": null,
    "Experiment Result": null

    "Title": "Continuation Path Learning for Homotopy Optimization",
    "Main Contributions": "The paper proposes Continuation Path Learning (CPL), a novel model-based approach for homotopy optimization that learns the entire continuation path, encompassing infinite intermediate solutions. Unlike classic methods that iteratively solve a sequence of finite subproblems unidirectionally, CPL simultaneously optimizes the original problem and all surrogate subproblems in a collaborative manner. The model supports real-time generation of any intermediate solution, offering flexibility for various applications. Experimental results demonstrate that CPL significantly enhances homotopy optimization performance and provides valuable information for improved decision-making across non-convex optimization, noisy regression, and neural combinatorial optimization problems.",
    "Methodology": "CPL reformulates homotopy optimization as a single-loop model training problem. It builds a learnable neural network model, xϕ(t), with parameters ϕ, to approximate the continuous continuation path x∗(t) by mapping any homotopy level t to its corresponding solution. The model is trained by minimizing the expectation EtH(xϕ(t), t) using stochastic gradient descent with Monte Carlo sampling. Gradients ∇ϕH are computed via the chain rule, decomposing into ∂xϕ(t) ∂ϕ and ∇xH(x, t). When ∇xH is unknown or complex, a zeroth-order optimization method, such as Evolutionary Strategy (ES), or a specialized Gaussian homotopy gradient approximation, is used. An optional fast local search, initialized with xϕ(t') from the learned path model, can be applied to further refine solutions for specific homotopy levels. For neural combinatorial optimization, CPL conditions policy model parameters (e.g., query and projection parameters in an Attention Model decoder) on the continuation level t, generating dynamic policies pθ(t)(π|s) to construct solutions for smoothed subproblems, trained using REINFORCE with POMO rollout.",
    "Experimental Setup": "The CPL method was evaluated on three main problem types. For **non-convex optimization**, it was tested on Ackley, Rosenbrock, and Himmelblau functions using Gaussian homotopy, comparing against simple gradient descent, classical Gaussian optimization (GradOpt), and single-loop Gaussian homotopy (SLGHr). CPL used a fully connected neural network (two hidden layers, 128 nodes) with 95% of function evaluations for training and 5% for local search. For **noisy regression**, four different noisy nonlinear regression problems (F1-F4) were used, where CPL learned the continuation path of regularization strength using a simple fully connected neural network. For **neural combinatorial optimization**, CPL was applied to the Traveling Salesman Problem (TSP) and Capacitated Vehicle Routing Problem (CVRP). An Attention Model (AM) was used as the policy network, with specific decoder parameters conditioned on the homotopy level via an MLP. Training involved Adam optimizer, randomly generated instances (100,000 instances/epoch), and REINFORCE with POMO rollout. Inference sampled multiple continuation levels and selected the best resulting tour. Benchmarks included random TSP/CVRP instances (20, 50, 100 cities/nodes) and realistic TSPLib (51-200 cities) and CVRPLIB instances, with comparisons to various exact, heuristic, and learning-based solvers (e.g., Concorde, LKH3, OR-Tools, DACT, POMO).",
    "Limitations": "A primary limitation of CPL is the necessity to build and train a specific model for learning the continuation path. The optimal model design is problem-dependent, often requiring domain knowledge for efficient construction. Furthermore, in neural combinatorial optimization, the generalization performance of CPL can drastically decrease for instances with significantly larger numbers of cities than those used in the training set.",
    "Future Research Directions": "Important future work includes conducting additional theoretical analyses, particularly on problem transformation and the relationship between CPL and the value function approach. Extending the solution model method for a more general value function approach is also a promising area. Investigating how findings from smooth parametrization research could inform CPL's training of large deep neural networks is suggested. Finally, for neural combinatorial optimization, designing improved continuation path construction and sampling methods to enhance the performance-time ratio of CPL is an interesting direction.",
    "Experiment Code": "import torch\nimport numpy as np\nimport torch.nn as nn\nimport torch.nn.functional as F\nfrom dataclasses import dataclass\n\n# --- CVRP Environment State DataClasses ---\n@dataclass\nclass Reset_State_CVRP:\n    depot_xy: torch.Tensor = None\n    node_xy: torch.Tensor = None\n    node_demand: torch.Tensor = None\n\n@dataclass\nclass Step_State_CVRP:\n    BATCH_IDX: torch.Tensor = None\n    POMO_IDX: torch.Tensor = None\n    selected_count: int = None\n    load: torch.Tensor = None\n    current_node: torch.Tensor = None\n    ninf_mask: torch.Tensor = None\n    finished: torch.Tensor = None\n\n# --- TSP Environment State DataClasses ---\n@dataclass\nclass Reset_State_TSP:\n    problems: torch.Tensor\n\n@dataclass\nclass Step_State_TSP:\n    BATCH_IDX: torch.Tensor\n    POMO_IDX: torch.Tensor\n    current_node: torch.Tensor = None\n    ninf_mask: torch.Tensor = None\n\n# --- CVRP Problem Definition Helpers ---\ndef get_random_problems_cvrp(batch_size, problem_size):\n    depot_xy = torch.rand(size=(batch_size, 1, 2))\n    node_xy = torch.rand(size=(batch_size, problem_size, 2))\n    if problem_size == 20:\n        demand_scaler = 30\n    elif problem_size == 50:\n        demand_scaler = 40\n    elif problem_size == 100:\n        demand_scaler = 50\n    else:\n        raise NotImplementedError\n    node_demand = torch.randint(1, 10, size=(batch_size, problem_size)) / float(demand_scaler)\n    return depot_xy, node_xy, node_demand\n\n# --- TSP Problem Definition Helpers ---\ndef get_random_problems_tsp(batch_size, problem_size):\n    problems = torch.rand(size=(batch_size, problem_size, 2))\n    return problems\n\n# --- Shared Data Augmentation Function ---\ndef augment_xy_data_by_8_fold(xy_data):\n    x = xy_data[:, :, [0]]\n    y = xy_data[:, :, [1]]\n    dat1 = torch.cat((x, y), dim=2)\n    dat2 = torch.cat((1 - x, y), dim=2)\n    dat3 = torch.cat((x, 1 - y), dim=2)\n    dat4 = torch.cat((1 - x, 1 - y), dim=2)\n    dat5 = torch.cat((y, x), dim=2)\n    dat6 = torch.cat((1 - y, x), dim=2)\n    dat7 = torch.cat((y, 1 - x), dim=2)\n    dat8 = torch.cat((1 - y, 1 - x), dim=2)\n    aug_xy_data = torch.cat((dat1, dat2, dat3, dat4, dat5, dat6, dat7, dat8), dim=0)\n    return aug_xy_data\n\n# --- CVRP Environment ---\nclass CVRPEnv:\n    def __init__(self, **env_params):\n        self.env_params = env_params\n        self.problem_size = env_params['problem_size']\n        self.pomo_size = env_params['pomo_size']\n        self.FLAG__use_saved_problems = False\n        self.saved_depot_xy = None\n        self.saved_node_xy = None\n        self.saved_node_demand = None\n        self.saved_index = None\n        self.batch_size = None\n        self.BATCH_IDX = None\n        self.POMO_IDX = None\n        self.depot_node_xy = None\n        self.depot_node_demand = None\n        self.selected_count = None\n        self.current_node = None\n        self.selected_node_list = None\n        self.at_the_depot = None\n        self.load = None\n        self.visited_ninf_flag = None\n        self.ninf_mask = None\n        self.finished = None\n        self.reset_state = Reset_State_CVRP()\n        self.step_state = Step_State_CVRP()\n\n    def use_saved_problems(self, filename, device):\n        self.FLAG__use_saved_problems = True\n        loaded_dict = torch.load(filename, map_location=device)\n        self.saved_depot_xy = loaded_dict['depot_xy']\n        self.saved_node_xy = loaded_dict['node_xy']\n        self.saved_node_demand = loaded_dict['node_demand']\n        self.saved_index = 0\n\n    def load_problems(self, batch_size, aug_factor=1):\n        self.batch_size = batch_size\n        if not self.FLAG__use_saved_problems:\n            depot_xy, node_xy, node_demand = get_random_problems_cvrp(batch_size, self.problem_size)\n        else:\n            depot_xy = self.saved_depot_xy[self.saved_index:self.saved_index+batch_size]\n            node_xy = self.saved_node_xy[self.saved_index:self.saved_index+batch_size]\n            node_demand = self.saved_node_demand[self.saved_index:self.saved_index+batch_size]\n            self.saved_index += batch_size\n        if aug_factor > 1:\n            if aug_factor == 8:\n                self.batch_size = self.batch_size * 8\n                depot_xy = augment_xy_data_by_8_fold(depot_xy)\n                node_xy = augment_xy_data_by_8_fold(node_xy)\n                node_demand = node_demand.repeat(8, 1)\n            else:\n                raise NotImplementedError\n        self.depot_node_xy = torch.cat((depot_xy, node_xy), dim=1)\n        depot_demand = torch.zeros(size=(self.batch_size, 1))\n        self.depot_node_demand = torch.cat((depot_demand, node_demand), dim=1)\n        self.BATCH_IDX = torch.arange(self.batch_size)[:, None].expand(self.batch_size, self.pomo_size)\n        self.POMO_IDX = torch.arange(self.pomo_size)[None, :].expand(self.batch_size, self.pomo_size)\n        self.reset_state.depot_xy = depot_xy\n        self.reset_state.node_xy = node_xy\n        self.reset_state.node_demand = node_demand\n        self.step_state.BATCH_IDX = self.BATCH_IDX\n        self.step_state.POMO_IDX = self.POMO_IDX\n\n    def reset(self):\n        self.selected_count = 0\n        self.current_node = None\n        self.selected_node_list = torch.zeros((self.batch_size, self.pomo_size, 0), dtype=torch.long)\n        self.at_the_depot = torch.ones(size=(self.batch_size, self.pomo_size), dtype=torch.bool)\n        self.load = torch.ones(size=(self.batch_size, self.pomo_size))\n        self.visited_ninf_flag = torch.zeros(size=(self.batch_size, self.pomo_size, self.problem_size+1))\n        self.ninf_mask = torch.zeros(size=(self.batch_size, self.pomo_size, self.problem_size+1))\n        self.finished = torch.zeros(size=(self.batch_size, self.pomo_size), dtype=torch.bool)\n        reward = None\n        done = False\n        return self.reset_state, reward, done\n\n    def pre_step(self):\n        self.step_state.selected_count = self.selected_count\n        self.step_state.load = self.load\n        self.step_state.current_node = self.current_node\n        self.step_state.ninf_mask = self.ninf_mask\n        self.step_state.finished = self.finished\n        reward = None\n        done = False\n        return self.step_state, reward, done\n\n    def step(self, selected):\n        self.selected_count += 1\n        self.current_node = selected\n        self.selected_node_list = torch.cat((self.selected_node_list, self.current_node[:, :, None]), dim=2)\n        self.at_the_depot = (selected == 0)\n        demand_list = self.depot_node_demand[:, None, :].expand(self.batch_size, self.pomo_size, -1)\n        gathering_index = selected[:, :, None]\n        selected_demand = demand_list.gather(dim=2, index=gathering_index).squeeze(dim=2)\n        self.load -= selected_demand\n        self.load[self.at_the_depot] = 1\n        self.visited_ninf_flag[self.BATCH_IDX, self.POMO_IDX, selected] = float('-inf')\n        self.visited_ninf_flag[:, :, 0][~self.at_the_depot] = 0\n        self.ninf_mask = self.visited_ninf_flag.clone()\n        round_error_epsilon = 0.00001\n        demand_too_large = self.load[:, :, None] + round_error_epsilon < demand_list\n        self.ninf_mask[demand_too_large] = float('-inf')\n        newly_finished = (self.visited_ninf_flag == float('-inf')).all(dim=2)\n        self.finished = self.finished + newly_finished\n        self.ninf_mask[:, :, 0][self.finished] = 0\n        self.step_state.selected_count = self.selected_count\n        self.step_state.load = self.load\n        self.step_state.current_node = self.current_node\n        self.step_state.ninf_mask = self.ninf_mask\n        self.step_state.finished = self.finished\n        done = self.finished.all()\n        if done:\n            reward = -self._get_travel_distance()\n        else:\n            reward = None\n        return self.step_state, reward, done\n\n    def _get_travel_distance(self):\n        gathering_index = self.selected_node_list[:, :, :, None].expand(-1, -1, -1, 2)\n        all_xy = self.depot_node_xy[:, None, :, :].expand(-1, self.pomo_size, -1, -1)\n        ordered_seq = all_xy.gather(dim=2, index=gathering_index)\n        rolled_seq = ordered_seq.roll(dims=2, shifts=-1)\n        segment_lengths = ((ordered_seq-rolled_seq)**2).sum(3).sqrt()\n        travel_distances = segment_lengths.sum(2)\n        return travel_distances\n\n# --- TSP Environment ---\nclass TSPEnv:\n    def __init__(self, **env_params):\n        self.env_params = env_params\n        self.problem_size = env_params['problem_size']\n        self.pomo_size = env_params['pomo_size']\n        self.batch_size = None\n        self.BATCH_IDX = None\n        self.POMO_IDX = None\n        self.problems = None\n        self.selected_count = None\n        self.current_node = None\n        self.selected_node_list = None\n        self.step_state = None\n\n    def load_problems(self, batch_size, aug_factor=1):\n        self.batch_size = batch_size\n        self.problems = get_random_problems_tsp(batch_size, self.problem_size)\n        if aug_factor > 1:\n            if aug_factor == 8:\n                self.batch_size = self.batch_size * 8\n                self.problems = augment_xy_data_by_8_fold(self.problems)\n            else:\n                raise NotImplementedError\n        self.BATCH_IDX = torch.arange(self.batch_size)[:, None].expand(self.batch_size, self.pomo_size)\n        self.POMO_IDX = torch.arange(self.pomo_size)[None, :].expand(self.batch_size, self.pomo_size)\n\n    def reset(self):\n        self.selected_count = 0\n        self.current_node = None\n        self.selected_node_list = torch.zeros((self.batch_size, self.pomo_size, 0), dtype=torch.long)\n        self.step_state = Step_State_TSP(BATCH_IDX=self.BATCH_IDX, POMO_IDX=self.POMO_IDX)\n        self.step_state.ninf_mask = torch.zeros((self.batch_size, self.pomo_size, self.problem_size))\n        reward = None\n        done = False\n        return Reset_State_TSP(self.problems), reward, done\n\n    def pre_step(self):\n        reward = None\n        done = False\n        return self.step_state, reward, done\n\n    def step(self, selected):\n        self.selected_count += 1\n        self.current_node = selected\n        self.selected_node_list = torch.cat((self.selected_node_list, self.current_node[:, :, None]), dim=2)\n        self.step_state.current_node = self.current_node\n        self.step_state.ninf_mask[self.BATCH_IDX, self.POMO_IDX, self.current_node] = float('-inf')\n        done = (self.selected_count == self.problem_size)\n        if done:\n            reward = -self._get_travel_distance()\n        else:\n            reward = None\n        return self.step_state, reward, done\n\n    def _get_travel_distance(self):\n        gathering_index = self.selected_node_list.unsqueeze(3).expand(self.batch_size, -1, self.problem_size, 2)\n        seq_expanded = self.problems[:, None, :, :].expand(self.batch_size, self.pomo_size, self.problem_size, 2)\n        ordered_seq = seq_expanded.gather(dim=2, index=gathering_index)\n        rolled_seq = ordered_seq.roll(dims=2, shifts=-1)\n        segment_lengths = ((ordered_seq-rolled_seq)**2).sum(3).sqrt()\n        travel_distances = segment_lengths.sum(2)\n        return travel_distances\n\n# --- Shared Neural Network Utility Functions ---\ndef _get_encoding(encoded_nodes, node_index_to_pick):\n    batch_size = node_index_to_pick.size(0)\n    pomo_size = node_index_to_pick.size(1)\n    embedding_dim = encoded_nodes.size(2)\n    gathering_index = node_index_to_pick[:, :, None].expand(batch_size, pomo_size, embedding_dim)\n    picked_nodes = encoded_nodes.gather(dim=1, index=gathering_index)\n    return picked_nodes\n\ndef reshape_by_heads(qkv, head_num):\n    batch_s = qkv.size(0)\n    n = qkv.size(1)\n    q_reshaped = qkv.reshape(batch_s, n, head_num, -1)\n    q_transposed = q_reshaped.transpose(1, 2)\n    return q_transposed\n\ndef multi_head_attention(q, k, v, rank2_ninf_mask=None, rank3_ninf_mask=None):\n    batch_s = q.size(0)\n    head_num = q.size(1)\n    n = q.size(2)\n    key_dim = q.size(3)\n    input_s = k.size(2)\n    score = torch.matmul(q, k.transpose(2, 3))\n    score_scaled = score / torch.sqrt(torch.tensor(key_dim, dtype=torch.float))\n    if rank2_ninf_mask is not None:\n        score_scaled = score_scaled + rank2_ninf_mask[:, None, None, :].expand(batch_s, head_num, n, input_s)\n    if rank3_ninf_mask is not None:\n        score_scaled = score_scaled + rank3_ninf_mask[:, None, :, :].expand(batch_s, head_num, n, input_s)\n    weights = nn.Softmax(dim=3)(score_scaled)\n    out = torch.matmul(weights, v)\n    out_transposed = out.transpose(1, 2)\n    out_concat = out_transposed.reshape(batch_s, n, head_num * key_dim)\n    return out_concat\n\n# --- Shared Neural Network Modules ---\nclass AddAndInstanceNormalization(nn.Module):\n    def __init__(self, **model_params):\n        super().__init__()\n        embedding_dim = model_params['embedding_dim']\n        self.norm = nn.InstanceNorm1d(embedding_dim, affine=True, track_running_stats=False)\n    def forward(self, input1, input2):\n        added = input1 + input2\n        transposed = added.transpose(1, 2)\n        normalized = self.norm(transposed)\n        back_trans = normalized.transpose(1, 2)\n        return back_trans\n\nclass FeedForward(nn.Module):\n    def __init__(self, **model_params):\n        super().__init__()\n        embedding_dim = model_params['embedding_dim']\n        ff_hidden_dim = model_params['ff_hidden_dim']\n        self.W1 = nn.Linear(embedding_dim, ff_hidden_dim)\n        self.W2 = nn.Linear(ff_hidden_dim, embedding_dim)\n    def forward(self, input1):\n        return self.W2(F.relu(self.W1(input1)))\n\nclass EncoderLayer(nn.Module):\n    def __init__(self, **model_params):\n        super().__init__()\n        self.model_params = model_params\n        embedding_dim = self.model_params['embedding_dim']\n        head_num = self.model_params['head_num']\n        qkv_dim = self.model_params['qkv_dim']\n        self.Wq = nn.Linear(embedding_dim, head_num * qkv_dim, bias=False)\n        self.Wk = nn.Linear(embedding_dim, head_num * qkv_dim, bias=False)\n        self.Wv = nn.Linear(embedding_dim, head_num * qkv_dim, bias=False)\n        self.multi_head_combine = nn.Linear(head_num * qkv_dim, embedding_dim)\n        self.add_n_normalization_1 = AddAndInstanceNormalization(**model_params)\n        self.feed_forward = FeedForward(**model_params)\n        self.add_n_normalization_2 = AddAndInstanceNormalization(**model_params)\n\n    def forward(self, input1):\n        head_num = self.model_params['head_num']\n        q = reshape_by_heads(self.Wq(input1), head_num=head_num)\n        k = reshape_by_heads(self.Wk(input1), head_num=head_num)\n        v = reshape_by_heads(self.Wv(input1), head_num=head_num)\n        out_concat = multi_head_attention(q, k, v)\n        multi_head_out = self.multi_head_combine(out_concat)\n        out1 = self.add_n_normalization_1(input1, multi_head_out)\n        out2 = self.feed_forward(out1)\n        out3 = self.add_n_normalization_2(out1, out2)\n        return out3\n\n# --- CVRP Model Components ---\nclass CVRP_Encoder(nn.Module):\n    def __init__(self, **model_params):\n        super().__init__()\n        self.model_params = model_params\n        embedding_dim = self.model_params['embedding_dim']\n        encoder_layer_num = self.model_params['encoder_layer_num']\n        self.embedding_depot = nn.Linear(2, embedding_dim)\n        self.embedding_node = nn.Linear(3, embedding_dim)\n        self.layers = nn.ModuleList([EncoderLayer(**model_params) for _ in range(encoder_layer_num)])\n\n    def forward(self, depot_xy, node_xy_demand):\n        embedded_depot = self.embedding_depot(depot_xy)\n        embedded_node = self.embedding_node(node_xy_demand)\n        out = torch.cat((embedded_depot, embedded_node), dim=1)\n        for layer in self.layers:\n            out = layer(out)\n        return out\n\nclass CVRP_Decoder(nn.Module):\n    def __init__(self, **model_params):\n        super().__init__()\n        self.model_params = model_params\n        embedding_dim = self.model_params['embedding_dim']\n        head_num = self.model_params['head_num']\n        qkv_dim = self.model_params['qkv_dim']\n        self.Wq_last = nn.Linear(embedding_dim+1, head_num * qkv_dim, bias=False)\n        self.Wk = nn.Linear(embedding_dim, head_num * qkv_dim, bias=False)\n        self.Wv = nn.Linear(embedding_dim, head_num * qkv_dim, bias=False)\n        self.multi_head_combine = nn.Linear(head_num * qkv_dim, embedding_dim)\n        self.k = None\n        self.v = None\n        self.single_head_key = None\n\n    def set_kv(self, encoded_nodes):\n        head_num = self.model_params['head_num']\n        self.k = reshape_by_heads(self.Wk(encoded_nodes), head_num=head_num)\n        self.v = reshape_by_heads(self.Wv(encoded_nodes), head_num=head_num)\n        self.single_head_key = encoded_nodes.transpose(1, 2)\n\n    def forward(self, encoded_last_node, load, ninf_mask):\n        head_num = self.model_params['head_num']\n        input_cat = torch.cat((encoded_last_node, load[:, :, None]), dim=2)\n        q_last = reshape_by_heads(self.Wq_last(input_cat), head_num=head_num)\n        q = q_last\n        out_concat = multi_head_attention(q, self.k, self.v, rank3_ninf_mask=ninf_mask)\n        mh_atten_out = self.multi_head_combine(out_concat)\n        score = torch.matmul(mh_atten_out, self.single_head_key)\n        sqrt_embedding_dim = self.model_params['sqrt_embedding_dim']\n        logit_clipping = self.model_params['logit_clipping']\n        score_scaled = score / sqrt_embedding_dim\n        score_clipped = logit_clipping * torch.tanh(score_scaled)\n        score_masked = score_clipped + ninf_mask\n        probs = F.softmax(score_masked, dim=2)\n        return probs\n\n# --- CVRP Model ---\nclass CVRPModel(nn.Module):\n\n    def __init__(self, **model_params):\n        super().__init__()\n        self.model_params = model_params\n        self.encoder = CVRP_Encoder(**model_params)\n        self.decoder = CVRP_Decoder(**model_params)\n        self.encoded_nodes = None\n\n    def pre_forward(self, reset_state):\n        depot_xy = reset_state.depot_xy\n        node_xy = reset_state.node_xy\n        node_demand = reset_state.node_demand\n        node_xy_demand = torch.cat((node_xy, node_demand[:, :, None]), dim=2))\n        self.encoded_nodes = self.encoder(depot_xy, node_xy_demand)\n        self.decoder.set_kv(self.encoded_nodes)\n\n    def forward(self, state):\n        batch_size = state.BATCH_IDX.size(0)\n        pomo_size = state.BATCH_IDX.size(1))\n        if state.selected_count == 0:\n            selected = torch.zeros(size=(batch_size, pomo_size), dtype=torch.long)\n            prob = torch.ones(size=(batch_size, pomo_size))\n        elif state.selected_count == 1:\n            selected = torch.arange(start=1, end=pomo_size+1)[None, :].expand(batch_size, pomo_size)\n            prob = torch.ones(size=(batch_size, pomo_size))\n        else:\n            encoded_last_node = _get_encoding(self.encoded_nodes, state.current_node)\n            probs = self.decoder(encoded_last_node, state.load, ninf_mask=state.ninf_mask)\n            if self.training or self.model_params['eval_type'] == 'softmax':\n                while True:\n                    with torch.no_grad():\n                        selected = probs.reshape(batch_size * pomo_size, -1).multinomial(1) \\\n                            .squeeze(dim=1).reshape(batch_size, pomo_size)\n                    prob = probs[state.BATCH_IDX, state.POMO_IDX, selected].reshape(batch_size, pomo_size)\n                    if (prob != 0).all():\n                        break\n            else:\n                selected = probs.argmax(dim=2)\n                prob = None\n        return selected, prob\n\n# --- TSP Model Components ---\nclass TSP_Encoder(nn.Module):\n    def __init__(self, **model_params):\n        super().__init__()\n        self.model_params = model_params\n        embedding_dim = self.model_params['embedding_dim']\n        encoder_layer_num = self.model_params['encoder_layer_num']\n        self.embedding = nn.Linear(2, embedding_dim)\n        self.layers = nn.ModuleList([EncoderLayer(**model_params) for _ in range(encoder_layer_num)])\n\n    def forward(self, data):\n        embedded_input = self.embedding(data)\n        out = embedded_input\n        for layer in self.layers:\n            out = layer(out)\n        return out\n\nclass TSP_Decoder(nn.Module):\n    def __init__(self, **model_params):\n        super().__init__()\n        self.model_params = model_params\n        embedding_dim = self.model_params['embedding_dim']\n        head_num = self.model_params['head_num']\n        qkv_dim = self.model_params['qkv_dim']\n        self.Wq_first = nn.Linear(embedding_dim, head_num * qkv_dim, bias=False)\n        self.Wq_last = nn.Linear(embedding_dim, head_num * qkv_dim, bias=False)\n        self.Wk = nn.Linear(embedding_dim, head_num * qkv_dim, bias=False)\n        self.Wv = nn.Linear(embedding_dim, head_num * qkv_dim, bias=False)\n        self.multi_head_combine = nn.Linear(head_num * qkv_dim, embedding_dim)\n        self.k = None\n        self.v = None\n        self.single_head_key = None\n        self.q_first = None\n\n    def set_kv(self, encoded_nodes):\n        head_num = self.model_params['head_num']\n        self.k = reshape_by_heads(self.Wk(encoded_nodes), head_num=head_num)\n        self.v = reshape_by_heads(self.Wv(encoded_nodes), head_num=head_num)\n        self.single_head_key = encoded_nodes.transpose(1, 2)\n\n    def set_q1(self, encoded_q1):\n        head_num = self.model_params['head_num']\n        self.q_first = reshape_by_heads(self.Wq_first(encoded_q1), head_num=head_num)\n\n    def forward(self, encoded_last_node, ninf_mask):\n        head_num = self.model_params['head_num']\n        q_last = reshape_by_heads(self.Wq_last(encoded_last_node), head_num=head_num)\n        q = self.q_first + q_last\n        out_concat = multi_head_attention(q, self.k, self.v, rank3_ninf_mask=ninf_mask)\n        mh_atten_out = self.multi_head_combine(out_concat)\n        score = torch.matmul(mh_atten_out, self.single_head_key)\n        sqrt_embedding_dim = self.model_params['sqrt_embedding_dim']\n        logit_clipping = self.model_params['logit_clipping']\n        score_scaled = score / sqrt_embedding_dim\n        score_clipped = logit_clipping * torch.tanh(score_scaled)\n        score_masked = score_clipped + ninf_mask\n        probs = F.softmax(score_masked, dim=2)\n        return probs\n\n# --- TSP Model ---\nclass TSPModel(nn.Module):\n\n    def __init__(self, **model_params):\n        super().__init__()\n        self.model_params = model_params\n        self.encoder = TSP_Encoder(**model_params)\n        self.decoder =            probs = self.decoder(encoded_last_node, ninf_mask=state.ninf_mask)\n\n            if self.training or self.model_params['eval_type'] == 'softmax':\n                while True:\n                    selected = probs.reshape(batch_size * pomo_size, -1).multinomial(1) \\\n                        .squeeze(dim=1).reshape(batch_size, pomo_size)\n                    prob = probs[state.BATCH_IDX, state.POMO_IDX, selected] \\\n                        .reshape(batch_size, pomo_size)\n                    if (prob != 0).all():\n                        break\n            else:\n                selected = probs.argmax(dim=2)\n                prob = None\n        return selected, prob\n\n# --- Trainer Utility ---\nclass AverageMeter:\n    def __init__(self):\n        self.sum = 0\n        self.count = 0\n    def reset(self):\n        self.sum = 0\n        self.count = 0\n    def update(self, val, n=1):\n        self.sum += (val * n)\n        self.count += n\n    @property\n    def avg(self):\n        return self.sum / self.count if self.count else 0\n\n# --- CVRP Trainer (Core Training Logic) ---\nimport torch.optim as Optimizer # Imported locally for snippet clarity\nimport self.env.load_problems(batch_size)\n        reset_state, _, _ = self.env.reset()\n        self.model.pre_forward(reset_state)\n        prob_list = torch.zeros(size=(batch_size, self.env.pomo_size, 0))\n        state, reward, done = self.env.pre_step()\n        while not done:\n            selected, prob = self.model(state)\n            state, reward, done = self.env.step(selected)\n            prob_list = torch.cat((prob_list, prob[:, :, None]), dim=2)\n        advantage = reward - reward.float().mean(dim=1, keepdims=True)\n        log_prob = prob_list.log().sum(dim=2)\n        loss = -advantage * log_prob\n        loss_mean = loss.mean()\n        max_pomo_reward, _ = reward.max(dim=1)\n        score_mean = -max_pomo_reward.float().mean()\n        self.model.zero_grad()\n        loss_mean.backward()\n        self.optimizer.step()\n        return score_mean.item(), loss_mean.item()\n\n# --- TSP Trainer (Core Training Logic) ---\nclass TSPTrainer:\n    def __init__(self,     "Title": "Continuation Path Learning for Homotopy Optimization",
    "Main Contributions": "The paper proposes Continuation Path Learning (CPL), a novel model-based approach for homotopy optimization. Classic homotopy methods are sensitive to continuation schedule design and often ignore useful intermediate solutions. CPL learns the entire continuation path, encompassing infinite intermediate solutions, by collaboratively optimizing the original problem and all surrogate subproblems. It enables real-time generation of any intermediate solution, which is valuable for various applications. Empirical results demonstrate CPL's significant improvement in homotopy optimization performance and its ability to provide additional information for better decision-making across non-convex optimization, noisy regression, and neural combinatorial optimization problems.",
    "Methodology": "CPL builds a learnable neural network model, xϕ(t), that maps any continuation level t to its corresponding solution. The method reformulates the traditional unidirectional homotopy optimization into a single-loop model training problem, minimizing the expected homotopy function H(xϕ(t), t) over t. Training is performed using stochastic gradient descent with Monte Carlo sampling for t. Gradients are computed via the chain rule, with ∂xϕ(t)/∂ϕ determined by backpropagation and ∇xH(x, t) either analytically, through a zeroth-order Evolutionary Strategy approximation, or a specific Gaussian homotopy approximation. An optional local search, initialized with solutions from the learned path, can be applied for specific homotopy levels. For Neural Combinatorial Optimization (NCO), the policy model's parameters (specifically query and projection parameters in the Attention Model decoder) are dynamically conditioned on t via a multi-layer perceptron. NCO models are trained using REINFORCE with POMO rollout and inference involves sampling multiple continuation levels to generate and select the best solution for the original problem.",
    "Experimental Setup": "CPL's performance was evaluated on three problem categories: 1) Non-convex Optimization using synthetic Ackley, Rosenbrock, and Himmelblau functions. 2) Noisy Regression on four distinct noisy nonlinear regression problems. 3) Neural Combinatorial Optimization (NCO) applied to the Traveling Salesman Problem (TSP) and Capacitated Vehicle Routing Problem (CVRP), including both randomly generated and realistic TSPLib/CVRPLIB instances. Baselines for non-convex optimization included Gradient Descent, classical Gaussian optimization (GradOpt), and Single Loop Gaussian Homotopy (SLGHr). NCO experiments compared CPL against exact solvers (Concorde), heuristic solvers (LKH3, OR-Tools), and various learning-based methods (e.g., NeuRewriter, DACT, AM-greedy, POMO). Evaluation metrics included optimal value/loss and optimality gap, runtime, and average solution cost. CPL models were neural networks: a simple fully connected network for non-convex optimization and a modified Attention Model for NCO, with specific layer and neuron configurations. Function evaluations were controlled and CPL typically allocated 95% for model training and 5% for optional local search.",
    "Limitations": "A primary limitation of CPL is the necessity to design and train a specific model for learning the continuation path. The effectiveness of this model is highly dependent on the problem at hand, potentially requiring significant domain knowledge for efficient model building. The paper also implies that the generalization ability for NCO might decrease for instances with sizes drastically different from the training set, a limitation shared by other neural combinatorial optimization methods.",
    "Future Research Directions": "Future work includes conducting additional theoretical analyses regarding problem transformation and CPL's relationship to the value function approach. The authors suggest extending the solution model method to a more general value function approach within parametric optimization. Further research could also explore improving CPL's performance by integrating it with learning-to-optimize frameworks. Additionally, investigating better continuation path construction and sampling methods is proposed to enhance the performance-time ratio, particularly for Neural Combinatorial Optimization. Finally, further studies could leverage findings from smooth parametrization work (e.g., Levin et al., 2022) to gain insights into CPL's problem transformation.",
    "Experiment Code": "The core CPL method described, specifically the building of a model xϕ(t) that maps a continuation level t to its solution, the explicit minimization of a homotopy function H(xϕ(t), t) over t, Monte Carlo sampling for t during training, and sampling multiple continuation levels for inference, are not directly implemented in the provided repository content. The repository implements a Neural Combinatorial Optimization (NCO) model using the POMO (Pooling-based, Optimal-policy, Multi-start) architecture, which is mentioned as a component used within CPL's application to NCO.\n\nHowever, if the problem-specific state variable 'load' (for CVRP) or 'capacity' (for KP) is interpreted as a 'continuation level t' within the NCO context, then its use to condition query parameters in the Attention Model decoder can be seen as an analogous mechanism for CVRP and KP. This mechanism is absent in the TSP model provided.\n\nFor CVRP, the relevant section from NEW_py_ver/CVRP/POMO/CVRPModel.py is:\n```python\nclass CVRP_Decoder(nn.Module):\n    def __init__(self, **model_params):\n        super().__init__()\n        self.model_params = model_params\n        embedding_dim = self.model_params['embedding_dim']\n        head_num = self.model_params['head_num']\n        qkv_dim = self.model_params['qkv_dim']\n\n        # Wq_last takes (embedding_dim + 1) as input, where +1 is for 'load'\n        self.Wq_last = nn.Linear(embedding_dim+1, head_num * qkv_dim, bias=False)\n        self.Wk = nn.Linear(embedding_dim, head_num * qkv_dim, bias=False)\n        self.Wv = nn.Linear(embedding_dim, head_num * qkv_dim, bias=False)\n\n        self.multi_head_combine = nn.Linear(head_num * qkv_dim, embedding_dim)\n\n        self.k = None\n        self.v = None\n        self.single_head_key = None\n\n    def set_kv(self, encoded_nodes):\n        # ... (key and value setup) ...\n\n    def forward(self, encoded_last_node, load, ninf_mask):\n        # encoded_last_node.shape: (batch, pomo, embedding)\n        # load.shape: (batch, pomo)\n        # ninf_mask.shape: (batch, pomo, problem)\n\n        head_num = self.model_params['head_num']\n\n        # Concatenate encoded_last_node with 'load' (interpreted as 't')\n        input_cat = torch.cat((encoded_last_node, load[:, :, None]), dim=2)\n        # shape = (batch, group, EMBEDDING_DIM+1)\n\n        # Query computed using Wq_last on the concatenated input\n        q_last = reshape_by_heads(self.Wq_last(input_cat), head_num=head_num)\n        # shape: (batch, head_num, pomo, qkv_dim)\n\n        q = q_last\n\n        out_concat = multi_head_attention(q, self.k, self.v, rank3_ninf_mask=ninf_mask)\n\n        mh_atten_out = self.multi_head_combine(out_concat)\n\n        score = torch.matmul(mh_atten_out, self.single_head_key)\n\n        sqrt_embedding_dim = self.model_params['sqrt_embedding_dim']\n        logit_clipping = self.model_params['logit_clipping']\n\n        score_scaled = score / sqrt_embedding_dim\n        score_clipped = logit_clipping * torch.tanh(score_scaled)\n        score_masked = score_clipped + ninf_mask\n        probs = F.softmax(score_masked, dim=2)\n\n        return probs\n```\nSimilar logic is found in OLD_ipynb_ver/POMO_CVRP/source/MODEL__Actor/grouped_actors.py and OLD_ipynb_ver/POMO_KP/source/MODEL__Actor/grouped_actors.py, where `remaining_loaded` (CVRP) or `capacity` (KP) is concatenated to form part of the query input. This feature is not present in the TSP models.",
    "Experiment Result": "The repository provides implementations for the Capacitated Vehicle Routing Problem (CVRP) and Traveling Salesperson Problem (TSP) using a POMO-based Reinforcement Learning approach.     "Title": "Deep Ranking Ensembles for Hyperparameter Optimization",
    "Main Contributions": "The paper introduces Deep Ranking Ensembles (DRE), a novel neural network Bayesian Optimization (BO) surrogate, to address the sub-optimal performance of traditional HPO methods that treat surrogate training as a regression task. DRE reformulates this into a Learning-to-Rank (L2R) problem, specifically preserving the ranks of hyperparameter configuration performances with emphasis on top-performing ones. The method meta-learns neural network surrogates optimized for ranking and models uncertainty via ensembling. It achieves new state-of-the-art results in HPO across a large-scale experimental protocol.",
    "Methodology": "The core methodology involves training an ensemble of diverse neural network 'scorers' (MLPs) to predict the ranking score of hyperparameter configurations. These scorers are optimized using a weighted list-wise L2R loss, where higher penalties are assigned to top-performing configurations. Uncertainty estimation is achieved by training multiple diverse scorers (10 MLPs) using the Deep Ensemble paradigm, with diversity encouraged by different per-scorer seeds. The method incorporates meta-learning where surrogates are transferred from evaluations on previous datasets, and dataset meta-features (extracted by a DeepSet architecture) are used as an additional input to condition the scorers, enhancing transfer quality. Bayesian Optimization (BO) is guided by acquisition functions like Expected Improvement (EI), which leverages the ensemble's mean and variance of estimated ranks.",
    "Experimental Setup": "Experiments were conducted on HPO-B, the largest public HPO benchmark, comprising 16 search spaces with meta-train, meta-test, and meta-validation splits, covering 86 datasets/tasks. DRE was compared against 12 state-of-the-art HPO baselines, including non-transfer methods (e.g., Random Search, GP, HEBO, DNGO, BOHAMIANN, DKGP) and transfer methods (e.g., TST, RGPE, FSBO, GCP, DKLM). The DRE architecture included a DeepSet meta-feature extractor with 5 hidden layers and 32 neurons, and an ensemble of 10 MLPs, each with 4 hidden layers and 32 neurons. Meta-learning involved 5000 epochs (Adam optimizer, LR 0.001, batch size 100), with fine-tuning for 1000 epochs during meta-test. Performance was evaluated based on average ranks over 100 BO iterations, starting with 5 initial configurations, and statistically validated using critical difference diagrams.",
    "Limitations": "The paper acknowledges that DRE incurs a higher computational cost per BO step compared to some baselines like FSBO and HEBO, although it qualifies this as a 'very small overhead' relative to the cost of actual hyperparameter evaluations. Additionally, it was found that using a small list size (e.g., 10 elements) during meta-training leads to underperforming settings, indicating sensitivity to this hyperparameter. The depth of the scorer network was also noted to be slightly more important to tune than the number of neurons per layer for optimal performance.",
    "Future Research Directions": "The authors suggest that the DRE surrogate can be effectively applied to improve HPO performance in various sub-problems, including multi-fidelity HPO, multi-objective HPO, and neural architecture search. They also express belief that this work will set a new trend in the HPO community, encouraging a shift away from regression-learned surrogate functions in Bayesian Optimization.",
    "Experiment Code": "import torchimport torch.nn as nnfrom sklearn.preprocessing import MinMaxScalerimport copy import numpy as npimport osfrom torch.utils.tensorboard import SummaryWriterimport timeimport gpytorchimport loggingfrom fsbo_utils import totorch, Metric, EInp.random.seed(1203)RandomQueryGenerator= np.random.RandomState(413)RandomSupportGenerator=        optimizer = torch.optim.Adam(self.parameters(), lr=lr)        scheduler = torch.optim.lr_scheduler.CosineAnnealingLR(optimizer, epochs, eta_min=1e-7)        for epoch in range(epochs):            self.train_loop(epoch, optimizer, scheduler)        def train_loop(self, epoch, optimizer, scheduler=None):                self.epoch_end()        assert(self.training)        for task in self.tasks:            inputs, labels = self.get_batch(task)            for _ in range(self.n_inner_steps):                optimizer.zero_grad()                z = self.feature_extractor(inputs)                self.model.set_train_data(inputs=z, targets=labels, strict=False)                predictions = self.model(z)                loss = -self.mll(predictions, self.model.train_targets)                loss.backward()                optimizer.step()                mse = self.mse(predictions.mean, labels)                self.train_metrics.update(loss,self.model.likelihood.noise,mse)        if self.curr_valid_loss:            self.save_checkpoint(os.path.join(self.checkpoint_path,'weights'))            self.curr_valid_loss = validation_results['loss']        self.valid_metrics.reset()               self.train_metrics.reset()            def test_loop(self, task, train): # no optimizer needed for GP        (x_support, y_support),(x_query,y_query) = self.get_support_and_queries(task,train) self.likelihood(self.model(z_query))            loss = -self.mll(pred, y_query)            lower, upper = pred.confidence_region() #2 standard deviations above and below the mean        mse = likelihood,config,dims ):        super(ExactGPLayer, self).__init__(train_x, train_y, likelihood)        self.mean_module  = gpytorch.means.ConstantMean()        ## RBF kernel        if(config['kernel']=='rbf' or config['kernel']=='RBF'):            self.covar_module = gpytorch.kernels.ScaleKernel(gpytorch.kernels.RBFKernel(ard_num_dims=dims if config['ard'] else None))        elif(config['kernel']=='matern'):            self.covar_module = gpytorch.kernels.ScaleKernel(gpytorch.kernels.MaternKernel(nu=config['nu'],ard_num_dims=dims if config['ard'] else None))        ## Spectral kernel        else:            raise ValueError('[ERROR] the kernel \";' + str(config['kernel']) + '\"; is not supported for regression, use \";rbf\"; or \";spectral\".')                def forward(self, x):        mean_x  = self.mean_module(x)        covar_x = self.covar_module(x)        return gpytorch.distributions.MultivariateNormal(mean_x, covar_x)    class MLP(nn.Module):    def __init__(self, input_size, hidden_size=[32,32,32,32], dropout=0.0):                super(MLP, self).__init__()        self.nonlinearity = nn.ReLU()        self.fc = nn.ModuleList([nn.Linear(in_features=input_size, out_features=hidden_size[0])])        for d_out in hidden_size[1:]:            self.fc.append(nn.Linear(in_features=self.fc[-1].out_features, out_features=d_out))        self.out_features = hidden_size[-1]        self.dropout = nn.Dropout(dropout)    def forward(self,x):                for fc in self.fc[:-1]:            x = fc(x)            x = self.dropout(x)            x = self.nonlinearity(x)        x = self.fc[-1](x)        x = self.dropout(x)        return xfrom genericpath import existsimport torchfrom fsbo_modules import EI(incumbent, mu, stddev):        mu             = mu.reshape(-1,)    stddev         = stddev.reshape(-1,)    with np.errstate(divide='warn'):        imp = mu - incumbent        Z = imp / stddev        score = imp * norm.cdf(Z) + stddev * norm.pdf(Z)    return score        class Metric(object):    def __init__(self,prefix='train: '):        self.reset()        self.message=prefix + 'loss:     "Experiment Result": "The extracted experimental code implements the FSBO (Transfer Learning for Bayesian HPO with End-to-End Landmark Meta-Features) method, which aligns with several components of the described method (MLPs, meta-learning, BO with EI, uncertainty estimation). However, there are some notable differences from the 'Method' description:  1. The 'Method' describes 'training an ensemble of diverse neural network 'scorers' (MLPs) to predict the ranking score of hyperparameter configurations' and 'optimized using a weighted list-wise L2R loss'. The FSBO implementation uses an MLP as a feature extractor for a Gaussian Process (GP) surrogate model, which performs regression (predicts mean and standard deviation) rather than directly predicting ranking scores, and uses Marginal Log-Likelihood (mll) and Mean Squared Error (MSE) as loss functions, not list-wise L2R loss.  2. The 'Method' mentions 'Uncertainty estimation is achieved by training multiple diverse scorers (10 MLPs) using the Deep Ensemble paradigm'. The provided FSBO code trains a single MLP feature extractor and a single GP. While diversity could be introduced by running the method with different seeds (which DeepKernelGP supports via its 'seed' parameter), an explicit ensemble of 10 MLPs is not directly implemented.  3. The 'Method' states 'dataset meta-features (extracted by a DeepSet architecture) are used as an additional input to condition the scorers'. The FSBO implementation uses an MLP as a feature extractor for the input, but it does not explicitly use a DeepSet architecture for extracting dataset meta-features to condition the scorers.     "Title": "Landscape Surrogate: Learning Decision Losses for Mathematical Optimization Under Partial Information",
    "Main Contributions": "The paper addresses challenges in learning-integrated optimization, particularly the computational expense and sparse gradients associated with implicit optimization (f \b\bi\b g). It proposes Landscape Surrogate (LANCER) losses, a smooth and learnable replacement for f \b\bi\b g, which can be computed faster, provides dense and smooth gradients, and generalizes to unseen problems. The authors introduce a generic optimization formulation for coupled learning and optimization problems and an efficient alternating optimization algorithm to train LANCER. Experimental results demonstrate that LANCER achieves comparable or superior objective values compared to state-of-the-art baselines on synthetic and real-world problems, significantly reducing solver calls, and notably outperforming existing methods for computationally expensive high-dimensional problems.",
    "Methodology": "The core methodology involves formulating the learning problem through a compound function f \b\bi\b g, where f is the objective and g is a parametric solver. LANCER introduces a learnable neural network, M, to act as a smooth and differentiable landscape surrogate for this composite function f \b\bi\b g. This surrogate M is used to train the target parametric mapping c\b\bi\b, avoiding direct backpropagation through the often non-differentiable or computationally expensive solver g. The training is performed using an efficient alternating optimization algorithm. In the 'w-step,' the parameters of M are updated by fitting M to the observed f \b\bi\b g values (obtained by running the solver g with the current c\b\bi\b outputs). In the '\b\bi\b-step,' the parameters of c\b\bi\b are updated by minimizing the learned surrogate M. The approach supports black-box solver access, only requiring evaluation of f at g's solution. LANCER unifies settings like Smart Predict+Optimize (P+O) and learning surrogate costs for Mixed Integer Nonlinear Programming (MINLP). Enhancements include reusing a trained M for deployment without solver access and caching past f \b\bi\b g evaluations in a replay-buffer-like mechanism to diversify M's training data.",
    "Experimental Setup": "LANCER was validated across two settings: smart predict+optimize (P+O) and learning surrogate costs for MINLP, covering linear, nonlinear, combinatorial, synthetic, and real-world scenarios. Synthetic P+O problems included Shortest Path (5x5 grid, 1000 instances) and Multidimensional Knapsack (5-dimensional, 100 items, 1000 instances), using SCIP for LP/MILP solutions. Baselines for P+O were 2-stage, SPO+ [13], DBB [34], and LODLs [37]. For synthetic MINLP, nonlinear shortest path problems (5x5 and 15x15 grids) were used, solved by Bellman-Ford, and compared against SCIP, a domain heuristic, and SurCo–zero [16]. Real-world applications included quadratic portfolio selection (Markowitz model using Quandl data, 50 portfolios) for P+O, using CvxPy, with baselines like 2-stage, LODLs, and MDFL [41]. A more complex combinatorial portfolio selection problem with a third-order objective (Quandl data, 100 portfolios) was also tested against SCIP (MILP, MIQP, MINLP variants) and SurCo-prior/zero. LANCER's neural network architectures (MLPs with tanh activation) and training hyperparameters (learning rates, iterations) were detailed for each experiment. Computational efficiency was analyzed by comparing solver calls vs. decision loss/regret. Ablation studies explored reusing the learned M and the stability of LANCER across various hyperparameters and neural network architectures for the nonlinear shortest path problem.",
    "Limitations": "The current LANCER framework lacks theoretical guarantees regarding the convergence of its alternating optimization algorithm, which trains both the landscape surrogate model (Mw) and the target mapping (c\b\bi\b). This absence of formal guarantees could potentially impact the stability of the algorithm, although empirical investigations suggest robustness in many cases. Another identified drawback is the inherent complexity in tuning the parameters of the landscape surrogate model (M), which necessitates careful model selection and training.",
    "Future Research Directions": "Future research directions include addressing the noted drawback regarding the complexity of tuning the landscape surrogate model (M), potentially through more automated or robust model selection strategies. Additionally, exploring extensions of LANCER to fully black-box objective function (f) scenarios is suggested. The authors also indicate that further ablation studies on the algorithm's stability, beyond the initial investigations presented, could be a valuable area for future work.",

    "Title": "SurCo: Learning Linear SURrogates for COmbinatorial Nonlinear Optimization Problems",
    "Main Contributions": "The paper introduces SurCo, a systematic framework to efficiently solve combinatorial nonlinear optimization problems by learning linear surrogate costs. SurCo bridges the gap between challenging nonlinear problems and efficient linear combinatorial solvers by differentiating through the solver end-to-end. It proposes three variants: SurCo-zero for individual problems, SurCo-prior for problem distributions, and SurCo-hybrid for combining both. Key findings show SurCo obtains better solutions faster than state-of-the-art and domain-expert approaches in real-world applications such as embedding table sharding, inverse photonic design, and nonlinear route planning. Theoretically, it provides intuition that predicting surrogate costs is more effective than directly predicting solutions due to potentially infinite Lipschitz constants in the latter.",
    "Methodology": "SurCo learns linear surrogate cost coefficients (ˆc) which are then fed into existing efficient combinatorial solvers (gΩ) to find solutions (x) that minimize the original nonlinear objective function f(x). The core mechanism is end-to-end optimization of the surrogate costs by backpropagating through the differentiable combinatorial solver. For a single instance (SurCo-zero), c is updated via gradient descent on f(gΩ(c)). For problem distributions (SurCo-prior), a neural network model ˆc(y; θ) predicts surrogate costs based on instance description y, minimizing Σ f(gΩ(yi)(ˆc(yi; θ)); yi). SurCo-hybrid combines both by initializing SurCo-zero's coefficients with predictions from SurCo-prior and then fine-tuning. Differentiability of f(x; y) and ∂f/∂x is assumed, or a learned cost model is used. Existing tools like CVXPYLayers (for MILP with relaxed integrality during training) and blackbox differentiation (for discrete linear optimizers like Bellman-Ford) are leveraged to compute gradients through the solvers.",
    "Experimental Setup": "SurCo was empirically evaluated across three real-world optimization problems. For **embedding table sharding**, the DLRM dataset was used to distribute 10 to 60 tables on 4 GPUs (5GB memory limit), validated against Greedy, Domain-Heuristic, and Dreamshard (SoTA offline RL). For **inverse photonic design**, randomly generated instances from the Ceviche Challenges (Waveguide Bend, Mode Converter, Wavelengths Division Multiplexer, Beam Splitter) were used (25 train/25 test per type) with 1270nm/1290nm wavelengths, comparing against the Pass-Through method. For **nonlinear route planning**, 5x5 grid graphs with 25 draws of normally distributed edge parameters were used, with loose, normal, and tight deadlines, comparing against a domain-specific heuristic and SCIP with time limits. Evaluation metrics focused on solution quality (f(ˆx; y)) and query count of f. Experiments were conducted on a cluster with Nvidia A100 GPUs and PyTorch.",
    "Limitations": "One limitation is that due to the linear surrogate, SurCo will always return a vertex in the feasible region, whereas the optimal solution to the original nonlinear objective might reside in the interior. However, the authors note that many real-world problems' solutions are indeed on the vertices. Additionally, the paper states that SurCo's performance is 'somewhat limited to binary problems due to the lack of interior integer points'.",
    "Future Research Directions": "Future research directions include proving convergence guarantees or generalization bounds for SurCo. Furthermore, improvements to SurCo could enable scalable solving for settings in stochastic optimization, game theory, and combinatorial reinforcement learning. Addressing the limitation of only returning vertex solutions by finding ways to explore interior points for nonlinear objectives is also an area for future work.",
    "Experiment Code": "The provided repository content does not contain direct implementation sections related to the SurCo method, such as code for learning surrogate cost coefficients, differentiable combinatorial solvers (e.g., CVXPYLayers or Bellman-Ford integration), or neural networks for predicting costs. Output:
{
    "open_problems": "BOIL converts every learning curve into a single scalar through a logistic (sigmoid) compression whose midpoint m and growth g are learned jointly with the GP surrogate.  \n1.  m and g must be optimised by expensive marginal-likelihood search every few BO steps – a bottleneck when curves are short or very noisy.  \n2.  A poor initial guess for (m,g) makes the compressed targets almost constant, degrading the GP fit and causing ill-conditioning.  \n3.  The same (m,g) pair is applied to all tasks although different curves can plateau at very different levels or speeds.  \nA minimal, task-independent, parameter-free curve compression could remove this weakness while keeping BOIL’s advantages.",
    "methods": "Adaptive Area-Under-Curve Compression (AAUC-BOIL)  \nReplace BOIL’s sigmoid-based scalar   s(m,g; r_{1:T})  with a parameter-free scalar that measures the *cumulative* normalised progress of the learning curve:  \n1.  Let r_t be the raw reward/accuracy at iteration t and r_max = max_t r_t.  \n2.  Form the normalised sequence  \\bar r_t = (r_t – r_0) / (r_max – r_0 + ε).  \n3.  Define the compression score   \n     AAUC = \\frac{1}{T} Σ_{t=1}^{T} \\bar r_t · w_t,   w_t = 1 – e^{-α(t/T)} (α=4 fixed)  \n   – a simple trapezoidal approximation of the (discounted) area under the curve that (i) is smooth, (ii) gives larger weight to late iterations (via w_t) while still using the whole curve, and (iii) contains **no learnable parameters**.  \n4.  Plug AAUC in place of the sigmoid score everywhere in BOIL – no change to virtual-observation logic, GP kernel, or acquisition function.  \nTheoretical motivation: AAUC is a bounded (0-1), Lipschitz, strictly monotone function of the entire curve; it therefore preserves the ordering of configurations by their *overall* training quality while eliminating the unstable optimisation of (m,g).",
    "experimental_setup": "• Task: tune learning-rate and dropout of an MLP on MNIST.  \n• Baseline: original BOIL (sigmoid score with learnt m,g).  \n• Proposed: AAUC-BOIL (same code-base, single line change in score function).  \n• 10 random seeds, 20 BO iterations, same initial Sobol points.  \n• Metrics: (i) best test accuracy found after 20 iterations; (ii) wall-clock time per BO iteration; (iii) GP condition-number (log-det) statistics.  \nExpectation: AAUC-BOIL reaches higher accuracy in fewer iterations and shows lower variance across seeds, with ~25 % reduction in time per BO step (no m,g optimisation).",
    "experimental_code": "import numpy as np\n\ndef aauc_score(curve, alpha=4.):\n    \"\"\"Parameter-free compression of a learning curve.\n    curve: list/np.array of rewards r_t, length T >=1\"\"\"\n    r = np.asarray(curve, dtype=float)\n    r0, rmax = r[0], r.max()\n    norm = (r - r0) / (rmax - r0 + 1e-8)\n    T = len(r)\n    t = np.arange(1, T + 1)\n    w = 1.0 - np.exp(-alpha * t / T)\n    return np.sum(norm * w) / T\n\n# drop-in replacement inside BOIL -----------------------------------------\n# old line: y_original = sigmoid_score(curve, m, g)\n# new line:\ny_original = aauc_score(curve)",
    "expected_result": "• Mean best-accuracy after 20 BO steps rises from 98.1 % (±0.15) to 98.4 % (±0.07).  \n• Average time per BO step drops from 3.8 s to 2.9 s (≈-24 %).  \n• Log-condition-number of the GP kernel reduced by ~1.2 on average, indicating better numerical stability.  \nThese gains come solely from replacing the two-parameter sigmoid with the AAUC score.",
    "expected_conclusion": "AAUC-BOIL shows that a *parameter-free* curve compression can remove the main practical pain-point of BOIL – repeatedly optimising the sigmoid parameters – while improving both robustness and speed.  The change touches only the curve-to-scalar function, so existing BOIL implementations can adopt it with a single line of code, yet it yields measurable accuracy gains and lower computational overhead, making hyperparameter optimisation over iterative learners even more efficient."
